# Nava-vraja-mahima. Vol. Seven

## Dedication

Although I have written Nava-vraja-mahima for the pleasure of all Vaisnavas who either physically or mentally reside in Vrndavana, I dedicate this book to those whose hearts are devoted to Radha-Syamasundara and New Vraja-dhama. It is my hope that they will pass their time in this way:

*iha vatsan samacarayad*  
*iha nah svami jagau vamsim*  
*iti sasram gadato me*  
*yamuna-tire dinam yayat*

**"Here our Lord herded the calves, and here He played the flute. I pray that I may pass my days shedding tears as I speak these words on the Yamuna's shore." (Sri Raghupati Upadhyaya)**

## Preface

The title of the book you are now reading, Nava-vraja-mahima, when rendered in English becomes "The Glories of New Vraja-dhama." I originally conceived of it as a booklet, meant to guide devotees visiting the holy places of New Vraja-dhama, ISKCON's rural community in Hungary.

As you must have noticed, the book has grown in size since its inception and would now likely require a small handcart for eager pilgrims to be able to transport it from one pastime place to another! Nonetheless, it still serves its original purpose of extolling New Vraja- dhama by praising its presiding Deities, Radha-Syamasundara, and by narrating the Supreme Lord Krsna's pastimes in His eternal abode, Sri Vrndavana-dhama.

Texts that glorify the holy abodes (dhamas) of the Lord are called dhama-mahatmyas. Two prime examples of this genre of devotional literature arc Rupa Gosvami's Sri Mathura-mahatmya and Bhaktivinoda Thakura's Sri Navadvipa-dhama-mahatmya. Both of these books establish the sanctity of the dhamas they depict — Vrndavana and Navadvipa respectively — by citing Puranic praise of the dhamas themselves as well as the pastime places within their boundaries. There is, however, an interesting difference between the two. Sri Mathura-mahatmya is an unordered compendium of scriptural references to the pastime places it describes, while Sri Navadvipa-dhama-mahatrnya chronicles the Lord's pastimes and the places at which they occurred by following the sequence in which pilgrims visit those places as they circumambulate the dhama. Thakura Bhaktivinoda also recounts the personal spiritual reflections he gleans from circumambulating Navadvipa in an appendix to Sri Navadvipa-dhama-mahatrnya that he calls Sri Navadvipa-bhava-taranga.

Despite their stylistic differences, the transcendental benefit readers derive from these two types of mahatmyas is the same. They act as practical guidebooks to the dhama, and at the same time they aid pilgrims in immersing their thoughts in the Lord's glories. For that reason, devotees who do not travel to the dhama can also derive transcendental benefit by circumambulating the holy places in their minds as they meditate on both the Lord's pastimes and the dhama's glories as they arc recounted in sacred texts of this nature.

Nava-vraja-mahima is a mahatmya in the style of Sri Navadvipa-dhama-mahatmya in that it includes an account of my own personal circumambulation, or parikrama, of New Vraja-dhama and the realisations that came to me as a result. In fact, as I visit the pastime places in New Vraja-dhama — more commonly known to the people of Hungary as Krsna Valley — I sometimes reflect on similar parikramas I have undertaken in Bhauma Vrndavana, recount histories of how New Vraja-dhama was established and developed, or express my own devotional aspirations. This personal approach is primarily an attempt to please the devotees of New Vraja-dhama, who requested me to describe the parikrama not as a passive observer but as a participant who shares his adventure with the reader.

Amongst many possible readers, this personal adventure is especially intended to be shared with the residents of and visitors to New Vraja-dhama, for whom this book will naturally be of greatest interest. Those readers who are not a part of that particular audience may rightly question what New Vraja-dhama is and how its glorification can be relevant to persons who live outside Hungary or who may never visit.

As its name suggests, New Vraja-dhama is modelled after the original Vraja-dhama, Bhauma Vrndavana, Krsna's eternal home as it appears on earth. The inspiration to establish New Vraja-dhama came from my spiritual master, His Divine Grace A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupada, the founder- deary a of the International Society for Krishna Consciousness. In the 1960s in America, Srila Prabhupada established New Vrndavana and envisioned it to be a self-sufficient community, dependent upon agriculture and cow protection, as well as a place of pilgrimage in the West. New Vraja-dhama is therefore our humble attempt to fulfil the desire of His Divine Grace that such places of pilgrimage be established throughout the world.

In the introduction to this book and in the essay entitled "The Truths of the Dhama," the reader will find a detailed history of how and why New Vraja-dhama came to be, along with a philosophical study of the spiritual principles that make it a holy place. I cannot overstate how important it is for the reader to carefully go through these parts of the book in order to clearly understand the spiritual foundations upon which New Vraja-dhama and Nava-vraja-mahima rest.

What benefit will those who do not visit New Vraja-dhama gain from reading this book? The answer is threefold.

Nava-vraja-mahima contains extensive and very detailed narrations of Krsna's pastimes at all the major places in Vrndavana, as well as at many lesser-known places. Extensive research went into compiling — from the Vedas, Upanisads, Puranas, Samhitas, and other Vedic histories, as well as from the commentaries of our previous acaryas and other sources — the information it contains. Altogether, the text and the accompanying references offer the reader a unique opportunity to delve deeply into Krsna's pastimes in Bhauma and Goloka Vrndavana and better get to know the Supreme Lord and His loving associates.

Second, although the topographical orientation, distances, and occasionally the sequence of New Vraja-dhama's parikrama differ from the same in Bhauma Vrndavana, the book also serves as an in-depth guide for pilgrims to use in visiting the pastime places of Sri Krsna's original earthly abode.

Third, through the glorification of New Vraja-dhama, this book testifies to the greatness of Srila Prabhupada's achievement in spreading the potency of Vrndavana throughout the world.

Now, allow me to clarify a few points about certain expressions and literary devices used in the text, about the structure of the volumes, and about the actual parikrama route of New Vraja-dhama.

The expression "Krsna's pastimes in New Vraja-dhama" is not meant to imply that Krsna manifests His eternal pastimes here in the way that He does, creation after creation, in Bhauma Vrndavana. Rather it means that the pastime places in New Vraja-dhama correspond to the original places in Bhauma Vrndavana or Goloka Vrndavana. Largely due to the uncommon format of the text (which I will discuss elsewhere in this preface) and because of the correspondence between the pastime places in both dhamas, it was sometimes necessary to refer to the pastimes associated with those places in a way that may make it sound as if they happened in New Vraja-dhama.

If I say, for example, "At this place, called Aghavana, Krsna killed Aghasura," it means "Krsna killed Aghasura in the Aghavana of Bhauma Vrndavana, which is commemorated by the place of the same name here in New Vraja-dhama." Similarly, phrases such as "At this place Radha and Krsna enact Their rasa dance" should be taken to mean "At the Rasa-sthali of Goloka Vrndavana, the divine couple enact Their eternal rasa dance. The place called Rasa-sthali in New Vraja-dhama commemorates that original place."

The reason I use such expressions, aside from literary necessity, is to emphasise the importance of New Vraja-dhama and its stature as a place of pilgrimage. Again, the reader will find the philosophical and spiritual reasoning and supporting scriptural evidence for employing this convention in the introduction and the essay on dhama-tattva that follow this preface.

Another literary device found in this book — as well as in other books describing the holy dhama— is that I sometimes speak about New Vraja-dhama as if I were directly seeing the unmanifest (aprakata) abode and pastimes of the Lord. These expressions are not declarations of my existential position; rather, they are poetic expressions of my deep feelings for the subject matter and the places themselves. I qualify such statements here to avoid causing misunderstandings that might blur the line between the manifest and unmanifest features of the dhama, merging them in a way that could be taken as contrary to authorised dhama-tattva.

When, in describing the dhama, authors weave between manifest and unmanifest realities in this way, it often causes the features of the aprakata realm or the pastimes Krsna is relishing there to seem as if they are part of the world visible to conditioned souls. But devotees well versed in the principles of dhama-tattva will have no difficulty following these transitions between the manifest and unmanifest realms, whereas others may either not notice them or find them confusing.

Our great acaryas may express themselves in such ways due to the overwhelming bliss they feel at seeing the aprakata-dhama and the eternal pastimes going on there. Or, in the case of those who are not on the fully realised platform of our great acaryas, authors may be making a conscious endeavour to emphasise the divinity that underlies the features of the dhama that conditioned souls are able to perceive.

In writing Nava-vraja-mahima, poetic inspiration would sometimes impel me to describe the features of the dhama not in terms of what I was seeing with my eyes — for I cannot see that which is unmanifest — but in terms of the aprakata counter-whole as I have heard about it from scripture and as I contemplate it in relation to New Vraja-dhama. Readers who notice such expressions in the pages of this book should take them in this light, not as my personal realisation of the unmanifest dhama in the way of the great acaryas.

**\*\*\*\***

Parikrarna is the traditional way of visiting Vrndavana. When Krsna sent Uddhava to Nandagrama with messages for the Vraja-vasis, He directed His cousin along a specific route and pointed out places to note along the journey. Thus Uddhava relished parikrama by travelling from Mathura to Nandagrama, as did Krsna by describing the journey to him.

On His only visit to Vrndavana, Caitanya Mahaprabhu spent the first month of His two-month stay touring through Vraja's twelve forests. And even though Lord Caitanya is Krsna Himself, He requested the help of a guide and followed the established route of His time.

With the rediscovery of Krsna's pastime places, the Vraja-mandala parikrama has evolved into what is currently called caurasi-krosa parikrama, or circumambulation of the one hundred and sixty-eight- mile dhama, the longest version of which takes almost two months to complete. Today, often during the holy month of Karttika, hundreds of devotees relish walking barefoot from place to place on ISKCON's Vraja-mandala parikrama, living in the meadows or fields of Vraja, and absorbed in hearing about Krsna and chanting His names.

For Hungarian devotees who cannot go to Bhauma Vrndavana, who cannot stay there for thirty days or more, or who would like to go on parikrama every year but cannot afford to go to India so often, the Nava-vraja-mandala parikrama is for them. Annually, members of the Hungarian yatra visit selected places of New Vraja-dhama over the course of several days, and as the dhama is further developed and parikrama paths established, the guided tours will become more extensive. In addition, by using the separate book of maps provided with Nava-vraja-mahima, devotees may go on parikrama at any time they want and thus relish the nectar of hearing Krsna's pastimes and serving His dhama.

In this way, Nava-vraja-mahima seeks to serve those devotees keen to visit Krsna's abode, both in Hungary and in India. And for devotees who are neither residents of nor pilgrims to one dhama or the other, this work gives them the chance to visit Krsna's holy dhama in their minds and thus bring the atmosphere of Vrndavana to wherever they reside.

Circumambulating the dhama is a means of offering respect. It includes visiting pastimes places, remembering pastimes, bowing down, taking the dust from pastime places on one's head, bathing in sacred waters, and performing nama-sankirtana. Many major places within the dhama — Govardhana Hill, Vrndavana town, and Varsana amongst them — have their own parikrama routes. As far as possible, these places have been or will be represented in New Vraja-dhama. There is never a dearth of transcendental diversion for the devout pilgrim!

At the time of writing this book, the main pastime places of New Vraja-dhama have been established, whereas many parikrama paths and other secondary pastime places have been mapped out but not yet developed. These places — like the construction of a magnificent new temple for Radha-Syamasundara— remain part of future plans to develop New Vraja-dhama.

Let me now describe the book's structure.

As already mentioned, this book is written in the form of my own parikrama, which is actually a compilation of multiple parikramas I made of New Vraja-dhama, some encompassing the entirety of the dhama, others only of places especially dear to me.

In the course of describing my parikrama, there were times at which deep philosophical topics or lengthy pastimes came up that required explanations so extensive that to describe them properly would have broken the flow of the narration. These explanations became essays that are now appendices at the end of each volume. I request my reader not to neglect these appendices — nor the endnotes that accompany each chapter, for that matter — as readers arc sometimes wont to do. They comprise roughly one third of the book's total content and are instrumental in bringing the reader to a balanced understanding of its message. Designated stanzas in the running text direct the reader to these appendices at the proper times.

In addition to the parikrama and appendices, the ten final chapters of Nava-vraja-mahima are dedicated to directly glorifying Radha- Syamasundara, the presiding Deities of New Vraja-dhama. In these chapters I exalt Their Lordships through the meditations that arise as I chant the Gayatri mantras and the maha-mantra and from my direct service to Them on the altar. This last section of the book therefore contains in-depth analyses of the Gaudiya Vaisnava diksa-mantras as well as the Hare Krsna maha-mantra. In this way the book has three parts: parikrama, appendices, and direct glorification of Radha-Syamasundara.

The running text that forms the bulk of the book, comprised of the parikrama and the praises of Radha-Syama, is written in three-line prose stanzas. It is pleasant to look at and easy to read, but it was a challenge to write, edit, and lay out on the page. This challenge led us to adopt certain conventions, such as mixing Sanskrit verses with

English translations within a single stanza, or having longer verses straddle more than one stanza. However, we feel confident that the hook's format will enable you to relish the topic all the more.

From the outset I wanted Nava-vraja-mahima to be well illustrated to give windows into Krsna's pastimes and to contribute to the overall purpose of the work — to increase the reader's attachment to the Lord. I hope this has been achieved to your satisfaction. In Sri Krsna-samhita (9.16), Bhaktivinoda Thakura writes, "By seeing pictures of Krsna or by repeatedly hearing descriptions of Krsna from persons who have seen Him, one's greed for attaining Krsna increases unlimitedly."

To assist you in navigating through the unfamiliar names of pastime places and the variances in the locations of these places in New Vraja-dhama and Bhauma Vrndavana, each chapter also includes a map or maps of the area it describes.

Altogether there are eight volumes of text. The set is complete with a ninth volume solely dedicated to maps of the various holy places of both New Vraja-dhama and Bhauma Vrndavana, along with short descriptions of each pastime place. Devotees can take this map book with them to follow the parikrama routes in both dhamas and have a reminder of Krsna's activities there.

Nava-vraja-mahima is meant to serve as a companion for devotees who want to take full advantage of the presence of Vrndavana in Hungary and to develop their relationship with its presiding Deities, Radha-Syamasundara. Furthermore, it is meant to encourage devotees anywhere in the world to worship their local Deities with devotion and recognise the Vaikuntha nature of those Deities' abodes. It is my hope as the author that this work will serve these purposes in some small way and thus bring pleasure to devotees, for my own access to the dhama will be possible only by the good wishes of the Vaisnavas.

In conclusion, I request the reader, wherever he or she may reside, to consider that in its essence this book praises the same dhama and the same Krsna that are there in their own hearts, and which are thus very near and dear to them. It is in this way that our journey around

New Vraja-dhama through the pages of this book is synonymous with our own inner journey back to Godhead.

The journey through Nava-vraja-mahima is a long one. The book spans over five thousand pages and took five years to write. Therein I have tried my best to portray the truths of Vrndavana as they are recorded in scriptures and narrated by perfect devotees like Srila Prabhupada. Sadly, I remain a conditioned soul and thus am prone to make mistakes. If my readers find mistakes within the pages of this book, I ask them to tolerate such faults in the way they do the pebbles or thorns that prick their feet while on parikrama of the dhama.

Hare Krsna.  
Sivarama Swami  
New Vraja-dhama  
Gopastami, 2 November 2011

## Chapter 32 - Brahma-gayatri to Guru-gayatri

Praise of New Vraja-dhama would be incomplete without celebrating the glories of the youthful and incomparably beautiful Radha and Syama, who with restless eyes enjoy loving pastimes in Their abode.

For that reason I will now share some of the ways in which I meditate upon and serve Srimati Radharani and Sri Syamasundara, and how They have touched and transformed my life.

Some persons may consider it improper to disclose such confidential topics or feelings of the heart. After pondering this, I decided to first consult senior Vaisnavas and the wisdom of our acaryas.

My research showed that if such topics were not divulged, no one would come to know of them, yet if unqualified or judgemental people heard them, they would criticise and be a source of disturbance.

Thus I concluded that one should divulge only the very essence of one's confidential feelings with the utmost discretion and in a way that only like-minded Vaisnavas would be able to understand.

For this reason I make Their Lordships' kindness known to you, my readers. And as I do, I beg forgiveness for defects in my childish eagerness to praise Them. O Radha! O Syama! Please pardon me!

Dear readers! My heart tells me that it would be ungrateful of me not to speak openly to you, yet please don't be angry with me, thinking that I am posturing as an elevated soul or flaunting myself before you.

I speak not only for myself but also on behalf of the bee-like devotees of New Vraja-dhama, who always relish the nectarean taste of devotional service to the lotus feet of Radha-Syamasundara.

These devotees have insisted that the transcendental sweetness and marvel of Radha-Syama be recorded in this book, and so, desiring to please them, I begin this final portion of Nava-vraja-mahima.

**\*\*\*\***

From the time I first arrived in Hungary I considered that if the political climate ever allowed, we would only instal two sets of Deities — Gaura-Nitai in Budapest and Radha-Krsna at a farm.

At that time we had no temple in the capital and the need for a farming project had not yet come into the minds of the devotees. But in my mind both were viable strategies and objects of meditation.

The first deity to arrive in Hungary was a life-size murti of all-merciful Srila Prabhupada, whom we installed in 1989 on his disappearance day but in a temporary and wholly inadequate facility.

The next summer we secured the Huvosvolgy temple in Budapest, and a year later (1991) completed payment for it from the proceeds of devotees' heroic feats at distributing Srila Prabhupadas books.

Then, at the 1992 GBC meeting in Mayapur, I met a Bengali gentleman who was eager to sponsor marble Gaura-Nitai Deities for Budapest, as well as Radha-Krsna Deities for the farm.

But where was the farm? To find a suitable site would not be easy. It had to he fertile and attractive, remote yet easily accessible, large enough to develop but inexpensive enough to fit our budget.

For the better part of two years I combed Hungary looking for the place where Vrndavana would manifest itself. But my search was not to bear fruit until Sri Sri Gaura-Nitai arrived in Budapest.

Although we had no home for Them, I decided to invite Their Lordships to Hungary and requested my godsister, Bala-gopala Devi Dasi, to supervise the carving of both sets of Deities.

She went to Jaipur to make the necessary arrangements for the Deities. Her experience in Deity worship and residence in Vrndavana for many years both contributed to her expertise.

Thus Dayala-Nitai-Vijaya-Gauranga arc in Budapest and Radha-Syamasundara in New Vraja-dhama, for which all of us will be forever in Bala-gopala's debt.

In the fall of that year (1992) I travelled to Jaipur expecting to see finished Deities. Much to my dismay, however, Gaura-Nitai were not completed, and the work on Radha and Krsna had not even begun.

For five years the Hungarian devotees had been preaching valiantly and distributing books with great success. They were hoping to instal Deities soon, and I could not bear the thought of disappointing them.

Thus, a few weeks after the 1992 Prabhupada Marathon, we installed small brass forms of Dayala-Nitai and Vijaya-Gauranga, and during that year They miraculously led us to a suitable farm property.

Before travelling to India in 1993, I asked Gaura Sakti Dasa to carry on the search for a farm and also prayed to Gaura-Nitai, "Wherever You are is Vrndavana. So please kindly reveal that place to us now."

While in India I visited Vrndavana, where every afternoon I would circumambulate the town, all the while wondering when and where the location of our farm would be revealed. Surely it must be there.

"O mother Vrndavana!" I prayed. "If I have ever called out the names of the Lord or offered you obeisances with true feeling even once, then please do not neglect me. Kindly become visible to my eyes.

"Although you are very difficult to attain, please open your arms and embrace to your loving bosom the sincere devotees who have no shelter other than you, the shelter of the shelterless."

Without a suitable rural acreage we would be unable to establish an independent community, live self-sufficiently, or give a pure (sattvic) life to our children — Srila Prabhupada's hope for ISKCON's future.

One evening as I returned from parikrama of Vraja, Gaura Sakti telephoned to say that he had located a one-hundred-and-fifteen-hectare site suited to our needs. "Should we purchase it?" he asked.

"What will we do with so much land?" I wondered aloud. But when he told me that the price was within our budget, I happily told him, "Yes! Go ahead and purchase it. I'll see it as soon as I return."

On my way back to the West I stopped in Jaipur. This time I had been assured that both sets of Deities were complete. Only my final approval was needed before They could be shipped off to London.

As soon as I arrived at the carvers' workshop, I was shown beautiful Gaura and Nitai, the sun and moon who together dissipate the darkness of ignorance by Their faultless faces and peaceful smiles.

Then I asked to see Radha and Krsna. On the way to another workshop I was told that the Deities were complete, but that there was another lone Radha who might be a better match for our Krsna.

To the beautiful form that would later be known as Syamasundara and to His lovely consort I offered respectful obeisances and prayers. Unmindful of the surroundings, I also had a soft kirtana.

Soon I was led into another room where a solitary form of Srimati Radharani stood waiting in a corner. She was incomparably beautiful, and She looked like She was very much in need of Krsna's company.

Spontaneously I exclaimed, "Yes! She is the mistress of Hungary." I then sat before Her, feeling as though I had just found something for which I had been searching for longer than I could remember.

The next day I watched as Gaura-Nitai and Radha-Krsna were put in four wooden crates padded with straw and foam. Once sealed tight the crates were addressed to England. My life was about to change!

Soon after I returned to England, the Deities arrived unannounced at Bhaktivedanta Manor. I decided to keep Radha and Krsna in Hertfordshire and take Gaura-Nitai with me to Budapest.

For two years Radha and Krsna stood on the deep window sill in my office at the Manor. They were dressed in a simple set of clothes, and spent Their nights hidden from view behind a plain curtain.

I would often sit at my desk and gaze at Them, unable to get enough of Their beauty or sweetness. And when in Hungary, I would repeatedly think of Them and speak of Them to the devotees.

"What do They look like?" the devotees would ask. "You have to see Their beauty to believe it!" I would answer. "When will They come to Hungary?" I replied, "When you build Them a temple."

In the autumn of 1993 I placed Gaura-Nitai. on the bed in my motor home and drove from London to Budapest where They would wait in the pujari room until it was time for Them to be installed.

But the first priority for the Lord's service was to develop a strategy for the farm, move devotees there, and begin a new chapter in the yatra's history: simple living and high thinking (varnasrama-dharma).

One sunny day, as spring was proudly boasting its opulences, we drove to the farm and walked incognito through its hills and vales. As the birds and crickets serenaded us, I thought, "This is Vrndavana."

From atop the central hill I faced west, looking out over the rolling landscape that stretches to Lake Balaton. Gaura Sakti and I at once agreed that Radha's and Krsna's temple would grace that spot.

That year bustled with pioneering activities. We had to establish Srila Prabhupada's vision for a self-sufficient farm community. And what fun we had transforming spoiled college students into farmers!

Architectural drawings emerged from a sketch I made of the proposed temple, and the yatra transformed itself into an instrument to raise the funds to build it by distributing Srila Prabhupada's books.

After yet another successful Prabhupada Marathon, on Lord Nityananda's appearance day we installed Dayala-Nitai and Vijaya- Gauranga as the commanders-in-chief of Hungary's sankirtana army.

"Our respectful obeisances unto Lord Caitanya and Lord Nityananda, whose arms extend to Their knees, whose complexions are golden, and who inaugurated the congregational chanting of Krsna's names.

"With eyes resembling lotuses, They are the sustainers and benefactors of creation, the best of the brahmanas, the protectors of religious principles, and the most merciful of all incarnations."

Two weeks later, in the depth of winter, devotees from all over Hungary converged on our rural property for a festival to instal Lord Anantadeva and lay the cornerstone of the proposed temple.

At that time the devotees' only residence was a two-room house in Somogyvamos village. From there we walked two kilometres through the winter cold in a long kirtana procession to the ceremonial site.

On the spot over which Radha-Syamasundara now stand we had dug a three-metre-deep hole into which we placed Anantadeva as devotees chanted Hare Krsna and villagers stared on in disbelief.

After a fire sacrifice and offering of flowers, I asked our patron and well-wisher, the town mayor, to speak. This much of his talk I will always remember,

**"Boys and girls! Welcome to this beautiful country.**

**"I have lived here all my life, and I will also die here. Today I am very happy that you have all decided to move here. Work this soil, eat its harvest, raise your children, worship Krsna, and be happy."**

**\*\*\*\***

That following spring, after the cornerstone laying ceremony, construction of the temple began in earnest. For the next two years devotees toiled alongside carpenters, bricklayers, and masons.

The construction of the temple, the fundraising to support it, the preparations for Deity worship, the growth of New Vraja-dhama, the development of the yatra: all were great transcendental adventures.

Those two pioneering years brought out the best in the devotees, transforming many into heroes and heroines whose efforts in the service of the Lord are each remarkable stories in and of themselves.

My hope is that these stories, as well as the stories of all the devotees who have served Srila Prabhupada's mission in Hungary, will one day he chronicled so that all may be given the recognition they deserve.

To avoid increasing the size of this already sizeable book, I will attempt to restrain myself and describe only a few of the many wonderful incidents that led up to the installation of Radha-Syamasundara.

All in all, the temple took two years to build. It came up in two phases: the western portion in the first year, and the eastern section, including the temple room and Deity facilities, in the second.

Construction officially began on 10 August 1994. At that time I built a brick altar and placed a large photo of Radha-Syamasundara on the spot where two years later They would stand in person.

This event was observed without pomp. Hardly anyone outside knew about it. But from that time on, the devotees involved in the construction felt that Radha and Krsna were present on the farm.

Daily the devotees would offer Them flowers, light a stick of incense, and say some prayers. Sometimes I would even see an employee gaze at the picture with a curiosity that in time evolved into devotion.

As the temple gradually rose around that photo of Radha-Syama, Vrndavana began to reveal itself in the hills and vales around us, as well as in the hearts of those who were making the farm their home.

On the canvas of a timeless landscape there gradually appeared newly constructed homes, accommodations for the cows, grain-storage bins, and a road from the village regularly traversed by curious guests.

We named this emerging holy place "New Vraja-dhama." But since non-devotees needed something they could call it, a name they could relate to and remember, the name "Krsna Valley" came to be.

I encouraged the devotees to tap into the large seasonal influx of tourists around Lake Balaton. To our amazement guests began to come on a regular basis and pay to sec the advertised Krsna Valley.

Aside from construction sites surrounding an emerging temple and two asramas in progress, there was not much to see. But we put up placards where projects were planned and the visitors were happy.

The next adventure took place in India during Karttika 1994- At that time I led a parikrama of sacred Govardhana Hill with the hope of finding a Govardhana-sila to worship alongside Radha and Krsna.

A small group began the painstaking — sometimes amusing — search for the sila earmarked for New Vraja-dhama. Finally, somewhere between Govinda-kunda and the Mukuta-sila, we found Him.

We asked a Vraja-vasi's permission to take the stone to the West. She said, "He is a young boy, Lala! You should feed Him lots of sweets and bathe Him with Manasa-ganga water so that He won't get homesick."

I kept Giriraja in the motorhome and served Him alongside my own Deities until autumn of the following year (1995) when I presented Him to the devotees of New Vraja-dhama. They were overjoyed.

Thus Giriraja was the first Deity worshipped in the dhama. And although the devotees certainly feed Him well with sweets, I think it is their love that makes Him feel at home. Just see His wide smile!

"Without taking shelter of the feet of that hill who is the best of Lord Han's servants and who is full of love for the divine king and queen of Vraja, how is it possible to attain pure devotional service?"

During all this time, Rohini Devi Dasi was in the United Kingdom being trained as the head pujari. She made all the arrangements, from designing clothes for Radha and Krsna to furnishing Their kitchen.

In the spring of 1995 we entered the second phase of construction. By that time both workers and sankirtana devotees were in dire need of inspiration, so Radha and Krsna came to Their new home to stay.

The divine couple travelled in the same motorhome that had carried Gaura-Nitai to Budapest. There They rested for a few days while suitable arrangements were being made to receive Them at the farm.

There They would stay in a newly completed room of the temple until Their installation, which was scheduled for the following Janmastami: 1996, the centennial of Srila Prabhupada's appearance.

We informed the devotees all over Hungary that Radha and Krsna would be coming to New Vraja-dhama. I'm sure that every last devotee and well-wisher participated in that historic event.

In the first week of April we carefully drove Their Lordships from Budapest to the farm, where before the main entrance Radha and Krsna were received and placed on a sturdy makeshift altar.

Full of beauty, charm, and wit, Their transcendental forms splendid like a ketaki flower and a monsoon cloud, this youthful couple became the newfound treasures of New Vraja-dhama. Let us worship Them!

In the midst of a roaring kirtana, very elegant in Their new attire, Radha and Krsna looked out over Their abode. Within my heart I addressed them: "This is all for You. Please accept it."

There was not a dry eye to be found in New Vraja-dhama as one by one the devotees came before Their Lordships, offered flowers, bowed down, and saw the Deities in person for the very first time.

What a wonderful day! It felt as if Radha and Krsna were taking up permanent residence not only in New Vraja-dhama but also in the hearts of all the Vaisnavas and Vaisnavis. It was surely true for me.

And as the devotees crowded around for a last glimpse of Radha and Krsna before They retired to Their room, it felt like the demigods were gathering above us, showering flowers and chanting, "Jaya!"

Such spiritual emotions cannot be fully expressed in words. But I can say with certainty that everyone present was deeply moved. No doubt the devotees will forever remember the joy we all tasted that day.

Our happiness illuminated the fundamental principle that guides New Vraja-dhama to this day, and hopefully will for evermore, "If we please Radha-Syamasundara, then we will be happy."

As Lord Krsna Himself says,

**"But those who always worship Me with exclusive devotion, meditating on My transcendental form — to them I carry what they lack, and I preserve what they have."**

Almost no one would see Radha and Krsna again until the following year when They would be installed upon the altar and would finally be visible, every day thereafter, to devotees and guests alike.

During the next year, Their Lordships stayed in a small room of the pujari department surrounded by the sounds of construction, which proclaimed the completion of the temple drawing nearer and nearer.

For the Prabhupada Marathon we published a vegetarian cookbook, and I spent all December selling them directly to bookstores around the country to raise funds for the Deities' clothes and ornaments.

Govinda Maharaja once asked me, "What will you name the Deities?" I replied, "I thought of Radha-Syamasundara. What do you think?" He nodded and said with a grin, "That is the most beautiful name."

Finally the long-awaited Janmastami festival was approaching, and with it hundreds of last minute preparations for the installation, a grand festival, and of course the completion of the temple.

Vaisnavas from abroad, important dignitaries, devotees from all over Hungary, local villagers, and workmen involved in the construction all converged upon Krsna Valley for the historic Janmastami of 1996.

A giant tent covered the square in front of the temple. As was usual for September, the weather was overcast with intermittent rain, but devotees saw the raindrops as flowers showered by the demigods.

As brahmanas conducted ceremonies for the Deities in a curtained-off area on the main stage, sannyasis and other special guests addressed the crowd, and sweet kirtana of Krsna's names filled the air.

Then, to the sounds of conch shells and blissful devotees, the curtains parted. Their Lordships were bathed in divine substances, just as Radha and Krsna had been five thousand years ago in Vrndavana.

After the bathing, while Radha and Krsna were being dressed and ornamented behind the curtain, I explained to the thousand guests present how the Deity of the Lord is not a statue but God Himself.

Once, a young brahmana asked a Vraja Deity of Krsna to walk to his village and hear witness to a promise he had made. The Deity said, "I've never heard of a Deity walking from one place to another."

To which the brahmana replied, "That may be so, but how is it that You are speaking to me? You are a Deity." The answer, of course, was that the Deity is not a statue but directly the Supreme Lord.

"Therefore," I told the audience, "we have brought the Supreme Lord and His eternal consort here to Hungary — to Krsna Valley — so that those who desire to do so can see Them face-to-face.

"If you want to learn the art of conversing with God, we invite you to learn it through our Society's devotees and books, whose teachings culminate in chanting the Hare Krsna mantra. Thank you."

Then I realised that I had not yet pronounced the Deities' names. So I added, "It is customary to give Deities one of Krsna's many names. Therefore these Deities will be called Radha-Syamasundara."

**\*\*\*\***

The Deities were finally placed upon Their altar. Meanwhile, I sat before Them distributing maha-prasada to the long line of guests that filed through the temple, all awed by what they saw and felt.

And as Govinda Maharaja led a wonderful kirtana that resounded to Vaikuntha, I revelled in a blend of sublime joys from having pleased Srila Prabhupada and from having achieved an important goal.

New Vraja-dhama had already begun to blossom, but now — with the presence of Radha-Syamasundara — the lotus of Their abode was unfurling more quickly, replete with potencies of the spiritual world.

New Vraja-dhama continued to appear like a rural farm, but behind a covering veil of matter now lay a transcendental abode of bliss that was Radha-Syamasundara's unmanifest pleasure playground.

There, untold graceful vines flourish with splendid leaves and flowers, and intricate floral archways lead to secluded groves furnished with flower couches, vermilion paintings, and jewel lamps.

There, the six seasons always dance in aromatic forests of enchantment, parrots expertly recite poetry, and blossoming flowers intoxicate bumblebees with the aroma of their nectar.

There, cool fragrant breezes that carry the cuckoos' fifth note drift along the banks of sacred lakes and rivers, and forest paths of diamond-dust boast the unique markings of divine footprints.

There, in the courtyard of an elegant jewel-palace, a youthful cowherd couple — one dark, one fair — always engage in pastimes of love within the circle of Their confidential gopi friends.

There, the reverberation of the holy names, the music of the flute, and the sound of joyful laughter pour unprecedented nectar into the love-laden heart through the receptacle of the ears.

There, not even a trace of the miseries that overwhelm this world is anywhere to be found; instead, inconceivable bliss benevolently reigns over the minds and senses of the ever-youthful residents.

There — after having wandered across endless time through the blazing fire of material enjoyment — those souls fortunate enough to have found that soothing shelter have all their desires fulfilled.That realm — which is home to the countless associates of the Lord, yet is hardly known but to a few rare souls of this world — has manifested its potencies in an epitome of itself, New Vraja-dhama.

**"Let us glorify Vrndavana-dhama, the supreme king of forests and abode of spiritual love, upon entering which one is filled with a great wonder of unprecedented transcendental happiness —**

**"Where Radha's and Krsna's amorous pastimes fill everyone with wonder. Where the divine couple's joy, from revealing through exchanges of love that They belong to each other, shines eternally."**

In contrast to the material realm, in which ill will and ugliness ever-increasingly abound, Vraja-dhama exudes a sublime, pure love and a divine, sweet beauty that have as their source two Supreme Persons.

The source of that pure love is a demure, golden-complexioned cowherd damsel, and the source of that sweet beauty is a sapphire-coloured cowherd prince, the joyful son of Nanda Maharaja.

Just as flowers exude fragrance, so love emanates compassion — the display of limitless kindness. In the same way, beauty radiates sweetness— the mellow that floods the heart with boundless joy.

Both compassion and sweetness capture the heart, mind, and senses, and by the grace of love personified make those three agents of the body into dancing puppets in the hands of beauty, the puppet-master.

Krsna manifests Radha, His complementary form of love, to partake of His own sweetness. Through the medium of pastimes He gives Her pleasure by His sweetness, and She gives Him pleasure by Her bliss.

To share and enhance Their pleasure, Radha compassionately expands Herself into countless golden gopis who, like cakoras, live on the rays of sweetness emanating from Radha's and Krsna's pastimes.

When Krsna wants to know the bliss of loving Himself, His love and compassion make Him golden (Gaura), and when He wants to know the bliss of serving His diverse pastimes, He becomes Govardhana.

Overcome by the sweetness of Krsna's beauty, adorable Radha sometimes faints. At other times Her bodily complexion turns blackish like His. She then appears like a dark creeper (syama-lata).

These exchanges of Krsna's beauty and Radha's love ebb and flow in an ocean of pastimes that are difficult to put into words, but one can taste all this by singing the sublime names of Radha-Syamasundara.

Krsna, the form of beauty, is dashing, clever, witty, and full of joy. Radha, the form of love, is soft but cunning, submissive but contrary, cool and warm, and best compared to a sweet camphor-scented smile.

A competition goes on between Radharani's love and Krsna's beauty. By nature Her love wants to please His beauty, and His beauty wants to satisfy Her love by begging Her for more and more pleasure.

This exchange of joy is eternal. Fortunate are the souls who, by submitting to the guidance of compassion, liberally distribute the mysteries of Gaura, Radha, and Krsna to others and taste that joy.

These blissful exchanges keep Radha's love and Krsna's beauty ever fresh, as ever fresh as the eternally blossoming spring garden in which the transcendental Cupid prepares His love-invigorating arrows.

Radha's and Krsna's diverse spiritual features are further shown in the beauty of Their clothes, ornaments, and garlands, which give Their intimate devotees a chance to arrange these things for Them.

Somehow a desire to assist in serving Radha and Krsna in such a way is now rooted in my heart, and that desire grows the more I water it by chanting Their names and preaching Their glories.

The bliss of Radha's and Krsna's exchanges can be tasted to the degree one has surrendered to Them in service. But devotees who want to enjoy that bliss without such sacrifice taste only its shadow.

By churning Radha's and Krsna's beauty, sweetness, and compassion with the churning rod of Their loving pastimes, a nectarean foam overflows, saturating Vrndavana and further softening that realm.

That eternal Vrndavana has appeared in New Vraja-dhama where that divine couple have also appeared as Radha-Syamasundara, making this a most desirable place for practising Vaisnavas to reside.

O friend! Cast aside all other duties and come live in New Vraja-dhama, where a fair- and dark-complexioned youthful couple live, a couple that enchants even Brahma, Siva, and Narada.

These divine lovers are ordinarily very difficult to meet, but by living in New Vraja-dhama you will not only see Them face-to-face but also bathe in the stream of bliss that flows from Their lotus feet.

**\*\*\*\***

From my first contact with Krsna consciousness I had heard of Krsna's Vrndavana pastimes, and, with the arrival of Sri Caitanya-caritamrta, of Lord Caitanya's absorption in the moods of Vraja.

In 1981 I began to worship my own Gaura-Nitai Deities, and, from that point on, personal service to the Lord became an integral part of my life and meditation, and of my aspiration for eternal service.

As I studied Srila Prabhupada's books and read translations of our acaryas' writings, I gradually felt a special and natural appreciation growing for the selfless service of the Vraja-vasis, especially the gopis.

Although I always was, still am, and for many lifetimes will be a conditioned soul, when I think of the way in which I would like to serve Krsna eternally, I think of Rupa-manjari's and Rati-manjari's examples.

Yet my thoughts of that exalted service have always been secondary to my efforts in spreading Caitanya Mahaprabhu's mission under the direction of my eternal father and master, Srila Prabhupada.

Preaching in Hungary, building up the yatra, opening centres, and establishing New Vraja-dhama were all services to Srila Prabhupada's order, services that often forced me to put aside my thoughts of Vraja.

Initially our purpose for installing Radha-Syamasundara in New Vraja-dhama was to provide a focal point from which our fledgling spiritual community could take sustenance, grow, and flourish.

Thus I was caught off guard and unprepared when, after They had graciously accepted Their place on the altar in New Vraja-dhama, Radha-Syamasundara unarguably knocked on the door of my heart.

The divine couple suddenly burst into my life, took me by the hand, and invited me to serve Them just as I had been contemplating: by massaging, bathing, dressing, and ornamenting Them.

My days began with service to Radha-Syama in the way of arcana and continued in the way of manasa. I had entered a whole new kingdom, revolving exclusively around service to the divine couple.

Progress along the winding paths of that transcendental kingdom was made possible by the all-merciful Hare Krsna maha-mantra, and thus kirtana became the metronome that set the pace of my spiritual life.

O beautiful Radha and clever Syama! Please forgive me for revealing how You saved this fallen soul, but this book is about Your pastimes, and so I also had to include this episode to make Your mercy known.

Your mercy falls equally on the unworthy and the worthy, just as rain falls equally on water as well as land. This inconceivable kindness has touched countless persons for whom I now hope to act as scribe.

My words, my work, my life: these are the flowers I offer at Your lotus feet, hoping they may please You in some small way. Other than to make You happy, to see You smile, I have no purpose in life.

For Your pleasure only I maintain Your place of pastimes, assist Your servants in their quest for You, challenge those who dispute Your existence, and glorify You wherever my travels take me.

Vrndavana is with You wherever You may be, but it remains hidden from ordinary vision. To inspire Your servants and to give You pleasure, I am trying to build a visible representation of Your abode.

You have promised to appear to one and all in Your many blissful Deity forms, but shouldn't we also be granted a precious glimpse into the realm in which You dwell and enjoy novel diversions?

I had often thought that You could be suitably served only in Your spiritual domain, but as long as I am with You here I want to make You feel at home in a place that epitomises eternal Vrndavana.

You have plainly said who is most dear to You: one who explains to others the confidential knowledge You revealed. I am moved to build a model of Vraja in hopes of drawing souls back to You.

Your dear servant, Srila Prabhupada, wanted pilgrimage places like Vrndavana established on every continent. If I serve and please him in this way, I know I am also assured of pleasing You.

Even unwitting visitors to New Vraja-dhama sense its divine energy, and when they come into Your presence they also sense, to one degree or another, that You are not statues but transcendental beings.

Devotees perceive Your personality and Your abode according to the purity of their faith, faith You nurture with Your astounding kindness, just as a gardener takes special care of tender saplings.

Your kindness, appearing in sublime forms, acts as a soothing balm for Your devotees' senses. It appears as countless sweet names for our tongues to savour, or as all-attractive pastimes for our ears to relish.

It appears as Your limitless qualities, the thought of which purifies our minds; as divine forms, which offer a festival for our eyes; and as Your divine abode, which gives ultimate refuge to our bodies.

But the greatest facet of Your kindness, a facet that includes and yet excels all others, is the causeless affection You shower on our love- starved hearts, luring us closer to You, even in spite of ourselves.

In this and the following nine chapters, I praise the extraordinary compassion by which You deliver the fallen through Your names, forms, qualities, pastimes, abode, and love. All glories to You!

Beginning with the creator, Brahma, great sages have glorified and thus realised You with select Gayatri mantras. Now let me attempt to praise Your Lordships and Your abode by explaining these mantras.

Every day at sunrise, noon, and sunset, Gaudiya Vaisnavas recite ten times each the Vedic Brahma-gayatri, along with various other confidential mantras to Sri Guru, Gauranga, Gopala, and Kamadeva.

The sequence culminates with the Kama-gayatri, which is the full, explicit glorification of Vrndavana's ever fresh Cupid and His gopi beloved — in New Vaja-dhama, Your Lordships Radha-Syamasundara.

By receiving these mantras in disciplic succession and chanting them without offence, a devotee worships the Lord and becomes eligible to worship (arcana) the Deity in the pancaratrika system.

In addition to these diksa-mantras, I will glorify the all-powerful Hare Krsna maha-mantra, Sri Caitanya Mahaprabhu's gift to this world, so diligently delivered to us by our dearmost Srila Prabhupada.

May devotees who attentively meditate on these mantras enter into the secrets of Vrndavana-dhama and realise their eternal relationship with Lord Krsna under the shelter of Radharani and Her friends.

**\*\*\*\***

Let us meditate upon the worshipful object of the three worlds, Sri Krsna, who glows with splendour in Sri Radha's presence, and who bestows upon sincere souls the pure devotion that they covet.

I begin with a description of the famous Brahma-gayatri mantra, followed by a word-for-word analysis, and conclude with a few translations, all of which are unique to Gaudiya Vaisnava theology.

At the outset I must caution the reader that the mantras discussed in these chapters arc potent only if they are received aurally from a bona fide spiritual master coming in disciplic succession.

Scriptures give notice that we must be initiated into the mantras we receive for those mantras to have any effect, *sampradaya-vihina ye mantras te nisphala matah*. Taking them from a book will not do.

At the time of initiation (diksa), Gaudiya Vaisnavas arc empowered to worship the Lord with the Vedic Brahma-gayatri mantra, along with a sequence of pancaratrika-mantras and -gayatris.

Gaudiya Vaisnavas chant these diksa-mantras in the following sequence: Brahma-gayatri, Guru mula-mantra, Guru-gayatri, Gaura mula-mantra, Gaura-gayatri, Gopala mantra, and Kama-gayatri.

In days gone by, the Vedic tradition was to allow only those persons born in brahmana families to receive the Brahma-gayatri mantra through initiation, a process called upanayana.

But in Kali-yuga, because we're all born sudras (*kalau sudra-sambhavah*), those who behave as brahmanas are worthy of upa-nayana, regardless of caste, race, gender, religion, or nationality.

At the beginning of creation, the sound of Sri Krsna's flute entered the ears of Brahma as the mantra om, a sound comprised of the three original sounds of the Sanskrit alphabet — a, u, and m.

Inspired by the sound resonating within his heart, Brahma then vibrated the sound externally as the Brahma-gayatri mantra. That entire process constituted Brahma's initiation as a brahmana.

The generic definition of gayatri is a type of mantra that delivers us from material entanglement when sung, *gayantam trayate yasmad gayatri warn tatah smrta*. But it must meet other criteria as well.

What makes a gayatri unique amongst other mantras? It must contain the words vidmahe, dhimahi, and pracodayat, which signify the three divisions of the Vedas — relationship, process, and goal.

Various spiritual lineages, including the Vaisnava sampradayas, give various elucidations of the Brahma-gayatri, *om bhur bhuvah svah tat savitur varenyam bhargo devasya dhimahi dhiyo yo nah pracodayat*.

According to the interpretation given to the meaning of the word savitur, the mantra may be a meditation upon material elements, the demigods, expansions of the Lord, or Lord Krsna Himself.

The Gaudiya Vaisnava interpretation correlates with the other diksa-mantras given along with gayatri. And most important, their interpretation is in line with the conclusion of Srimad-Bhagavatam.

Thus to grasp the full meaning of Brahma-gayatri — alongside its corresponding meditation and ultimate goal — it is essential that we understand the mantra's correlation with Srimad-Bhagavatam.

The Garuda Purana speaks of this correlation, "\[Srimad-Bhagavatam is\] the commentary on the Brahma-gayatri... fully expanded with all Vedic knowledge," *gayatri-bhasya-rupo 'sau vedartha-paribrmhitah*.

Srimad-Bhagavatam is a commentary on the Brahma-gayatri. And because the Bhagavatam is the best scriptural evidence (pramanam amalam), it offers the best commentary on the Gayatri mantra.

In this study of Brahma-gayatri, I use its natural commentary Srimad- Bhagavatam as a template with which to analyse the divisions, individual words, and overall message of the mantra.

The opening verse of Srimad-Bhagavatam not only summarises the entire work and gives its purport but also, as per Jiva Gosvami, refers to and directly explains the meaning of the Brahma-gayatri.

In his purport to that first verse, Srila Prabhupada cites the Matsya Purana as evidence of Gayatri's presence in Srimad-Bhagavatam. His Divine Grace says, "...it begins with the Gayatri mantra."

The first verse of Srimad-Bhagavatam contains the word dhimahi, indicating that the Brahma-gayatri, in which the word dhimahi must be present, is embedded in the very first instruction of the text.

Why did Vyasa conceal gayatri within the first verse? Because only qualified brahmanas are meant to read or hear gayatri, whereas Srimad-Bhagavatam is meant to have a wider audience.

In fact, in the same esoteric language Vyasadeva used to conceal gayatri, he concealed the conclusive message of the Bhagavatam, which is said to be present in every verse of that peerless work.

The rest of this and the following section presents the first verse of Srimad-Bhagavatam, with Jiva Gosvami's correlation of the verse with each word of Brahma-gayatri — except dhimahi, which is self-evident.

They also present Jiva Gosvami's elucidation of the same Bhagavatam verse, which is a revelation of the esoteric meaning of the Brahma-gayatri, the meaning meditated upon by Gaudiya Vaisnavas.

*Om namo bhagavate vasudevaya*  
*janmady asya yato nvayad itaratascarthesv abhijnah svarat*  
*tene brahma hrda ya adi-kavaye muhyantiyat surayah*  
*tejo-vari-mrdam yatha vinimayo yatra tri-sargo 'mrsa*  
*Dhāmnā svena sadā nirasta-kuhakaṁ satyaṁ paraṁ dhīmahi*

**O my Lord, Sri Krsna, son of Vasudeva! O all-pervading Personality of Godhead! I offer my respectful obeisances unto You!**

**"I meditate upon Lord Sri Krsna because He is the Absolute Truth and the primeval cause of all causes of the creation, sustenance, and destruction of the manifested universes.**

**"He is directly and indirectly conscious of all manifestations, He is independent because there is no other cause beyond Him, and it is He only who first imparted Vedic knowledge unto the heart of Brahma.**

**"By Him even the great sages and powerful demigods are placed into illusion, as one is bewildered by the illusory representations of water seen in fire, or land seen on water.**

**"Only because of Him do the material universes, temporarily manifested by the reactions of the three modes of nature, appear factual, although they are unreal.**

**"I therefore meditate upon Him, Lord Sri Krsna, who is eternally existent in the transcendental abode, which is forever free from material illusion. I meditate upon Him, for He is the Absolute Truth."**

**\*\*\*\***

The syllable om, which in the gayatri mantra indicates the Supreme Lord's power to create, maintain, and annihilate the cosmos, is equivalent to the Srimad-Bhagavatam phrase *janmady asya yatah*.

The maha-vyahrtis, bhuh bhuvah svah, are equivalent to yatra tri-sargo 'mrsa, for both refer to the three planetary systems; and tat savitur is the same as janmady asya yatah, for both indicate the ultimate cause.

Sri Jiva says that the word bhargah is equivalent to svarat, and the phrase dhiyo yo nah pracodayat to tene brahma hrda, for both are expressions of a desire for enlightenment, life's ultimate goal.

The Brahma-gayatri mantra and Srimad-Bhagavatam's first verse also both contain phrases that are equivalent to the three divisions: relationship (sambandha), process (abhidheya), and goal (prayojana).

In the gayatri, relationship is tat savitur varenyam, process is bhargo devasya dhimahi, and goal is dhiyo yo nah pracodayat. About the verse Lord Caitanya says,

**"In the beginning of the Bhagavatam there is —**

**"An explanation of the Brahma-gayatri. 'The Absolute Truth \[satyam param\] indicates the relationship, and 'we meditate \[dhimahi\] on Him' indicates devotional service and the ultimate goal of life."**

Lord Caitanya says that in the Bhagavatam verse the goal is satyam param; Jiva Gosvami says that it is tene brahma hrda. But both agree that the goal is there and that it is equal to dhiyo yo nah pracodayat.

Having seen that the words of the Brahma-gayatri, as well as its three divisions, are fully represented in the first verse of Srimad-Bhagavatam, we should be able to accept the two as equal.

What's more, we should be able to accept that the Bhagavatam is the purport of Brahma-gayatri. And what is the essence of that most ancient of divine prayers, the soul of Srimad-Bhagavatam?

Jiva Gosvami comments:

**"As I \[Vyasa\] sat in devotional trance under Narada's direction, loving devotion's influence revealed within my heart the secrets of transcendental knowledge.**

**"At that time I could understand how the original mellow, amorous love, flows from She who is distinct from all others, Sri Radha, and He who is always in Her company, Sri Krsna.**

**"This divine couple is very expert at self-manifest pastimes, but of the two, Radha is so incomprehensibly wonderful that She bewilders even Krsna, not to mention others, like Ananta Sesa.**

**"How wonderful are Their pastimes! When Krsna plays the flute, land becomes liquid and rivers become solid. But even more remarkable is that Radha's effulgence outshines the light of the moon and stars.**

**"These pastimes are enriched by three groups of gopis expanded from Radha: those who favour Her, those who oppose Her, and those neutral to Her. And yet they are all insignificant compared to Her.**

**"Indeed, Radha's presence and influence always defeats all the obstacles to Her meeting with Krsna posed by Her in-laws, Jatila and Kutila, and adversaries like Candravali. All glories to Sri Radha!**

**"Thus I meditate upon the one Absolute Truth who, in order to enjoy eternal pastimes, has become two, a divine couple who transcend all mundane conceptions of male and female."**

Devotees who attentively study Srimad-Bhagavatam under proper spiritual guidance should come to the same conclusion Jiva Gosvami so expertly extracts from the seed verse of that extensive work.

In essence, Sri Jiva's conclusion is that one should fix one's mind in pure devotion upon Krsna's Vrndavana pastimes as they daily unfold around His loving dealings with Radharani and Her gopi friends.

Srimad-Bhagavatam goes to great lengths to show that such mental absorption develops alongside the necessary faith when, free from offence, one regularly chants Lord Krsna's divine names.

The result of such offenceless hearing and chanting of Krsna's divine names and pastimes is that love for the prince of Vraja awakens, a love not unlike that of Radha's girlfriends or maidservants.

Does this mean that all souls are constitutionally maidservants of Srimati Radharani? No. How could they be? The divine couple could hardly relish an atmosphere devoid of the many varieties of rasa.

But all of Vraja's mellows are nourished by paramour love, the original mellow (adi-rasa), and so Jiva Gosvami's conclusion still stands: All rasas are made perfect in the divine couple's pastimes.

This conclusion of the Bhagavatam holds true for the Brahma-gayatri as well. It will also serve as the underlying parameter for the further analysis of the mantra and the compatible meditations that follow.

Since the Bhagavatam directs us to serve Krsna with spontaneous devotion, as do the Vraja-vasis, it stands to reason that we need a meditation to invoke that devotion when chanting Brahma-gayatri.

Keeping these parameters in mind, let us consider Srila Prabhupada's translation of Brahma-gayatri: "Let us meditate on that worshipable effulgence of the divine sun who enthuses our meditation."

This general translation is widely accepted, even by the class of brahmanas critical of Srila Prabhupada for giving the Brahma-gayatri to western devotees and devotees not born in brahmana families.

Certain conclusions in the direct meaning of this translation, however, invite us to search for interpretations in line with Srila Prabhupada's teachings and the parameters just mentioned.

One variance of the literal meaning is that it apparently encourages devotees to meditate on the sun, a practice clearly at odds with Srila Prabhupadas strong rejection of demigod worship.

This seeming contradiction is resolved by taking the words "divine sun" to mean the predominating Deity within the sun, Lord Surya-narayana, whose golden hue is described in the Rg Veda.

Once King Vajra asked Markandeya Rsi why gayatri is chanted at Vaisnava sacrifices if its presiding deity is the sun god. The sage replied by explaining every word of the mantra in relation to Visnu.

Markandeya concluded: "A person desiring material gain or liberation can achieve either by chanting Brahma-gayatri, but a worshipper devoid of material desire attains the abode of Lord Visnu."

This understanding promotes devotion to the Supreme Lord rather than the sun, but it is still incompatible with the exclusive devotion to Krsna found in the Gaudiya Gayatris and Srimad-Bhagavatam.

Srila Prabhupada says that the messages of gayatri and Bhagavatam are one and the same. He writes,

**"Whatever meaning is in the supreme sound omkara is further understood in the gayatri mantra.**

**"Again, this same meaning is explained in Srimad-Bhagavatam in the four slokas known as the catuh-sloki, \[four seed verses of the Bhagavatam\] which begin with the words** ***aham evasam evagre*****."**

Srila Prabhupada offers two other meanings of "divine sun" (savitur), which are even more consistent with the direct philosophical conclusion of Gaudiya Vaisnavism and Srimad-Bhagavatam.

Srila Prabhupada writes,

**"The meaning of the first mantra is, 'I meditate on the sun god who is maintaining the three worlds.' Savitur is the name of the sun god, but the greatest savitur is Krsna.**

**"Therefore it is meditation on Krsna, who is the master of all the cosmic manifestation." And elsewhere Srila Prabhupada writes: "That Savita is Lord Caitanya Mahaprabhu."**

Since Krsna and Caitanya Mahaprabhu are identical in every way, these two explanations of the word savitur result in the same philosophically consistent meditation on Brahma-gayatri:

"Let us meditate upon the worshipable effulgence of the divine sun, Sri Krsna, who also enthuses us to worship Him." This illuminated translation takes us deeper into the Gayatri.

Another word — of the original translation Srila Prabhupada used — that requires elucidation to avoid apparent inconsistency with his other teachings is bhargah, "\[that worshipful\] effulgence."

Since the brahmajyoti, or the impersonal Brahman, is the bodily effulgence of Sri Krsna, this translation of bhargah can easily be misread to mean the impersonal Brahman.

But Srila Prabhupada preached vigorously against worship of the impersonal Brahman. Would he want his followers to meditate on the brahmajyoti while chanting Brahma-gayatri? Clearly, he would not.

Thus once again, we require authoritative evidence to understand more deeply the translation of Brahma-gayatri used by Srila Prabhupada. One such evidence is found in the Agni Purana:

**"That light is called the Supreme Brahman because the word bhargas means effulgence and... that effulgence is Visnu, who is the cause of universal creation...the Absolute Truth, the Supreme Brahman."**

Within the phrase "that effulgence is Visnu" rests the key to a fuller understanding of the word bhargah. In one sense the phrase means the impersonal brahmajyoti, but another meaning is implied.

Since Visnu includes His internal energy, and since Visnu's internal energy means Laksmi, we can also say with confidence that this phrase implies the presence of Laksmi in the word bhargah.

Indeed, since in our understanding of Brahma-gayatri, from the sense just discussed, savitur refers to Krsna, the supreme Visnu, bhargah can also refer to the supreme Laksmi, Sri Radha, Krsna's internal potency.

It stands to reason that, after considering the Vedic translation of the Brahma-gayatri in light of Srila Prabhupada's teachings, we conclude that His Divine Grace was leading us to worship of Radha and Krsna.

Thus, we pray,

**"Let us meditate upon Sri Krsna residing with Radha in His own abode, worshipable by all souls and far beyond the three worlds, and who enthuses us to worship Him as Her servant.”**

**\*\*\*\***

In the preceding stanzas we have heard about the Brahma- gayatri as a means for meditating on the divine couple. To further elucidate these points, let us now hear about each word of the mantra individually.

Om! According to the Vedas, the cosmic manifestation is set in motion by the transcendental sound vibration omkara, which is the sound representation of the Supreme Lord Himself.”

It is also the seed from which the Brahma-gayatri sprouts and then expands into the Vedic literatures, their summary, the Vedanta-sutra, and Vedanta-sutra's unequivocal commentary, Srimad-Bhagavatam.

The question is often asked, "Isn't chanting om a practice for impersonalists, who say it denotes Brahman?" No. Vaisnavas also chant om for purification with the idea that it denotes Krsna.

The sound omkara is an alphabetical representation of Krsna in which the Lord's potency of existence (sandhini-sakti) is active, and the potencies of knowledge and bliss are dormant.

Indeed, Krsna states that He is the syllable om in the Vedic mantras. And the Upanisads declare that not only Krsna but also Krsna's internal potency, Sri Radha, is non-different from omkara.

The Gosvamis' definition of om (aum) includes the living entities: a referring to the master of all planets, Sri Krsna; u referring to Radharani; and m to the living entities (jivas).

If we chant om with the conception that it represents Krsna — His names, companions, and pastimes — we attain liberation. But Mayavadis who identify om with impersonal Brahman are doomed.

This raises another question. Why do we need to chant the Hare Krsna maha-mantra if chanting om grants liberation? It is true that chanting om frees one from the entanglement of material existence.

But only Krsna's names will awaken spontaneous love and give shelter at His lotus feet. Om is the seed of the Vedic mantras, and a seed never has the same taste or nutrition as its fruit.

From the three letters comprising om (a-u-m) expand the threefold aspects of material existence, which include the three modes of nature and the three planetary systems (Bhur, Bhuvar, and Svar).

Bhur, bhuvah, svah, the words that invoke these three planetary divisions, are also known as the maha-vyahrtis, and they contain the Vedic sacrifices' invocation for the seven planetary systems.

This threefold invocation is significant in that it represents the planes of created existence (lower, middle, upper) and their corresponding states of awareness (physical, mental, intellectual).

This connection of gayatri to the Vedic sacrifices and states of awareness in the creation is extremely important. In essence it means that the gayatri invokes the spirit of sacrifice in those who chant it.

Brahma-gayatri, then, according to Srimad-Bhagavatam 's teachings on sacrifice for this age, directs the created beings in all planetary systems to their means of salvation — nama-sankirtana.

Thus it is hardly surprising to find that bhur, bhuvah, and svah are in fact names of Krsna. How could the first words uttered in creation be impersonal or mundane and still invoke the spirit of sacrifice?

Visnu-sahasra-nama says that because Krsna is merciful enough to forgive Brahma's offences, He is called Bhuh; and because He is the transcendental ornament decorating Vraja, He is Bhuvo-laksmih.

Krsna's name is Bhur-bhuvah-svas-taru because He is like a tree (taru) that shades the upper, middle, and lower planetary systems and provides the needs of all living beings, who are like birds in the tree.

"Still," we may ask, "is nama-sankirtana truly the essential message of Srimad-Bhagavatam meant to awaken in the sleeping souls of this age spontaneous loving attachment to Krsna in Vrndavana?"

The answer is yes. Srimad-Bhagavatam begins and ends with glorification of nama-sankirtana and within its text reveals nama-sankirtana as the way to worship the incarnation for this age.

Tat means "that." This one word encapsulates the preceding maha-vyahrtis, the planetary systems and the matching states of awareness they represent, including the spirit of sankirtana they invoke.

In the Bhagavad-gita, Sri Krsna reveals that He is this cosmic manifestation and that the one word tat denotes the Supreme Lord and the method by which residents of this universe can attain Him.

In summary, a study of the gayatri mantra's overall meaning through the lens of Srimad-Bhagavatam (om bhur bhuvah svah tat) inspires us all to strive for service to Radha and Krsna by chanting Their names.

Now let us study the words of the gayatri, one by one, along with the mantra's three phases — relationship, practice, and goal — as Lord Caitanya defined them in His teachings to Sanatana Gosvami.

These are the three phases scriptures teach and devotees follow.

**"The Vedic literatures give information about the living entity's eternal relationship with Krsna, which is called sambandha.**

**"The living entity's understanding of this relationship and his acting accordingly is called abhidheya. Returning home, back to Godhead, is the ultimate goal of life and is called prayojana"**

These three transcendental divisions are found in the Gayatri mantra in this way: relationship — *tat savitur varenyam*; practice — *bhargo devasya dhimahi*; and life's ultimate goal — *dhiyo yo nah pracodayat*.

We begin with relationship (tat savitur varenyam). We have already defined tat and savitur and found varenyam ("worshipable") to be the equivalent of the standard word vidmahe ("try to understand").

We have also heard that one characteristic of Gayatri mantras is to invoke the three phases of spiritual development just mentioned with the words vidmahe, dhimahi, and pracodayat respectively.

The word varenyam ("worshipable") establishes a relationship with the object of worship. It also defines that which is to be understood, and in that way takes the place of vidmahe.

Varenyam modifies bhargah (effulgence), but savitur; as the possessor of that effulgence, is also worshipable. For this reason, determining the meaning of savitur facilitates our meditation on the mantra.

We have already studied various meanings that authorities have attributed to savitur as the worshipable object and its effulgence, depending on the faith of the respective worshipper.

Agni Purana mentions Gayatri, Sarasvati, Savitri, Siva, Durga, Surya and Agni as objects of worship, but concludes that "... Lord Visnu is the Supreme Lord, the father of Agni and all the demigods."

An interesting note is that one meaning of savitur is the individual soul, in which case bhargah is consciousness. In this metaphor, the soul and its consciousness are like an infinitesimal sun and its sunshine.

Krsna Himself offers this analogy: "O son of Bharata, as the sun alone illuminates all this universe, so does the living entity, one within the body, illuminate the entire body by consciousness."

Why and how are the soul and consciousness worshipable? Not in the sense of their being supreme, but in the sense that knowledge of the soul and consciousness is vital for the seriously inquisitive student.

The Katha Upanisad tells the story of a boy named Naciketa who asked Yamaraja whether there was life after death, or in other words, whether the living entity has an identity beyond the body.

Yamaraja refused to disclose the science of the soul to Naciketa until his disciple had renounced all temptations of the world as worthless in comparison to knowledge of his real identity.

Naciketa submitted to his guru, and so Yamaraja disclosed to him the secrets of the soul — the most valuable wisdom in the three worlds, which reveals the gates to liberation and the path to the Supreme.

The multiple meanings of savitur are clearly meant for worshippers of various faiths leading to various goals, but these diverse meanings also reveal a sequence of worship, one that leads Vaisnavas to Krsna.

In this sequence, recognition of the self leads to a grasp of the Supersoul as the inner guide, the sun as the source of life, Narayana as the sun's presiding Deity, and to Krsna as the ultimate cause.

We see that Srila Prabhupada encouraged his disciples to worship savitur as the sun, and then as the sun god, trusting that through purification we would come to the conclusion of worshiping Krsna.

Varenyam invokes our relationship as worshippers of savita's effulgence. When our worship matures, it directs us neither to consciousness, nor sunlight, nor Laksmi-devi, hut to Sri Radha.

As cited earlier, Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati says that Gaudiya Vaisnavas worship Krsna due to His being Srimati Radharani's beloved. Interestingly, the grammar of Brahma-gayatri says the same.

Varenyam is an adjective modifying bhargah, and ultimately Radha is that effulgence (bhargah). She is our worshipable Deity, Krsna's (savita's) internal potency, and so we worship Him.

The same conclusion is implicit in our chanting of the maha-mantra. First we call to Srimati Radharani (Hare) before repeating the names of Krsna (Krsna or Rama). In short, we are Her worshippers first.

But because the truths and pastimes of Radha are most confidential, we are encouraged to worship Krsna first, and as our devotion matures we consciously worship Krsna through Srimati Radharani.

Varenyam also means "the best of all effulgent things." Scriptures portray this effulgent thing as the spiritual world, Goloka Vrndavana, as well as the best (varenyam) of all transcendental attainments.

Since the root of varenyam is vrnoti ("to choose"), Brahma-gayatri sets our sights on the best of available choices, the choice of what to aspire for — to establish our relationship with the Absolute Truth.

In conclusion, although the relationship portion of Brahma-gayatri offers us a variety of meditations, it also encourages us to focus on the best of all alternatives, service to Radha and Krsna in Goloka.

**\*\*\*\***

Once Brahma-gayatri has invoked the ultimate relationship, it sets forth, with the vibration bhargo devasya dhimahi, the process (abhidheya) by which we can successfully develop that relationship.

We have already studied how bhargah's general meaning ("effulgence") is fine-tuned to its pre-eminent — and for Gaudiya Vaisnavas the most preferred—meaning, Lord Krsna's internal potency, Srlmati Radharani.

Jiva Gosvami says that just as the words brahma and visnu are names of the Supreme, so is bhargah; therefore, the Brahma-gayatri implies that we must cultivate a personal conception of God.

Bhargah also implies Krsna's personal potency (svarupa-sakti), the full display of His six opulences, the embodiment of spiritual love, the feminine counterpart of His transcendental form of mellows.

The scriptural injunction, "One should meditate upon the spiritual effulgence (bhargah) of the Supreme Lord," may then be taken to read, "One should meditate upon bhargah as the Lord's favourite, Radha."

Srila B. R. Sridhara Maharaja offers another correlation between bhargah and Sri Radha. He portrays Her as the effulgent pride and joy of the family of King Bhanu (Vrsabhanu), bhanu meaning the sun.

Once, when Krsna had returned to His friends after being with Sri Radha, Subala chided Him, "My dear enemy of Kamsa! While You were gone, the heat of separation became extraordinary.

"And we felt this heat more intensely when we understood that in Bhandiravana forest You were being refreshed by the waves of the cooling river known as Bhanu-tanaya \[Radharani\]."

The word devasya ("heavenly" or "divine") qualifies savitur by confirming its transcendental nature, as well as the nature of its effulgence (bhargah), pointing once again to the divine couple.

And as implied by varenyam's meaning, namely "best," devasya also tells us to go beyond worship of the fire, the sun, and their radiances to Radha and Krsna and Their eternal abode Goloka.

Even more accurate meanings of devasya as an adjective modifying savitur are "most beautiful" and "transcendentally playful," which can, objectively speaking, only indicate Krsna, no one else.
