In such a transcendental state — at times in His own home, at times in the homes of His devotees —Caitanya Mahaprabhu and His companions relish the limitless nectar of nama-sankirtana.

Spurred on by Advaita, Gadadhara, and Nityananda, the Lord’s festival of chanting and dancing continues into midday. And when it finally winds down, He wanders in groves and gardens by the Ganges.

The cooling breezes and humming bees remind Gauranga of the seasonal forests at Radha-kunda, where Krsna relishes the company of Radhika and the gopis in varieties of love-soaked pastimes.

Surcharged with spiritual excitement, Gaura narrates Krsna’s pastimes to His companions and makes them enact those pastimes in order to taste the many and varied ecstasies of vraja-rasa.

These are the pastimes of Sri Caitanya Mahaprabhu as He performs them in the first four portions of the day: nisanta — dawn; pratah — morning; purvahna — forenoon; and madhyahna — midday.

By afternoon the Lord and His friends arc completely intoxicated by ecstatic love; still, they eventually leave the gardens for their homes, winding their way through the villages of Navadvipa.

As He sings in a sweet voice and dances enchantingly, Caitanya Mahaprabhu remembers Krsna’s afternoon return from the pasturing grounds and the joy His return brings to the Vraja-vasis.

As the townspeople see Gaurariga’s dancing, hear His singing, and feel His ecstasy, they become as satisfied as cakora birds in moonlight. Stunned by divine love, they appear like statues.

At long last Gauranga returns home, where His mother and wife lavish loving attention upon Him. Meanwhile, His friends sadly part from Him, returning to their own homes to attend to religious duties.

At dusk, when Krsna bathes, milks the cows, and enjoys His supper, Caitanya Mahaprabhu absorbs Himself in those pastimes, describing them to His family and friends as if He had a thousand mouths.

Still talking, He accompanies Nitai and the others to the Ganges, where they worship and bathe in Mother Ganga’s waters and then don clean clothes and adorn themselves with fresh, fragrant garlands.

Upon returning to Gaura’s home, everyone takes part in the arati ceremony for Lord Visnu. Then they sit down to eat the remnants of food and betel nuts that were just offered to the Lord.

With the arrival of evening, Lord Caitanya recalls how Radha secretly leaves Her husband’s home hoping to meet Krsna in a grove by the Yamuna, where She anxiously waits until Krsna finally arrives.

Then Gaura — in Radha’s mood, His hands on His hips, moving as Radha does as She heads to a rendezvous with Krsna — walks along the path that leads to Srivasa’s courtyard, accompanied by Advaita.

Lord Caitanya orchestrates an evening of prṛmṁ-sankirtana, excessively eager to taste the nectar of Krsna’s names. In no time at all He is soaring through the firmament of transcendental mellows.

Throughout the night Caitanya Mahaprabhu dances and chants in the courtyard of Srivasa, following the dictation of His spiritual ecstasies and the rhythm of musical instruments like the mrdanga.

Absorbed in thoughts of the rasa dance, Gaura dances with Gadadhara in the most astonishing ways, His body displaying every sign of ecstasy — sometimes one by one, sometimes all at once.

At the end of the festivities the Lord rests in a garden and eats some fruit. He then bids His companions farewell and returns home, where He falls asleep upon a jewelled bedstead and dreams of Krsna.

Such are the pastimes of Caitanya Mahaprabhu in the last four portions of the day: aparahna— afternoon; sayam— dusk; pradosa— evening; and nakta — night. Then a similar day begins at dawn.

It is said that if one recalls Gauranga’s pastimes before meditating on the pastimes of Sri Krsna, then the Lord of Navadvipa, immensely pleased, is eager to award that sadhaka the greatest good fortune.

Gaura Nataraja, the Deity of Lord Caitanya in New Vraja-dhama, is identical to Syamasundara in every way. Only because He thinks of Himself as Radha has His complexion changed from black to gold.

One night in London I dreamt that I was walking through the temple in New Vraja-dhama. As I entered Radha-Syamasundara’s sanctuary, I saw that Syamasundara had turned golden like Gauranga.

Obviously under the influence of intense spiritual emotion, Syama swayed back and forth like a vine of flowers in the wind. His rolling eyes shed diamond-drop tears that fell and washed His lotus feet.

While repeating, “Gaura! Gaura!” in a voice that defies description, beautiful golden-complexioned Syamasundara raised His strong arms. Gazing at me with a singular intensity, He commanded:

“Always offer Me a garland in the evening. It need not be big, but I want a garland.” Then He smiled and the entire scene was gone. I immediately woke up and wrote down what I had seen.

Since then Syamasundara has received a garland every evening at the time that He is dressed. And I always remember His beautiful, golden, dancing form that is just like the form of Gaura Nataraja.

## Chapter 34 - The Eighteen-Syllable Gopala Mantra

By the mercy of Lord Caitanya, we have briefly heard about the essential qualities of the Gaura mula-mantra and aura-gayatri, let us now hear the glories of the eternal eighteen-syllable Gopala mantra.

Srila Prabhupada translates the Gopala mantra (*klim krsnaya govindaya gopijana-vallabhaya svahad*) as follows: “I make offering of oblations unto Lord Krsna, who is Govinda, the lover of the gopis.”

When we chant this mantra without offence, by its power we become mad for Krsna, and awake or asleep, our constant meditation, then, is to follow the path of perfection chalked out by the mantra.

First I will speak briefly of the Gopala mantra’s glories, providing some scriptural references. Then I will more thoroughly examine the mantra, discussing the import and effect of the individual words.

Arms raised I say, “Jaya! Jaya!” to Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati Thakura. He conquered petty sectarianism. Now, sincere Vaisnavas the world over are being initiated into the Gopala mantra, as was Lord Brahma.

All glories to my spiritual master, Srila Prabhupada, who turned many depraved denizens of Kali-yuga into followers of Rupa Gosvami and enabled them to accept this matchless gift of Sarasvati Thakura.

Krsna’s pastimes go on eternally in Goloka Vrndavana, hut by the will of that all-merciful Lord, pure devotees see some of those pastimes in their hearts, even while they remain within this world.

Now that Krsna has departed for His own abode, His pastimes go on in this world in an unmanifest form, pastimes which can he seen only if anointed by the power of krsna-mantra and krsna-ndrna.

**“Simply by chanting the holy name of Krsna one can obtain freedom from material existence. Indeed, simply by chanting the Hare Krsna mantra one will be able to see the lotus feet of the Lord.”**

We procure that ointment of pure love by chanting the Hare Krsna maha-mantra without offence and enhancing it with aspirations refined through the pure chanting of the Gopala mantra.

It is said, muktir hitvdnyatha rupam sva-rupena vyavasthitih: “Liberation is the permanent situation of the form of the living entity after he gives up the changeable gross and subtle material bodies.”

With these words Sukadeva Gosvami stresses the point that the sure symptom of our being completely free from material contamination is the attainment of our eternal spiritual form (svarupa-siddhi).

The determined sages of Dandakaranya forest, for example, became muni-cari-gopis in Krsna’s earthly pastimes after attaining perfect liberation through the agency of the Gopala mantra.

In order to attain Krsna’s company in the rasa dance, these sages diligently chanted the Gopala mantra while residing in the Dandaka forest during the time of Lord Ramacandra’s exile from Ayodhya.

One day Lord Rama — with matted hair, wearing deerskin, and carrying a bow —appeared before the sages. By dint of their spiritual vision they recognised Him as Gopala, but in another form.

The sages rose from their meditation to greet the Lord, thinking, “Our Lord Gopala is here without His flute or stick.” They then bowed down and offered eloquent prayers to Lord Ramacandra.

And when the Lord said, “Please! Choose a benediction,” they replied, “We all desire to be like Sita!” But the Lord had already taken the vow to accept only one wife (eka-patni-vrata), so He said:

“I cannot fulfil your desire at the present time. But at the end of Dvapara-yuga, when in Mathura I again appear in My original form as Gopala, then you will all become as Sita is now. That I promise!”

A mantra and its presiding deity are said to be one and the same. As the origin of all Visnu forms, Krsna is greater than all the demigods, and so mantras composed of Krsna’s names are similarly supreme.

The Gautamiya-tantra declares,

**“Of all mantras the Visnu mantras are said to be the best. Of these, the Krsna mantras, which are the abode of happiness and liberation, are the best of all.”**

In His original form, Krsna is a cowherd boy, and so amongst Krsna mantras, those glorifying Gopala are best. And amongst Gopala mantras, the eighteen-syllable mantra is the most powerful.

Mantras aiming at temporary goals must be purified, but the Gopala mantra needs no cleansing, for it is self-manifest; indeed, it bestows the desired result upon anyone, regardless of varna or asrama.

A sincere devotee may sometimes stutter, mispronounce, or stop midway through the Gopala mantra, or even chant in a contaminated state, but such faults will not deprive him of this mantra’s benefits.

The opulences of the Gopala mantra are enhanced by the karna-bija (klim). This seed also graces the beginning of the next mantra in the sequence of Gaudiya diksa-mantras, the Kama-gayatri.

All things considered, the Gopala mantra is said to be the best amongst initiation mantras, a truth supported by statements in the Brahma-samhita and the Gopala-tapani Upanisad.

The Brahma-samhita first mentions the Gopala mantra in relation to the shape of Goloka Vrndavana, whose innermost portion is hex-agonal, each side corresponding to a word of the Gopala mantra.

Krsna is identical to His name and His abode, and so He takes on the Gopala mantra’s geometric form to define the borders of His home, in which He resides with Radha, surrounded by Their companions.

The Brahma-samhita next mentions the Gopala mantra in the description of Brahma’s birth. Despite his desire to create, Brahma was unable to do so, and thus goddess Sarasvati instructed him:

**“O Brahma, this mantra —** ***klim krsnaya govindaya gopi-jana-vallabhaya svaha*** **— will surely fulfil your heart’s desire. If you meditate on this mantra while executing penance, you will attain perfection.”**

Following her advice, Brahma was able to recreate the universe as it was before. Krsna, duly pleased, then initiated Brahma into the chanting of Kama-gayatri, by which Brahma achieved all success.

Because klim, the seed of desire, precedes the Gopala mantra, Brahma’s desire to create was fully satisfied. But because Brahma also desired to please Krsna by creating, his heart was purified as well.

By chanting the Kama-gayatri mantra in that purified state of mind, Brahma directly saw merciful Govinda, beheld the wonders of Goloka, and concluded, “I am an eternal maidservant of Sri Krsna.”

Therefore, like Brahma, we must dovetail our desires with Krsna’s satisfaction, otherwise those desires will distract us from pure devotion and, unlike Brahma, we will fail to meet the Lord.

The Gopala mantra is like a cintamani. After receiving it, we should be careful to not be cheated in the same way Devahuti was by false attachment to her husband. She said to Kardama Muni:

**“My Lord! Surely I have been cheated by the insurmountable illusory energy of Krsna, for in spite of having obtained your company, which gives liberation from bondage, I did not seek such liberation.’’**

**\*\*\*\***

The first part of the Gopala-tapani Upanisad contains an indepth glorification of the Gopala mantra in the form of a highly philosophical exchange between Brahma and an assembly of sages.

Their dialogue begins with the sages’ questions: Who is the Supreme Lord? Whom docs death fear? By knowing whom is there nothing left to be known? By whose authority does this creation move?

To these questions, Brahma replied, “Krsna is the Lord; death fears Govinda and never visits His devotees; Gopijana-vallabha is the end of all things knowable; and Krsna is the supreme controller.”

The sages then inquired further, “Who are the persons named Krsna, Govinda, and Gopijanavallabha?” Brahma replied that they are all one and the same Supreme Personality of Godhead.

Brahma then thoroughly explained these three names and introduced the mantra formed by their proper combination, assuring the sages that by chanting this mantra one will see Krsna and attain liberation.

Charmed by the creator’s revelations, the sages asked to know what Krsna looks like, how He can be pleased, and how He should be worshipped. Again Brahma answered their questions thoroughly.

In essence, Brahma said that although Krsna is worshipped with the Gopala mantra for many different reasons, pure Vaisnavas desire to serve Krsna with an unmotivated mood, typical of the Vraja-vasis.

The eighteen-syllable Gopala mantra can be elaborately worshipped in the form of a yantra, but pure chanting of the mantra yields the same result — it directs the worshipper back to Godhead.

Brahma concluded, “The Gopala mantra is Krsna’s form. With this mantra one should meditate upon Him and His pastimes, and offer homage to Him, the presiding Deity of the phrase om tat sat.”

By studying the truths and histories of the Gopala mantra, Jiva Gosvami concludes that Krsna is the mantra’s source, its syllables, its presiding Deity, as well as the personality it aims to worship.

There are other Gopala mantras with fewer syllables, such as the one chanted by Lord Caitanya, klim gopijana-vallabhaya svaha. These are all derived from the eighteen-syllable Gopala mantra.

Lord Siva, while once instructing the goddess Durga, said that there are thirty-three unique Gopala mantras, of which the eighteen-syllable one is the mantra-raja, the king and the most potent of all.

Lord Brahma gave that eighteen-syllable mantra to Siva, and Siva in turn gave it to Narada. Because the Kumaras desired liberation, they too received the mantra and, by its power, achieved their goal.

In the line of Nimbarkacarya, Lord Narayana gave the same eighteen- syllable mantra to His incarnation, Hamsa, who then initiated the four Kumaras, who in turn initiated Narada, and Narada, Nimbarka.

Some demigods received yet another Gopala mantra, Gopa-kumara chanted a version with an undisclosed number of syllables, and the Pancaratras mention a tantric form that begins orh srim.

And during Caitanya Mahaprabhu’s childhood, a mendicant brahmana who worshipped Bala-gopala received the mercy of little Nimai by chanting a six-syllable version of the Gopala mantra.

As we have already heard, all great mantras contain syllables or phrases that represent the three fundamental principles discussed in the Vedas: relationship, cultivation, and the final goal.

These principles of relationship, cultivation, and attainment are present in the words krsnaya, govindaya, and gopijana-vallabhaya, respectively, but the Gopala mantra stresses the first two principles.

Relationship and cultivation, then, are the subjects of the Gopala mantra, and the ultimate goal implied by gopijana-vallabhaya is the subject of Kama-gayatri, which is chanted after the Gopala mantra.

By coming to understand through mature contemplation the six principles of relationship, we are fixed in devotional service, which in its full maturity reveals the highest goal — service to Sri Radha.

Bhaktivinoda Thakura derives these six principles from the meanings of six words of the Gopala mantra, in the order in which they appear: *krsnaya, govindaya, gopijana, vallabhdya, sva,* and *ha*.

The six principles of sambandha-jdana we must come to know are: (1) Krsna’s transcendental form; (2) His blissful Vrndavana pastimes; (3) His Vraja-vasi companions — especially the gopis; (4) the gopis' process of surrender; (5) the spiritual form of the soul; and (6) the aspects of eternal service to Krsna that arise upon attaining our eternal spiritual body (ekadasa-bhava).

In analysing the words of the Gopala mantra for the benefit of the assembled sages, Lord Brahma defined Krsna as the one who removes and forgives the sins of humans and demons alike.

The creator described Govinda as the Lord of Vrndavana’s cows, of the earth, and of the infallible Vedas; and he portrayed Gopijana- vallabha as the beloved of the gopis, most notably of Sri Radha.

Finally, Brahma describes svaha by saying, tan-maya ceti: “and the Lord’s (sva) energy (ha)” A word-by-word analysis that gives the details of each word of the mantra will follow shortly.

**\*\*\*\***

Our offenceless chanting of the Gopala mantra — like all mantras composed of Krsna’s names — induces us to remember Krsna. And remembering Krsna naturally inspires us to glorify Him.

This takes place in much the same way as an electric current creates a magnetic field around the conductor through which it passes, or how a magnetic field induces a flow of electricity through a conductor.

Remembrance of Krsna takes two forms. The first is mantropasana-mayi, in which we make an effort to fix the mind on a single pastime of Krsna going on in Vraja at a particular place and time of day.

The other is called svarasiki, wherein one’s mind effortlessly flows through a sequence of pastimes unique to various places and times, in a way that is “appropriate for one’s own-original aptitude of love.”

The first of these two levels of meditation is in the realm of regulated devotional service with the bud of attachment, the second in mature spontaneous service born of blossoming attachment.

The Caitanya-caritamrta defines Lord Brahma’s meditation as upasana-mayi. Krsnadasa Kaviraja Gosvami writes, *yanra dhyana nija-loke kare padmasana astadasaksara-mantre kare upasana*:

**“Lord Brahma, sitting on his lotus seat in his own abode, always meditates on Him \[Krsna\] and worships \[upasana\] Him with the \[Gopala\] mantra consisting of eighteen syllables.”**

The following verse exemplifies this kind of meditation, in which Lord Brahma paints for us a static picture of Govinda.

**“I worship Govinda, the primeval Lord, who plays His transcendental flute —**

**“His eyes are like lotus flowers, He is decorated with peacock plumes, and His bodily colour resembles a fresh black cloud, although His bodily features are more beautiful than millions of cupids.”**

**In his Sri Radha-krsnasta-kaliya-lila-smarana-mangala-stotra, Srila Rupa Gosvami offers an example of svarasiki meditation, of which I cite a portion: “I take shelter of Srimati Radharani.**

**“Early in the morning, after bathing, dressing, and ornamenting Herself, Srimati Radharani accepts an invitation from Krsna’s mother, Mother Yasoda, to cook for Him.**

**“Along with Her girlfriends, Radhika goes to Nandagrama to prepare varieties of delicious foodstuffs, after which She takes His remnants, and occasionally also gets to meet Him in a secluded place.**

**“I also take shelter of Krsna. After rising from His bed, He follows a routine of going to the cowshed to milk the cows, taking a bath, and then eating His meals in the company of His friends.**

**“I remember Sri Krsna, who in the forenoon goes to the forest along with the cowherd boys and the cows, accompanied for a distance by Nanda Maharaja, Mother Yasoda, and other Vraja-vasis.”**

In this way svarasiki meditation flows from one daily activity of the divine couple to another, one day after another, as They interact with Their eternal associates in celestial Vrndavana.

Whether it be upasana or svarasiki meditation, we might wonder how it is possible to remember such intricate descriptions of the Lord’s attributes, form, or pastimes while chanting the Gopala mantra.

The first step is to memorise verses recommended by our gurus or the previous acaryas. Caitanya Mahaprabhu, for example, personally taught the verses of the Brahma-samhita to His disciples.

Of that sacred text, Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati Thakura writes,

**“Readers are requested to study and try to enter into the spirit of this hymn, with great care and attention, as a regular daily function.”**

The second step, then, is to study the verses and reach a proper understanding of their content. Subsequently, as we chant the Gopala mantra we should “try to enter into the spirit” of the verses.

We might, for instance, fix an image of Govinda in our mind based upon the picture Brahma paints in the verse venum kvanantam, concentrating on it with “great care and attention.” That is smarana.

Because our daily practice of chanting our diksa-mantras takes relatively little time, our meditation on the Gopala mantra — or the other mantras in the sequence — better lends itself to upasana-mayi.

However, we dedicate at least a couple of hours daily to japa of the Hare Krsna maha-mantra, which provides ample time to meditate in the manner of svarasiki if we are suitably accomplished.

The sincere endeavour to fix our mind on verses that elaborate on the words of the Gopala mantra will help us to enter into the mantra’s mood and keep the mind from wandering here and there.

Since Govinda is the centrepiece of the Gopala mantra, we can also meditate on the following sloka by Srila Rupa Gosvami, which also happens to have been a great favourite of Srila Prabhupada’s.

The acarya writes,

**“My dear friend! If you are indeed attached to your worldly friends, do not look at the smiling face of Lord Govinda as He stands on the bank of the Yamuna at Kesi-ghata.**

**“Casting sidelong glances, He places His flute to His lips, which seem like newly blossomed twigs. His transcendental body, bending in three places, appears very bright in the moonlight.”**

If we feel neither competent nor inclined to meditate like this, then at least we should attentively concentrate on the names of Krsna in the mantra. That will also awaken remembrance of Him.

In whichever way we chant the Gopala mantra, however, we must warily avoid inattentiveness through indifference, laziness, or distraction. Such carelessness will rob us of the benefits of chanting.

**\*\*\*\***

Beginners in the process of sadhana-bhakti will likely find the mental discipline required for steady meditation on the Gopala mantra challenging, but. pujaris may well avoid such difficulties with case.

Srila Prabhupada once said that “the pujaris are the most fortunate.” This is because they live within the pastimes of Krsna in His Deity form, the same Krsna other devotees try to capture in their minds.

Indeed, pujaris live the conclusion of the Vedas. In Prabhupada’s own words,

**“Anyone who engages in the worship of the Deity, ...is understood to be studying all the Vedas twenty-four hours a day.”**

From the time they wake the Deities until the time they put the Deities to rest, pujaris think about and participate in the different services to Radha and Krsna described in the pancaratrika system.

And even though the pastimes performed by the Deities of Radha and Krsna are not identical to the unmanifest pastimes of the divine couple described by the Gosvamis, they are pastimes nonetheless.

Waking the Deities, cooking an offering, performing arati, and dressing the Deities’ divine forms are all Radha’s and Krsna’s pastimes, and hence a golden opportunity to meditate upon Them.

Especially the head pujari, who thinks not only of his or her allotted duties but of the services offered by all the devotees to the Deities throughout the day, is immersed in svarasiki meditation.

Indeed, pujaris who take advantage of their good fortune can purify themselves through absorption in their service to the Deities and quickly arrive at absorption in Radha’s and Krsna’s eternal pastimes.

In Deity forms, Radha and Krsna perform Their manifest pastimes. Simultaneously, They perform unmanifest pastimes in Their original forms. This is certainly true for Radha-Syama in New Vraja-dhama.

For the attentive pujari, such direct access to the pastimes of Their Lordships greatly enhances the spiritual rewards of chanting the Gopala mantra. And for the inattentive, a great opportunity is lost.

Of course, Deity worship also means mandira-marjanadi — cleansing and managing the temple, as well as preaching activities like book distribution, which glorify and directly ornament the Deities.

At times Srila Prabhupada would even call the managers’ activities “better” when they directly serve the Deities, because such service sanctifies all their other duties. This is the pancaratrika path.

Thus devotees in Hungary should be conscious that in their daily activities they properly serve and represent Radha-Syamasundara, just as devotees elsewhere should serve their own presiding Deities.

How can we hope to invoke the divine couple’s unmanifest pastimes by chanting the Gopala mantra if we are not fully conscious of the pastimes Radha-Syama perform before our eyes in Their Deity forms?

With the hope of fixing the reader’s mind on Radha-Syamasundara, I will now briefly describe Their manifest daily pastimes (nitya-lila) and a few of Their occasional pastimes (naimittika-lila).

A certain degree of similarity may be apparent between these manifest pastimes and the unmanifest ones, for the same Supreme Person — with His unique, individual personality — performs them both.

A saintly pujari rings a silver bell. A snowstorm may be raging or summer birds may be chirping, but at the end of every night one of Radha-Syama’s servants stirs Them, softly saying, “Please wake up.”

Seeing Their Lordships still weary, that servant washes the divine couple’s mouths, faces, and feet, and offers Them milk sweets, lovingly prepared by carefully following Srila Prabhupada’s recipes.

At the appropriate time, the curtains to the altar open and the eager residents of New Vraja-dhama greet Radha-Syamasundara with melodiously chanted verses as the pujari offers Them mangala-arati.

In the morning hours that follow, the Deity room and its adjoining service area and kitchen arc all abuzz with blissful activity, much like Radha’s personal quarters in Yavata, or Krsna’s in Nandagrama.

The sun hurries to witness these activities, and all the while a few fortunate servants remove Radha-Syamasundara’s nightclothes, bathe Them, and then dress Them in fresh clothes and ornaments.

At the times when my devotional creeper sprouts a blossom of opportunity and allows me to dress Radha-Syama myself, I feel more privileged than if I were visiting Vaikuntha with ail opulences in tow.

As I approach Srimati Radharani, I consider myself an instrument of my illustrious guru, and I try to dress Her in a way that will bring the greatest pleasure to Syamasundara, for this is my service to Him.

From first wiping Radha’s body with a damp silken cloth, to placing a musk dot on Her chin — the final cosmetic touch — I chant my personal mangalacarana prayers, focusing on one prayer in particular.

**“O Gandharvika! Just as compassionate Lord Visnu never rejects a surrendered soul, no matter how wicked that soul may have been, please don’t reject me! Forgive my sins and accept me as Your own.”**

Just as the cowherd boys make much ado of blowing their horns and playing their flutes once Krsna is dressed for the day, so the devotees of New Vraja-dhama receive Radha-Syama with rapturous kirtana.

The vital ingredient in nectarean kirtana is the worship of Srila Prabhupada. It waters the creeper of devotion, wins the favour of Radha-Syamasundara, and makes us the objects of Their mercy.

Each of us has a story to tell about mercy received during the sort of otherworldly kirtanas that make the temple resound with divine names, tremble with dancing feet, and shine with blissful smiles.

And when we sit for the daily class, Radha-Syamasundara— through the pages of Srimad-Bhagavatam — speak to every one of Their servants individually, calling them back to home, back to Godhead.

During the forenoon, Radha-Syamasundara receive Their guests, but Their servants who are elsewhere busily engaged in the diverse duties that help maintain New Vraja-dhama miss seeing Them.

At that time, devotees in the kitchen lovingly prepare the divine couple’s lunch in much the same way that Mother Yasoda cooks Krsna’s midday meal and then has it delivered to Him in the pasture.

When lunch — made up of Krsna’s favourite vegetables, savouries, and sweets — is ready to be served, a pujari offers it to the divine couple, and after They have eaten to Their satisfaction, we offer arati.

Thakura Bhaktivinoda enjoins that at this time of day we should remember in song how in Nandagrama, under Yasoda’s supervision, Krsna and the gopas enjoy a feast cooked by Radha and Her friends.

Then, as devotees savour the sublime remnants of the divine couple, the curtains on the altar close, and stay closed until mid-afternoon, to give Radha-Syamasundara privacy to enjoy confidential pastimes.

**\*\*\*\***

During the summer months, when visitors flock to New Vraja-dhama from near and far, the curtains remain open even after lunch — such is the selfless magnanimity of Radha-Syamasundara.

In some cases, guests travel hundreds of kilometres to see New Vraja- dhama. Should they be deprived of the most important part of their visit, a glimpse of the dual form of the Absolute Truth? Certainly not!

Most visitors chant the holy names, fan the Deities, touch an imprint of Krsna’s feet, and dance to the rhythm of karatalas and mrdanga, tasting transcendence and thus beginning their spiritual lives.

Many guests have been transformed, even liberated, by the indelible impressions made on their hearts by the enchanting forms, smiling faces, and unbounded kindness of Radha-Syamasundara.

One agnostic bus driver used to daily chauffeur guests to Krsna Valley, but he would always flatly refuse to enter the temple. Nonetheless, he would always accept maha-prasada sweets.

By the influence of the Deities’ remnants, the driver one day started accompanying guests into the temple room, eventually teaching them to fan Radha-Syamasundara, exclaiming to them, “This is God!”

When the tour season was over he drove away with tears in his eyes. But he soon returned on his own, driving out weekly from Budapest just to pull the fan over Radha-Syamasundara during bhoga-arati.

There was also an elderly lady from the local area who would visit the temple from time to time, stand respectfully before Radha' Syamasundara, and listen to the devotees chant Hare Krsna.

Finally the time came for her to pass away. As her family gathered round, in her last moments she opened her eyes and asked, “Is Krsna here?” Then, after a pause, said, “Yes! Krsna is here!” and then died.

These are just two examples of Radha-Syamasundara’s occasional pastimes of delivering the poor and unfortunate, pastimes They enact constantly, but especially in the daytime during tourist season.

In the afternoon Their Lordships again rise from bed, enjoy a light snack, and then — at the time Krsna, His friends, and the cows begin their triumphant daily return home — They accept another arati.

By this time a few devotees have gathered in the temple room to sing bhajanas and chant Hare Krsna until the Deities’ evening pastimes are over and They have been affectionately tucked in for the night.

But as Their devotees chant, and before They retire for the day, Radha-Syamasundara take one more meal and accept gaura-arati to remind us that Gaura Nataraja is the same as Radha-Syama.

Moreover, Navadvipa is Vrndavana, but it appears differently to facilitate the mood and mission of Caitanya Mahaprabhu, who comes as a devotee of the divine couple to freely distribute Their names.

In Vraja, just before Krsna returns to Nandagrama, the demigods headed by Brahma often descend to worship Krsna, sing His praises, and offer Him arati —as the cowherd boys look on in amusement.

Those same demigods worship Caitanya Mahaprabhu in Navadvipa by the hanks of the Jahnavi at the home of Srivasa Thakura, where they relish nama-sankirtana, their voices choked in ecstatic love.

As evening arrives the Deities are changed into nightclothes before They offer Their devotees one final darsana, just as Krsna pleases the eyes of His subjects as He is being entertained in Nanda’s court.

I sometimes help dress Radha-Syamasundara in the evening. As They are getting dressed, the divine couple, pleased by the devotees’ heart-stirring kirtana, show Their pleasure by broadening Their smiles.

Non-devotees may object, insisting that someone who sees a statue smile is hallucinating. Even some Vaisnavas argue that, while the Lord can do anything, who are we to say that we see Him smile.

To these doubters I reply that the all-powerful Lord is fully present in His Deity and can do whatever He likes, which includes revealing His smile to anyone He deems a worthy object of such mercy.

Once asked whether seeing the Deity smiling wider than usual was imagination, Srila Prabhupada said, “Regarding Radharani’s smiling, that is not imagination — so She must smile. Let this be an impetus.”

Indeed, many devotees from New Vraja-dhama and elsewhere have confirmed that Radha-Syamasundara’s facial features change, especially when Their devotees are intensely chanting Their names.

Syamasundara has a tendency to smile into the right corner of His ruby red lips, making His mouth appear slightly crooked, thereby intensifying His enchanting spell over both the devotees and Radha.

In my estimation, Radha’s smile is more restrained and harder to win than that of Her beloved. Only when one is sold out to Syama through chanting His names does She visibly reveal Her pleasure.

With nightfall the Deity curtains close, as do the pastimes Radha- Syama make visible to the residents of New Vraja-dhama. And for ten minutes, a sole pujari enjoys a very special, intimate service.

When Radha-Syamasundara are tucked in at night, with Syamasundara’s flute placed at His feet along with an offering of khir, an extraordinary atmosphere pervades the Deity room.

This extraordinary mood is a result of the Lord’s reciprocating with His servant, the pleasure of a day’s service completed, and perhaps the divine couple’s anticipation of Their nocturnal pastimes.

After Their Lordships have been put to bed, a few devotees, whether it be on a balmy summer evening or a cold wintry night, usually walk with me back to Radha-Damodara’s temple, where I reside.

Then, the intensity of New Vraja-dhama’s spiritual atmosphere seems to increase. Srila Prabhupada once said that Krsna’s presence is most keenly felt in Vraja between midnight and two in the morning.

Night is a time when Radha-Syama are supposed to be asleep. But we know that in Their unmanifest pastimes They sneak out of Their homes to meet and enjoy nocturnal festivals of chanting and dancing.

On some nights I wake up and stand on the balcony outside my room, overlooking Seva-kunja and Nidhuvana, hoping to hear the sounds of the rasa dance. Sadly, my ears have yet to be so blessed.

As I survey this sacred home of Radha-Syamasundara’s pastimes, I look up at the stars and invariably think, and often say aloud, “For me, there is no other place like New Vraja-dhama — anywhere.”

It is said that home is where the heart is. In this way, Radha-Syama display Their daily activities, inviting us to participate in and always remember those pastimes, not just at the times we chant Gayatri.

Many occasional pastimes of Radha-Syamasundara take place throughout the year, such as Janmastami, Govardhana-puja, Gaura-purnima, and, my favourite of them all, autumn’s holy name weekend.

These, like the daily pastimes of the divine couple, leave lasting impressions in our minds, impressions that may inspire an upasana meditation as we chant the Gopala mantra.

Radha-Syamasundara give devotees accomplished in such worship the spiritual intelligence with which to enter into deeper realms of remembrance — *dadami buddhi-yogam tam yena mam upayanti te*.

Worship of the divine couple in Their Deity forms can help us to remember Them always. Moreover, by practice such worship can eventually bring us to the stage of ecstatic love for Radha and Krsna.

Srila Prabhupada establishes these points beautifully,

**“Krsna says four things, that ‘Always think of Me.’ Now, here is Krsna, Radharani, Krsna standing before you. Don’t think that this is idol. No!**

**“Krsna is present before you in that form to show you favour that you can handle with Krsna. You can dress Him. You can give Him for eating. You can...To give you facilities… But Krsna is here —**

**“So He says, personally...‘Always think of Me.’ So you are seeing Krsna, and the impression is within your mind. As soon as you will close your eyes, you will see Krsna within your heart.**

**“Krsna, Radharani is here, and if you see constantly, naturally the impression will be within your heart. So either you are in temple or outside the temple, you’ll be able to see Krsna always — if you practice.**

**“*****Premanjana-cchurita-bhakti-vilocanena sentah sadaiva hrdayesu*****. Those who are saintly persons, on account of love for Krsna, they see always Krsna within his heart. So this practice is not very difficult.”**

In glorifying service to Radha-Syama and showing how such service is linked to remembering Their Lordships while chanting the Gopala mantra, I did not mention the other Deities of New Vraja-dhama.

That is because Radha-Syamasundara are the presiding Deities of New Vraja-dhama. Everyone else, including the other forms of Godhead, consider themselves to be servants of the divine couple.

I worship Radha and Syama, the youthful couple who have appeared by Their own sweet will in New Vraja-dhama to enjoy loving pastimes as the presiding Deities of Their devotees’ hearts.

May that transcendental cowherd boy and His enchanting pleasure potency appear in our minds and bestow the treasure of loving service upon us as we faithfully chant Their names with attention.

**\*\*\*\***

Srila Prabhupada translates the Gopala mantra — *êlim krsnaya govindaya gopijana-vallabhaya svaha* — in this way;

**“I make offering of oblations unto Lord Krsna, who is Govinda, the lover of the gopis.”**

Now let us hear the meanings of the mantra’s seed, the kama-bija (klim). The word kama means “desire,” which testifies to the fact that klim fulfils the material and spiritual desires of those who chant it.

As mentioned earlier, the kama-bija is also not different from Radha and Krsna and Their mutual love; consequently, klim is especially the source of the pure amorous love we aspire for as Gaudiya Vaisnavas.

Celestial Vraja, with its flowing rivers, proud mountains, and charming forests is directly manifest from klim, and so it is saturated with the nectarean sweetness of that pure amorous love.

New Vraja-dhama is also saturated with the divine bliss issuing from klim, which devotees may envision to surround the platform upon which stands the throne of the eternally youthful cowherd couple.

The cowherd girl is the personification of the ecstatic love, both delightful and sweet, that emanates as a tender, soft golden radiance from each of Her perfectly formed limbs. Her name is Radha.

The cowherd boy is the source, embodiment, and connoisseur of the original divine mellow that colours His handsome form with cloud-like splendour and makes His eyes restless with bliss. His name is Syama.

These two innocent-looking youths, Radha and Syama, have artfully colluded to steal the hearts of those who but once see Them or chant Their names, or who render Them even the tiniest service.

With the rope of the kama-bija— extending from Their lotus feet — attached to the collar of the Gopala mantra, They capture and enslave Their devotees at the time of brahminical initiation.

Only with the greatest effort born of unimaginable misfortune could we who have once committed ourselves to Radha-Syamasundara’s followers forget the divine couple, even for a moment.

Krsna once stood at Vamsivata and blew into His flute the kama-bija, which acted like an hypnotic incantation. Capturing the minds, bodies, and souls of the gopis, it brought them running to His side.

But the effect of the flute - song intoxicated Krsna as well, and so instead of embracing the gopis when they arrived, He instructed them in the principles of dharma, which chaste ladies already know.

The gopis were not to be discouraged so easily, however, and with sweet words and steady determination they convinced Krsna to accept them in order to fulfil His eternal promise to His devotees.

Similary, we should never become discouraged. We must resolutely serve Radha-Syamasundara and Their home, despite the many obstacles we can expect to face in this conflict-ridden Age of Kali.

And to those of us who are steadily fixed in such determination, Krsna gives His assurance: “If you become conscious of Me, you will pass over all the obstacles of conditioned life by My grace.”

The kama-bija is more than just the seed of desire. Scriptures and acaryas describe it is that which both excites and fulfils desire. In other words, klim is both the cause and effect of desire.

The nature of transcendental desire, encapsulated in the seed mantra klim, is characterised by spiritual fulfilment. It is just the opposite of material desire, which is born from the frustration of deficiency.

Although the word kama is used to denote both spiritual and material desire, spiritual desire is more properly called aprakrta-kama, whereas material desire is prakrta-kama. Prakrta means “material.”

The pastimes Krsna enjoys with Srimati Radharani when He leaves the rasa dance with Her are symptomatic of His spiritual fullness, which Sukadeva carefully defined for the edification of non-devotees.

The sage said, *reme taya catma-rata atmaramo ’py akhanditah*:

**“Lord Krsna enjoyed with that gopi \[Sri Radha\], although He enjoys only within, being self-satisfied and complete in Himself.**

**“Thus by contrast He showed the wretchedness of ordinary lusty men and hardhearted women.”**

In other words, Krsna’s enjoying with Radha was not for the purpose of satisfying His senses.

The Svetasvatara Upanisad adds that the wise devotee who attains freedom from material existence — and in a spiritual body attains the Lord’s company in His abode — also realises spiritual fulfilment.

Krsna Himself uses similar terminology in describing those who have attained spiritual fulfilment. He calls them atmaramah (“spiritually self-satisfied”) and apta-kamah (“fulfilled in their desires”).

The Lord’s companions desire nothing for themselves- their only desire is to please Krsna. The wise concur that the desire to please Krsna, and the ability to do so, find their pinnacle in Sri Radha.

That said, kama-bija is often referred to as the gopis’ selfless desire to please Krsna. Klim is their love for Him, as well as a vessel comprising the basic elements of Radha’s and Krsna’s love for each other.

The Caitanya-caritamrta calls klim the means of worshipping (upasana) the ever fresh Cupid of Vrndavana, Sri Krsna — an effort that cannot bear fruit without the blessings of the gopis.

The kama-bija, then, identifies pure desire, shown through the medium of spiritual love. It also denotes three transcendental persons— Radha, Krsna, and Their combined form, Gaura.

That seed of desire expands into Goloka, assuming the form of a lotus of unlimited desires. And yet the same kama-bija is the centre of that lotus — manifest in sound as klim — which satisfies those desires.

That pure spiritual desire, filtered through matter within the hearts of the conditioned souls, transforms into the perverted desire to enjoy this world and entangles the souls in fruitive work and its reactions.

Aimed at satisfying the material senses, desire becomes the cause of material bondage, but aimed at satisfying the Supreme Lord Krsna’s senses, desire becomes the cause of spiritual emancipation.

By embracing the Gopala mantra and its kama-bija (klim), we make known our desire to be freed from the cycle of birth and death. We proclaim, “My desire is to serve Krsna, the master of the gopis!”

The elucidations of klim come in two divisions, each with opposite potentials. Those divisions are spiritual fulfilment, propelling one towards Krsna, or material attainment, fulfiling one’s selfish desires.

In fact everything that exists in the spiritual and material worlds can be explained in terms of the kama-bija (klim) because both realms exist as products of desire. In the Upanisads we find evidence of this.

Brahma says,

**“When I again desired to create, the Lord appeared before me as a gopa and showed me that the universe I was about to create was contained within the mantra I was chanting.”**

The Brahma-samhita also tells how Brahma chanted the Gopala mantra with a view to recreate the universe. In short, he worshipped the Lord in order to facilitate the fulfilment of his personal desire.

Empowered by the Lord, Brahma created water from the letter ka of the kama-bija, earth from la, fire from i, the moon from the candra- bindu, and the sun from all the letters combined.

From klim’s extension — the Gopala mantra — Brahma created sky from the word krsnaya, air from govindaya, a surabhi cow from gopijana, and the fourteen branches of knowledge from vallabhaya.

Brahma then completed his task by generating moving and non- moving beings, including humans. Some sages say that the universal ingredients were created from a different combination of words.

The primary identity of klim is Krsna Himself. Klim is not composed of alphabetical characters, it is the embodiment of Krsna formed when His limbs assume the functions of klim’s letters.

Once, as Sadasiva sat with Narada by the bank of the Yamuna, the Lord described how the letters of the kama-bija (klim) each correspond to different limbs of Sri Krsna’s transcendental body.

Sadasiva explained that ka denotes the upper portion of Hari’s head and face, la the lower portion, i denotes His upper torso, m His lower torso, and anusvara denotes His thighs and the rest of His legs.

Smrti states that Lord Krsna, although Himself the kama-bija, fulfils His desires by expanding Himself, and so to facilitate Krsna’s enjoyment, klim expands itself to be the fulfiler of His desires.

Furthermore, klim displays the means of Krsna’s spiritual fulfilment and the end of that fulfilment. This comprehensive form of the kama-bija is described in the Brhad-gautamiya-tantra as follows:

**“Ka is Krsna with His form of eternity, knowledge, and bliss; i is His energy, Radha, the queen of Vrndavana; l is the blissful happiness of Their love; and m is the sweetness of the bliss from Their kiss.”**

The spontaneous way in which Lord Krsna’s potencies fulfil His desires is portrayed in scripture with the words “everything is done naturally and systematically by His multifarious energies.”

Yet Krsna’s human-like nature impels Him to take a personal interest in fulfiling His own desires, and so in Vrndavana He is fully absorbed in His many pastimes, like playing with friends and attracting gopis.

The next chapter’s analysis of the Kama-gayatri will reveal Krsna as the transcendental Cupid, due to His romantic escapades in the groves of Vrndavana with Srimati Radharani and Her gopi friends.

In order to intensify His influence over Sri Radha, Krsna, like Cupid, equips Himself with arrows, which He shoots to convey the charm of His transcendental body and the needs only Radharani can fulfil.

To give Krsna His arsenal and help Him conquer Radha, the kama-bija takes the form of five flower-arrows: ka becomes a mango flower; la, an asoka; i, jasmine; m, a madhavi flower; and anusvara, a bakula.

In other words, klim can be defined in an endless number of ways. Why? Because, being non-different from pranava (omkara), it is an expression of everything that exists — *klim omkarasyaikatvam*.

It is vital for us practitioners to firmly resolve what we want from kama-bija. If, while absorbed in klim, we concentrate, mind and heart, on the Absolute Truth — Radha and Krsna — we will attain Them.

As Krsna explains to Brahma, to concentrate on the truth does not mean that we are at once free of all selfish desire. Selfless devotion can only be attained by steady, gradual progress towards it.

The essence of pursuing this gradual progression is to temper one’s worldly activities with the desire to follow the Lord’s order as He presents it through scripture and the teachings of self-realised souls.

If we take this approach towards worldly duties, those duties become indirect devotional service (gauna-bhakti), which is nurtured by diligent sadhana, including the chanting of the Gopala mantra.

By keeping loving devotion fixed as our ultimate goal, we become free of offences, attachment to Krsna grows in proportion to the strength and purity of our faith, and finally selfless love blossoms.

At initiation we receive the seed of love (klim). We need only chant our diksa-mantras alongside the maha-mantra and conduct ourselves theistically to nurture that seed into the tree of selfless love.

Sri Rupa describes the mentality of one who aspires for loving devotion,

*kva jano 'yam ativa pamarah kva durapam rati-bhagbhir apy adah*  
*iyam ullasayaty ajarjara gurur uttarsa-dhura tathapi mam*

**“Where am I, a fool? And where is that state \[of pure love\] even great devotees cannot attain? Still \[despite such a contradiction\], a new and intense thirst for that treasure now makes me tremble.”**

**\*\*\*\***

Having introduced the Gopala mantra and enumerated a few of the numberless glories of klim (the kama-bija), let us now hear about all six words that constitute this song of transcendence.

The presiding Deity of this mantra is Gopala, the protector of the cows who graze upon the luscious grasses of Vrndavana’s green pastures. It is from Him that this transcendental chant takes its name.

Gopala is always affectionate to all living entities because He considers them His parts and parcels. We should meditate, then, upon the following declaration: “I belong to Gopala, the Supreme Lord.”

Sri Íṁrṁ prays,

**“May playful Gopala’s flute-music, which calls the Surabhi cows by name, which is the mystic mantra that charms the gopis’ hearts, and which delights the deer of Vraja, bless you all.”**

The effect of Gopala’s beauty on young girls is just like that of Cupid’s weapons: the five flower-arrows, the sandalwood-scented breezes, and spring. Accordingly He is also called Madana-gopala.

A young cowherd girl once happened to hear a song glorifying Gopala’s extraordinary beauty. She meditated on what she had heard, became intoxicated with delight, and repeated the song in this way:

“Madana-gopala casually wears a peacock-feather crown in His black curling hair, and His flute music chases away the doe of chaste shyness from the hearts of the eternally young Vraja gopis.

“I pray that my mind may always dwell in thoughts of the form and qualities of Madana-gopala, who is a giant mango tree of amorous pastimes for the carefree parrot called Srimati Radharani.

“Madana-gopala stands under a splendid kalpa-vrksa tree by the shore of the Yamuna River. His hips are covered with a fine yellow silk dhoti and decorated with a saffron belt of softly tinkling bells.

“Youthful Madana-gopala’s dark complexion has stolen away the beautiful splendour of dark rain clouds, and His beautiful face is like a moon that enchants the cakora of King Vrsabhanu’s daughter.

“Beautifully decorated with ankle bells, bracelets, jewelled sharkshaped earrings, and a gunja necklace, Madana-gopala alluringly places the vamsi flute to His sublimely sweet bimba fruit lips.

“Madana-gopala’s smile is gentle and sweet, His eyes are extremely beautiful, and His heart always yearns to sport with gopis who are expert at enjoying the mellows of transcendental pastimes.

“A garland of forest flowers swarming with buzzing bees swings from Madana-gopala’s neck, His handsome, nicely rounded arms arc anointed with fragrant scents, and their hairs stand erect from bliss.

“Madana-gopala’s forehead is decorated with splendid jewelled tilaka, from His nose hangs a pearl set in gold, and His lotus face blossoms beneath the moonbeams of the gopis' eager, playful glances.

“His handsome, delicate, youthful body bends at the neck, waist and ankles, and He is the great hero who delights in chivalrous amorous duels in the cottages of ever fresh Vrndavana forest.

“Madana-gopala’s glistening limbs arc anointed with sandalwood, kunkuma, and other fragrant substances, the aroma of which blends with His own bodily fragrance and enslaves the gopis’ sense of smell.

“Like a cloud filled with the nectar of transcendental bliss, Madana-gopala’s statuesque form is inundated by undulating waves of transcendental amorous pastimes in an ocean without shores.

“Hosts of Cupids stand humiliated by the effulgence of the candramani toenails of Madana-gopala, the youthful prince of Vrajapura and connoisseur of wonderful amorous pastimes.”

Dear readers! This beautiful form of Gopala is present within His eighteen-syllable mantra. Simply chanting it invokes the appearance of Gopala in the way ancient mantras coax fire from arani wood.

Just as air originally enters the universe and is divided into five types, from the seed klim the one Lord Gopala reveals Himself in six features in the six words of this transcendental mantra.

We may reasonably enquire, “If Lord Gopala is indeed identical to and the cause of the seed of desire (klim), why and how does He appear from it in the form of the Gopala mantra’s other words?”

Just as Krsna, the cause of all causes, appears in the universe through Ksirodakasayi Visnu, thereby conforming to the rule that all incarnations appear as emanations from the third Purusa-avatara —

Similarly, Gopala, the cause of the kama-bija, appears from klim in the form of the names krsnaya, govindaya, and so on, in deference to the rule that a spiritual mantra’s words originate from its seed.

What happens when one meditates on Gopala via His mantra? Brahma answers emphatically, “He tastes nectar! He tastes nectar!” (y*o dhyayati rasayati bhajati so ’mrto bhavati so ’mrto bhavati*).

This verse glorifies the process of devotional practice and its ecstatic result. It begins with meditation directed by a regulated mood (dhyayati), which is synonymous with *mantropasana-mayi*.

The worship of Gopala by attentive chanting of His names in a mood of self-surrender grants practising devotees liberation from the influences of nescience, lust, and fruitive activities.

This freedom from material entanglement through constant communion with the all-auspicious Gopala mantra is a source of pleasure and happiness (rasayati) for the devotees of the Lord.

Continued purification of the heart results in bhajana — the awakening of a spontaneous desire to please Gopala always, a desire that impels us to remain perpetually engaged in Gopala’s service.

Such free-flowing, svarasiki absorption in thoughts of Gopala’s pastimes transports us beyond the stage of liberation to the realm of immortal nectar — so ’mrto bhavati so ’mrto bhavati.

Brahma twice declares the ultimate result of chanting the Gopala mantra to assure us that we will indeed taste nectar that excels even that drunk by the demigods: We will see Gopala face-to-face.

Jnanis devoid of faith in pure devotional service often question how it is that devotees so quickly achieve liberation in the Supreme, especially if they have not separately cultivated knowledge.

The answer is that bhajana — constant absorption in selfless service—immediately grants transcendental knowledge and detachment, which liberates one from the cycle of repeated birth and death.

Sri Suta Gosvami confirms this in Srimad-Bhagavatam:

**“By rendering devotional service unto Lord Sri Krsna, one immediately acquires causeless knowledge and detachment from the world.”**

The first stage of knowledge is to worship Gopala according to scriptural regulations — as Sri Krsna. Knowledge then progresses to the realisation that Gopala is the master of Gokula — Govinda.

And in its final phase, knowledge awakens attachment to Gopala as lover of the gopis — Gopijana-vallabha — an awakening that inspires attachment to spontaneous love like that of the gopis.

Brahma’s description of the Gopala mantra explains why and how the one Gopala appears in multiple moods: out of His unlimited compassion for the lost souls and by His inconceivable potencies.

Through klim, therefore, Gopala manifests three aspects that correspond to three successive degrees of knowledge and concomitant desire, culminating in the gopis’ acme of loving service to Gopinatha.

Regarding the principles of relationship, process, and goal, krsnaya represents the desire to know Gopala, govindaya, the desire to serve Him, and gopijana-vallabhaya, the desire to love Him.

Each of these three stages of spiritual advancement has its unique features, but devotional service is common to them all. They correspond to three aspects of the same person, Sri Krsna.

Grammatically speaking, the words Govinda and Gopijana-vallabha act as adjectives to modify the word Krsna. In this way the Gopala mantra stresses sambandha-jnana, the knowledge of relationship.

The mantra’s main Deity is Govinda, and so it also stresses abhidheya, learning how to serve Radha and Krsna in practice, because spiritual knowledge reveals its full value only through practical application.

The goal, inseparable from knowledge and cultivation, is also mentioned in the Gopala mantra, but the emphasis on prayojana is left to Kama-gayatri, which is exclusively devoted to Gopinatha.

Brahma first received the Gopala mantra from Sarasvati, but only after Krsna had initiated him into the Kama-gayatri did his spiritual realisation mature, and only then was he able to offer his samhita.

Gaudiya Vaisnavas follow in Lord Brahma’s footsteps, chanting first the Gopala mantra and then Kama-gayatri in order to realise fully the potencies, truths, and rewards of the kama-bija (klim).

**\*\*\*\***

After the kama-bija (klim) comes krsnaya, which means “unto Krsna.” This section elaborates on the name Krsna and how chanting it and offering obeisances to Him relates to the Gopala mantra.

Madana-mohana, Govindaji, and Sri Gopinatha are the presiding Deities that govern the three integral truths of the Gopala mantra — relationship, cultivation, and goal, respectively.

Of these Deities, Srila Prabhupada writes, “These three Deities are the life and soul of the Bengali Vaisnavas, or Gaudiya Vaisnavas, who have a natural aptitude for residing in Vrndavana.”

These Deities are the original gurus who instruct us in how to identify as Krsna’s servants, in the techniques of serving Radha and Krsna, and in the art of how to awaken love for the divine couple.

In New Vraja-dhama, these three Deities are all present in Syamasundara, by whose help, guidance, and blessings His devotees can attain perfection in Krsna consciousness without undue struggle.

Who is Krsna? Brahma told the Kumaras that Krsna is the Supreme Brahman, paramam daivatam. But they wondered, “How could Krsna, who looks like an ordinary human, be the Supreme Brahman?”

To clear his sons’ doubts, Brahma established Krsna’s divinity in many verses, concluding with

*om sri krsnaya govindaya gopijana-vallabhaya*  
*om tat sad bhur bhuvah svas tasmai vai namo namah*

**“I offer my obeisances unto the lotus feet of Sri Krsna, the form of îmkṁrṁ. He is eternal, full of bliss and knowledge, and He maintains everything—especially the cows, the earth, and the Vedas.**

**“As the master of unlimited and inconceivable energies, Krsna induces Maya to create, and He is the object of worship by the inhabitants of the three planetary systems, bhuh, bhuvah, and svah.**

Revealed scriptures and realised souls unanimously proclaim Krsna to be the Absolute Truth, the source of innumerable incarnations, inconceivable potencies, the Supersoul, and impersonal Brahman.

Amongst many other scriptural statements, this one attests to Krsna’s supreme divinity,

*aho bhagyam aho bhagyam nanda-gopa-vrajaukasam*  
*yan-mitram paramanandam purnam brahma sanatanam*

**“How greatly fortunate arc Nanda, the cowherd men and other Vraja- vasis, because the Absolute Truth, the source of supreme bliss, the eternal Supreme Brahman, has become their friend.”**

And the Visnu Purdna says, *yatravatirnam krsnakhyam param brahma narakrti*: “The Supreme Absolute Truth is a person, His name is Krsna, and He sometimes descends to this earth.”

A well-known statement of the Brahmanda Purdna is *param brahma narakrti*: “The Supreme Brahman has appeared in human form.” And in Padma Purdna we also find, *narakrti param brahma*.

People often ask, “If Krsna is God, then who created Krsna?” Although this question is patently illogical, Brahma still patiently answers it, saying, *atma-buddi-prakasam*: “Krsna is self-manifest!”

Brahma describes Krsna as one who keeps devotees free from trouble (aklista-karin) and who forgives the offences even of demons (papa-karsana), but the Lord’s glory is much more than that.

True, like Narayana, Krsna frees demons from sinful reactions by mercifully killing them. But while death at the hands of Narayana merely sends demons to heaven, Krsna gives them eternal service in Goloka.

Take Putana for example. She smeared poison on her breast intending to kill Krsna, but because she had assumed the role of a nurse, after killing her, Krsna granted her the position of mother.

What’s more, Krsna displays four qualities lacking in Narayana: His amazing pastimes, His circle of associates filled with spontaneous love, His all-attractive flute playing, and His sweet, unparalleled beauty.

About His unique and exquisite pastimes, Krsna Himself says, “Though all My pastimes are very attractive and profound, when I remember the rasa-lila I cannot explain what happens to My mind.”

The Vraja-vasis have unmatched love for Krsna, which they display in uncountable ways. During Krsna’s absence in the forest, for instance, they feel the passing of a second to be like a millennium.

The gopis showed that same unrivalled love while rasa dancing with Krsna. Yogamaya stretched each night into a night of Brahma, and yet the blissful gopis felt the nights slip by in hardly a moment.

Regarding Krsna’s extraordinary flute playing, the gopis once said, “O pious Mother Yasoda! Your son, who is expert in all the arts of herding cows, has invented many new styles of flute playing.

“When He takes His flute to His bimba-red lips and sends forth the tones of the harmonic scale in variegated melodies, Brahma, Siva, Indra, and other demigods become confused upon hearing the sound.

“Although they are the most learned authorities, they cannot ascertain the essence of that music, and thus, these leaders of heavenly hosts bow down their heads and hearts.”

Finally, because Krsna’s sublime form is the very summit of beauty, it inspires wonder even in the Lord of Vaikuntha. Indeed, it is said to be the ornament of all Krsna’s ornaments (*bhusana-bhusanangam*).

These sublime awe-inspiring features make the Supreme Personality of Godhead Krsna attractive to everyone — including Cupid, and even Krsna Himself. Thus “Krsna” means “the all-attractive one.”

Once, in response to the gopis' songs of separation, Krsna came before His beloveds. Impeccably dressed, garlanded, and smiling, He appeared as one who can bewilder the mind of Cupid himself.

To be sure, Cupid bewilders everyone, but Krsna’s all-attractive potency extends itself to His ornaments, His garland, His clothing, and so on, and bewilders even Cupid (manmatha-manmathah).

Krsna’s ornaments on their own are special, but by coming in contact with Krsna’s bodily qualities — such as its fragrance — they take on His all-enchanting power and become as extraordinary as He is.

Amorous mellow personified, Krsna is attractive to all devotees, regardless of their mood of devotion, to all incarnations, Narayana included, and to all women, even the goddess of fortune herself.

If an envious snake like Kaliya could appreciate Krsna’s bodily allure, as he did when Krsna jumped into the Yamuna, is it any wonder that the innocent, the pious, and the devoted swoon at Krsna’s sweetness?

Many times in New Vraja-dhama I have seen visitors enter the temple sceptical and leave disarmed by Radha-Syamasundara’s charm and sweetness. Some even say out loud, “They’re so beautiful!”

My own encounters with Syamasundara’s beauty have left me totally enamoured by His presence, His service, His abode, His mission, and everything else related to Him — except distance from Him.

Sanjaya once explained the name Krsna in his effort to convince Dhrtarastra and his sons, headed by the wicked Duryodhana, that with Krsna on Arjuna’s chariot the Kurus could not win the war.

After discussing the names Vasudeva, Madhava, and Madhusudana, Sanjaya said to his king,

*krsir bhu-vacakah sabdo nas ca nirvrti-vacakah*  
*tayor aikyam param brahma krsna ity abhidhiyate*

**“The word krs is the attractive feature of the Lord’s existence, and na means ‘spiritual pleasure.’ When the verb krs is added to na it becomes krsna, which indicates the Absolute Truth.”**

The translation “all-attractive bliss” mentioned earlier is the foremost meaning of krsna. Because Krsna’s attractiveness bewilders (mohana) even Cupid (known as Madana), Krsna is called Madana-mohana.

In summary, the word krsndya represents Krsna’s all-attractive form and is the first of the mantra’s six elements, the element that must be known in order to establish a relationship with Gopala.

The people of Kali-yuga, who are materially absorbed and thus spiritually feeble, can take to the elevated path of pure devotional service and advance quickly by surrendering to Madana-mohana.

Madana-mohana’s enchanting mien draws surrendering souls away from sense gratification, nullifies their attachment to long-standing bad habits, and in this way unclasps the fetters of Cupid.

The initiating spiritual masters — of whom Sanatana Gosvami is a paragon — are personal manifestations of Madana-mohana; they transmit the Lord’s beauty through transcendental knowledge.

The first phase of transcendental knowledge is called sambandha-jnana, understanding of the relationship, in which Krsna is known as the Absolute Truth and the living entities as His eternal servants.

Such knowledge inspires detachment from fruitive activities in favour of enthusiastic devotional practice, which gives us the spiritual strength to advance further in service and surrender.

Caitanya Mahaprabhu outlined the science of pure devotion in three stages, beginning with sambandha-jnana, and so in Vrndavana His followers installed three Deities to preside over these three stages.

Sanatana Gosvami installed Madana-mohana on top of Dvadasaditya Hill, Rupa Gosvami installed Govindaji at the Yoga-pitha, and Madhu Pandita installed Gopinatha near Vamsivata.

Known as the sambandha-vigraha, Madana-mohana is worshipped with this prayer,

*jayatam suratau pangor mama manda-mater gati*  
*mat-sarvasva-padambhojau radha-madana-mohanau*

**“Glory to the all-merciful Radha and Madana-mohana! I am lame and ill advised, yet They are my directors, and Their lotus feet are everything to me.”**

In this way Madana-mohana is our guide.

The spiritual master connects his disciples to Madana-mohana by initiating them with the Gopala mantra, and in so doing offers absolute shelter from the torments of material existence.

In this way, after wandering aimlessly through material creation for countless millennia, by the grace of the spiritual master and Lord Krsna we are again on the path back home, back to Godhead.

*Om gopalaya nija-svarupaya om tat sad bhur bhuvah svas tasmai vai namo namah*

**“My obeisances to Krsna. Gopala is His personal form of eternal knowledge and bliss. The three worlds are His opulence.”**

**\*\*\*\***

This next section explains the word *govindaya* in the Gopala mantra by elucidating the name Govinda, the significance of the stage of abhidheya, and the glories of the Govindaji Deity.

I offer my respectful obeisances to GovindajI, the treasure of Rupa Gosvami’s heart, who mercifully appeared in Deity form within Govinda-sthali Hill but who now resides in the city of Jaipur.

I offer my respectful obeisances to the Govindaji of New Vraja' dhama. Served by gopas and gopis, surrounded by cows, He stands beside Radha at the heart of His playground, and He is called Syama.

When Sanaka and his brothers asked Brahma whom death personified fears, their father answered, *govindan mrtyur bibheti*: “Death fears Govinda! By worshipping Him one becomes immortal.”

“But why would death be afraid of a cowherd boy?” wondered the sages. Brahma explained that Govinda is no ordinary cowherd. He is Krsna, whom Brahma had already proved to be the Absolute Truth.

Brahma said, *tam ekam govindam sac-cid-ananda-vigrahum*: “Lord Govinda is one without second, transcendental to the modes of nature, and possessed of a form of eternity, knowledge, and bliss.”

The creator went on to explain that by killing demons like Putana, Kaliya, and Kamsa, Govinda proves His unlimited power, which causes death to tremble in fear at the mere mention of His name.

Srimad-Bhagavatam agrees with Brahma. It narrates an incident in which Yamaraja, god of death, instructs his servants, “Those who chant Krsna’s holy names are not under my jurisdiction.

“My dear servants! Do not even approach the devotees of the Lord, who are always protected by His invincible club, for neither Brahma, nor I, nor even the time factor are competent to punish them.”

In defining the name Govinda, Brahma says, *go-bhumi-veda-vidito-vidita*: “Govinda means the Lord of the cows of Vraja, the Lord of the earth; in fact, He is the Lord of everything described in the Vedas."

After Krsna held Govardhana Hill aloft for seven days, the heavenly cow Surabhi prayed that He be made king (indra) or protector (vinda) of the cows (go) and thus always be known as Govinda.

Krsna is indeed known as the master of the cows, but the name Govinda has a more specific import: He who is especially fond of tending His father’s transcendental cows in the pastures of Vraja.

Just as Krsna is equally affectionate to all living entities and yet still feels especially inclined towards His devotees, Govinda loves all cows yet still feels a special affection for the cows of Vrndavana.

Srila Prabhupada generally gives three meanings for the word go: the cows, the senses, and the land or earth. In this way he defines Govinda as He who gives pleasure to these three things.

The higher pleasure we enjoy from serving Govinda and chanting His names enables us to refrain from the base pleasures that come from sensual gratification, the very cause of our bondage in this world.

At the outset of His teachings in Bhagavad-gita, Krsna speaks of this essential impetus for practising devotional service — the ability to control the senses. In the verse beginning *visaya vinwartante*, He says:

**“The embodied soul may be restricted from sense enjoyment, though the taste for sense objects remains. But, ceasing such engagements by experiencing a higher taste, he is fixed in consciousness.”**

And if Krsna blesses even neophytes with such nectar, we can only imagine what sort of transcendental pleasure is relished by liberated devotees or, above them, Govinda’s eternal companions.

Sri Caitanya has made it easy for one and all to drink deeply from the reservoir of bliss by chanting Krsna’s names. Bhaktivinoda Thakura therefore pleads, “Chant, ‘Govinda! Govinda! Radha-Govinda!’”

Draupadi, simply by desperately calling out, “O Govinda!” when she faced humiliation at the hands of Duhsasana, was personally delivered by Krsna as the Kuru court looked on in amazement.

And even though Draupadl was fully satisfied by her salvation, Krsna still remarked that, by calling out His name, “She has put Me in her debt, and that indebtedness is gradually increasing in My heart!”

When the demigods begged Lord Siva to drink the kalakuta poison generated from churning the ocean of milk, he first chanted om acyutanandaya govindaya namah to neutralise the toxin.

These examples reveal the name Govinda to be a cintamani that liberates us from all kinds of danger and also makes the owner of the name, Sri Govinda, the property of those who chant it.

Govinda is the presiding Deity of spiritual cultivation (abhidheya), or in Srila Prabhupada’s words, “The Govinda Deity is called the functional Deity because He shows us how to serve Radha-Krsna.”

Madana-mohana teaches the foundational principles upon which we can re-establish our eternal relationship with Krsna, and Govindaji teaches us how to apply that knowledge in our daily lives.

As the original preceptor, Govindaji teaches us how to serve Him and then gives us the privilege to do so. At times, as the exemplar, He even serves His own devotee, just as He became Arjuna’s charioteer.

The Bhagavad-gita is the essence of Govinda’s teachings, and the essence of the Gita is to absorb oneself in thoughts of Govinda, service to Govinda, worship of Govinda, and homage to Govinda.

To follow these instructions of Govindaji means to intently practise the nine limbs of devotional service, beginning with hearing and chanting about Him, *sravanam kirtanam visnoh*.

Such regulated practice gradually intensifies reverential worship of the Supreme Lord Krsna, and elevates the practitioner, by adding love and devotion for the carefree cowherd boy of Vraja, Govinda.

In other words, serving Govindaji gradually fixes regulated devotees on the path of spontaneous devotion, which leads to the loving blessings of Gopijana-vallabha and life’s ultimate perfection.

Govindaji’s merciful teachings act as the catalyst by which devotional service in practice gradually matures into ecstatic devotion, and finally into loving devotion like that of the Vraja-vasis.

Krsna outlines how following His teachings turns practising devotees into lovers of God, *bahavo jnana-tapasa puta mad-bhavam agatah*:

**“Being freed from attachment, fear, and anger, being fully absorbed in Me, and taking refuge in Me, many, many persons in the past became purified by knowledge of Me, and thus they all attained transcendental love for Me.”**

Thus Sri Govinda is the original instructing spiritual master (siksa-guru), and devotees who repeat Govindadeva’s message act as siksa-gurus, personal manifestations of the Govindaji Deity.

The original Deity of Govindaji was fashioned by Vajra under the supervision of Uttara, mother of Maharaja Pariksit. Further history of the Deity, up until relatively recent times, appears lost to antiquity.

Rupa Gosvami discovered Govinda’s Deity form within the hill of Govinda-sthali and inaugurated worship of the Deity in Vrndavana. GovindajI was later moved to Jaipur where He is still served today.

In fact Govinda is the presiding Deity of all Gaudiya Vaisnavas because He is the source of inspiration for practical service, which is the essential feature of devotion, in practice or perfection.

Similarly, Rupa Gosvami is the siksa-guru of Caitanya Mahaprabhu’s followers. We Gaudlyas are always under the loving protection of these two transcendental persons — Srila Govindaji and Sri Rupa.

Sometimes Sri Govinda gives valuable personal instruction to His devotees through their dreams, and sometimes He gives a devotee detailed instructions that are also meant to benefit others.

In a dream the great devotee-scholar Baladeva received from Srila Govindadeva an honorific title, Vidyabhrisana, and a commentary to Vedanta-sutra, which Baladeva later named Govinda-bhasya.

And Caitanya Mahaprabhu—Govinda Himself—personally taught Rupa Gosvami and Sanatana Gosvami the spiritual principles that those great saints later revealed to the world through their books.

About Sanatana, Kaviraja Gosvami writes,

**“Srila Sanatana Gosvami also compiled many other books—The basic principle of all these books is to show us how to love Madana-mohana and Govindaji.”**

Srila Prabhupada would often quote the following verse to show the importance of studying the Vrndavana Gosvami’s books,

*mpa-raghunatha-pade hoibe akuti*  
*kabe hama bujhabo se yugala-piriti*

**“When will I be eager to study the teachings of the Gosvamis from Rupa Gosvami to Raghunatha Dasa Gosvami? Then I may be able to understand the pastimes or loving affairs of Radha and Krsna.”**

We must always bear in mind that success in cultivating our spiritual lives is directly proportional to the degree of our faith in Srila Govindadeva and in the process of pure devotional service.

What’s more, the intensity of our faith increases in proportion to the degree of our piety, meaning our willingness to take shelter of the association of Vaisnavas, especially advanced Vaisnavas.

But even in good company, if we still have difficulty fixing our minds on Krsna while chanting the Gopala mantra, we should not despair. With perseverance we should remain confident.

With a determined vow to attain the Lord’s lotus feet, we should intently chant the names Krsna and Govinda with firm faith that by this practice attachment to the Lord will arise naturally.

Revealed scriptures advise us to chant the Hare Krsna mantra day and night, free from shame or fear, and gradually, by spiritual strength, to adopt frugal eating habits and to conquer over sleep.

Practising genuine detachment from mundane affairs, we should peacefully tread the path of spiritual truth, keeping faith in Krsna’s promise, na me bhaktah pranasyati: “My devotee never perishes!”

**\*\*\*\***

Gaudiya Vaisnavas chant the Gopala mantra and perform devotional service not to achieve something, but in order to cultivate separation from the transcendental beneficiary of their service, Sri Govinda.

Caitanya Mahaprabhu promoted this mood of separation (vipralambha-bhava), which transforms into service in separation (vipralambha-seva). The Lord speaks of both in His Sri Siksastaka:

**“O Govinda! Feeling Your separation, I consider a moment to be like twelve years or more. Tears are flowing from my eyes like torrents of rain, and I feel all vacant in the world in Your absence.”**

These sentiments voiced by Lord Caitanya cannot be imitated. But they can be cultivated by chanting Krsna’s names, as a result of which vipralambha-bhava evolves spontaneously in stages.

In the first stage we are convinced that we are Krsna’s servants and that material existence is the cause of our own endless and needless suffering. With this conviction we attain genuine humility and hope.

Earnestly giving up the pursuit of material happiness, which caused us to live separate from the Lord, we feel a natural anxiety to advance spiritually, which transforms into intense eagerness to find Govinda.

Srila Prabhupada’s favourite bhajana, Hari hari bifale, inspires a meditation favourable for cultivating the mood of separation in neophyte and advanced devotees alike. The first verse begins:
