**“O Lord Hari! Lord Hari! I have spent my life uselessly. Although I have obtained a human birth, I have refused to worship Radha and Krsna, and in this way I have knowingly drunk poison.”**

Spiritual realisation leads to heartfelt anxiety, which brings out in us a genuine outpouring of emotion in the form of a torrent of tears that is comparable only to a monsoon downpour.

Thereafter feelings of prolonged separation from Govinda produce a host of bittersweet ecstasies, and during the foremost of those ecstasies we suffer the passing of each moment as if it were an age.

Thus afflicted, in vain we constantly search the horizon for Lord Govinda, and in His absence we see the entire world transformed into a desert, and life itself appears to be an empty shell.

In such an anxious state, death seems close at hand. In a final act of desperation, acutely suffering, we seek out the residents of Vraja-dhama— surely they must know how and where to find Govinda.

Gripped by delirium, as desperate servants of Govinda we call out as if speaking to Maharaja Nanda or Subala, “I can no longer tolerate separation from your Krsna. If you do not show Him to me I will die.”

In that state we continue calling our Lords names as if we are gripped by madness. Then suddenly and unexpectedly, but heralded by a host of ecstasies, we see Syamasundara standing before us.

Indeed, the son of Maharaja Nanda appears with His companions and abode, standing in the shade of a kadamba tree by Yamuna’s shore, exchanging glances with Sri Radha while playing on His flute.

To behold our cherished goal after enduring so much suffering causes our bliss to soar to unprecedented heights, making the mind so spiritually unsteady that we swoon at Govinda’s feet.

And then, after an unknown length of time, we return to consciousness, but Radha, Govinda, and Vrndavana have disappeared. Once again the coils of vipralambha tighten their grip.

Once again the angst of separation inspires ecstasies that may resemble pain but are in fact the highest bliss. Once again we become mad in separation until, once again, Govinda appears before us.

Such is the mood of service taught personally by Govinda, a mood most effective in cultivating attachment to the Lord, a mood that makes meditating upon Him easy, honest, and authorised.

The thoughtful devotee may justifiably enquire why the impetus for cultivating pure attachment to Govinda should be a feeling of separation from the Lord, rather than a desire for union with Him.

Krsna has answered this question many times. On one occasion, He sent a message through Uddhava to the gopis. In order to console them after He had left Vrndavana for Mathura, Krsna said:

“But the actual reason why I, the beloved object of your sight, have stayed far away from you is that I wanted to intensify your meditation upon Me and thus draw your minds closer to Me.”

Lord Krsna then explained with the following example how separation deepens meditation: “When her lover is far away, a woman thinks of him more than when he is present before her.”

He concluded His message by reassuring the gopis that they would soon be reunited with Him, a message that should also reassure us that our own struggles in separation will one day bear fruit.

In concluding, Krsna mentioned the gopis who were restrained from going to the rasa dance. “They were nonetheless fortunate,” He said. “Indeed, they attained Me by thinking of My potent pastimes.”

Just as Govinda’s words acted as fuel to intensify the gopis’ love for Him, we should all likewise accept the Lord’s words as inspiration to cultivate our attachment to Him through meditating on Him.

Kaviraja Gosvami gives one meditation on Govinda,

*divyad-vrudaranya-kalpa-drumadhah-srimad-ratnagara-simhasana-sthau*  
*srimad-radha-srila-govinda-devau presthalibhih sevyamanau smarami*

**“In a temple of jewels in Vrndavana, underneath a desire tree, Sri Sri Radha-Govinda, served by Their most confidential associates, sit upon an effulgent throne. I offer my humble obeisances to Them.”**

This verse is a mantropdsana-mayi meditation — like the venum kvanantam verse of Brahma-samhita— which we may concentrate upon while chanting either Hare Krsna or the Gopala mantra.

The image in this verse concentrates one’s mind on the Yoga-pitha— the personal residence of Sri Radha and Govinda and the place from which the lotus of Goloka’s unlimited energies unfold.

In his own prayer Brahma discloses the secrets of Govinda’s residence, which is manifest through the agency of Baladeva’s potency and is the receptacle of Goloka’s thousand-ðṛtailed lotus.

The central platform (karnika), upon which stands Radha and Govinda’s golden throne, is a hexagonal yantra with the kama-bija at its centre, composed of the loving mellows of Their pastimes.

Along the various sides and petals of this yantra are several hymns in succession, beginning with the Krsna mantra, Gopala mantra, and then the twenty-four and forty-eight syllable Kama-gayatri mantras.

Surrounding Radha and Govinda are Their eight most intimate girlfriends, whose confidential maidservants surround them upon the petals of the lotus. This is meditation on the Yoga-pitha.

We can fix this picture of the Yoga-pitha in the mind by memorisation, or we can remember the forms of Radha and Govinda, realising the details of the Yoga-pitha by revelation.

In either case, meditation on the Yoga-pitha is a powerful tool with which to conquer lust and connect to the divine couple. Vaisnava literatures, such as Govinda-lilamrta, describe this in more detail.

That book, in the way of svarasiki-mayi, poetically narrates Govinda’s daily pastimes, pastimes recounted just to nourish those devotees who serve Radha and Govinda on the path of spontaneous devotion.

Govindaji bestows this path of sacred greed, called raganuga, upon only those devotees who have won the mercy of Madana-mohana by carefully observing the process of regulated devotional service.

And for devotees who have developed interest in the devotional service of the Vraja-vasis by hearing from authorised sources, Govindaji appears as siksa-guru to stoke the fire of their eagerness.

The meditation on Govinda most familiar to iskcon devotees is the Brahma-samhita, in which we hear of the Lord’s abode, His varied energies and expansions, His personal features, and His pastimes.

Srila Prabhupada, wanting his followers to remember Govinda, established that two verses of the Brahma-samhita be chanted every morning at the greeting of I temple Deities.

The following is iskcon’s Govinda-bhajana, *Govindam adi-purusam tarn aham bhajami*:

**“I worship Govinda, the primeval Lord, who is adept in playing on His flute, with blooming eyes like lotus petals —**

**“With head decked with peacock’s feather, with the figure of beauty tinged with the hue of blue clouds, and His unique loveliness charming millions of Cupids.”**

*Govindam adi-purusam tam aham bhajami*:

**“I worship Govinda, the primeval Lord, whose transcendental form is full of bliss, truth, substantiality and is thus full of the most dazzling splendour —**

**“Each of the limbs of that transcendental figure possesses... the full-fledged functions of all the organs, and eternally sees, maintains, and manifests the infinite universes, both spiritual and mundane.’**

Hearing and singing these songs for the last four decades has aroused my own eagerness to taste the sweetness of Govinda’s pastimes, an eagerness that with time is becoming more and more acute.

I worship the Govinda of New Vraja-dhama, Sri Syamasundara. The luminescent rays of His magnanimous smile give soothing shelter to His servants and dapple His graceful, bending form.

Syamasundara always lingers in my thoughts. By transforming this patch of Hungary into Vrndavana, He gives great pleasure to the earth and offers a wealth of service to His devotees.

Though Kali constantly tempts the minds and senses of the people in this age, Krsna’s devotees are protected from such allurements by their full engagement in the service of Syamasundara and His abode.

By their occupational duty the residents of New Vraja-dhama care for cows and work with oxen. As cowherd men and women we are proud to be led by the original gopi and gopa, Radha and Syama.

By living as Krsna did on earth, and as He does eternally in Goloka, we show our guests and the Hungarian people that a happy life is self-sufficient and God-centred, not artificial and consumer-based.

When not directly working for our livelihood, we assemble in the temple room and chant the holy names, thus taking further lessons from Syamasundara on how to love Him. This chanting is our life.

We’re convinced that Syamasundara, in response to our surrender to Him, has taken full charge of our lives. He guides us from within our hearts, through His devotees, and by the events of everyday life.

To ensure that Radha and Syama stay in the centre of our lives, we at New Vraja-dhama abandon all sense of proprietorship and live with the conviction that “everything belongs to Syamasundara.”

Day and night I meditate on how to improve Radha-Syamasundara’s personal service, beautify Their playground, care for Their devotees, and draw more people to the beauty and sweetness of Their lotus feet.

The sight of Their limitless elegance my eyes’ only satisfaction, the sound of Their sublime names my tongue’s only desire, my soul longs for Their direct service. Syama and His Radha arc the life of my life.

I worship Govinda, the guru of the three worlds. I am His pupil in the school of service in separation, where I study the art of loving Radha and Syama. One day I hope to graduate to Their eternal service.

I worship the lover of Srimati Radharani, who is rich in the mellow of amorous pastimes, who initiated Brahma with His flute-song, and who considers His companions His own indispensable wealth.

**“I worship Govinda, who is Syamasundara, Krsna Himself with numberless inconceivable attributes, whom the pure devotees sec in their hearts with the eye of devotion tinged with the salve of love.”**

**\*\*\*\***

*Sriman rasa-rasarambhi vamsivata-tata-sthitah*  
*êṁrsṁñ venu-svanair gopir gopi-nathah sriye ’stu nah*

**“Sri Srila Gopinatha, who originated the transcendental mellow of the rasa dance —**

**“Stands on the shore in Vamsivata and attracts the attention of the cowherd damsels with the sound of His celebrated flute. May they all confer upon us their benediction.”**

I offer my respectful obeisances to Gopinatha, the emperor of the rasa dance, who was worshipped by Madhu Pandita at Vamsivata until — along with Govindaji and other Deities — He left for Jaipur.

May a most beguiling gopi’s Lord called Syamasundara, who is always absorbed in playing His flute, remain forever carefree and happy as the honoured cowherd hero of New Vraja-dhama.

I worship the sweetheart of New Vraja-dhama, Sri Radha, who serves Syama’s lotus feet with crooked glances and gentle smiles. May She and Her gallant reveal pastimes within the Vraja of my heart.

Srila Prabhupada’s translation of gopijana-vallabhaya is “unto the lover of the gopis.” These two words, which form the goal (prayojana) portion of the mantra, can be explained separately or together.

Following the example of the Gopala-tapani Upanisad, the most authoritative and celebrated Vedic commentary on the Gopala mantra, I will explain the words gopijana-vallabhaya together.

After revealing two of Gopala’s features — Krsna, the Supreme Lord, and Govinda, the reservoir of pleasure — Brahma describes the Godhead’s ultimate feature, Gopljana-vallabha, the lover of the gopis.

The sages headed by Sanaka had asked Lord Brahma, “Who is that person by knowing whom nothing remains to be known?” The creator replied, *gopijanavallabha-jnanena taj jnatam bhavati:*

**“One who knows the characteristics of Gopljana-vallabha has come to the end of knowledge. For such a person nothing more remains to be known. Nothing more remains to be known.”**

Earlier in the creation, Krsna had explained to Brahma the essence of Srimad-Bhagavatam. At that time, with His typical full wisdom, Krsna encouraged Brahma to pursue knowledge to its ultimate goal:

**“One who is searching for the Supreme Absolute Truth, the Personality of Godhead, must certainly search for it up to this, in all circumstances, in all space and time, both directly and indirectly.”**

Brahma reassured his sons that Gopijana-vallabha was the same Absolute Truth referred to earlier as Krsna and Govinda, master of both the internal potency (yogamaya) and her shadow (mahamaya).

The role the Lord plays as the source of the material energy, however, is secondary to the ultimate part He plays as Gopijana-vallabha. His exchanges with His internal pleasure potency are the most sublime.

Gopala’s amorous pastimes in the company of the beautiful cowherd girls of Vrndavana — the embodiments of loving devotion to Him — constitute the summit of all things knowable.

To teach this truth, Sri Caitanya Mahaprabhu once asked the great devotee Raghupati Upadhyaya four questions: “Who is the foremost being? And which of Krsna’s abodes, ages, and mellows is best?”

Raghupati Upadhyaya gave the following answers,

*syamam eva parṁḍ ruðṁḍ puri madhu-puri vara*  
*vayah kaisorakam dhyeyam adya eva paro rasah*

**“The form of Syamasundara is the supreme form.**

**“The city of Mathura \[Vrndavana\] is the supreme abode. Lord Krsna’s fresh youth \[kaisora\] should always be meditated upon, and the mellow of conjugal love \[adi-rasa\] is the supreme mellow.”**

Yet even after hearing this conclusion from Brahma, the sages remained doubtful. They thought, “Which gopi is Gopala’s beloved? And if Gopijana-vallabha is the end of knowledge, why say svaha?

To clear his sons’ doubts, Lord Brahma then said, *gopijanavidya kala- prerakas*: “Gopijana-vallabha is He who inspires His loving devotees to participate in His transcendental pastimes.”

But if Krsna, Govinda, and Gopijana-vallabha are all but features of the same Supreme Person, how could one be a more complete and unrivalled expression of Godhead than the others?

It is not that Gopijana-vallabha possesses potencies or attributes that are absent in Krsna or Govinda; rather, Gopijana-vallabha displays spiritual assets in full that the others display only in part.

Spiritual energies like hladini and samvit certainly exist in Govinda or Krsna, but those potencies do not display themselves as fully as they do when they are serving Gopijana-vallabha in their personal forms.

The following six stanzas give examples in which Srimad-Bhagavatam confirms the supremacy of Gopinatha, the Lord of the gopis, even though the verses address Him more generally as Krsna or Govinda.

In describing the rasa dance — wherein the sounds of the gopis ’ bangles, anklets, and waist bells create a symphonic tumult — Srimad- Bhagavatam calls Krsna atisusubha, or “most brilliant.”

Thus Sri Suka actually describes Gopijana-vallabha when he says,

**“In the midst of the dancing gopis, Lord Krsna appeared most brilliant, like an exquisite sapphire in the midst of golden ornaments.”**

Later in the text, Sukadeva tells how Krsna sent Uddhava to Vrndavana to examine the spontaneous love of the Vraja-vasis and to study the ways in which vraja-bhakti manifests itself in them.

As a great devotee and scholar, Uddhava’s months in Vraja opened his eyes to new realms of devotion. At long last, as Uddhava was leaving for Mathura, he made a startling observation.

Uddhava said,

**“Amongst all persons on earth, these cowherd women alone have actually perfected their embodied lives, for they have achieved the perfection of unalloyed love for Lord Govinda.”**

In light of Lord Brahma’s assertion — that if one knows Gopijana- vallabha, nothing else remains to be known—Uddhava’s glorification of the gopis can be more clearly understood in this way: “All people, including great sages and even myself, possess imperfect knowledge. Only the gopis’ knowledge, with which they serve Krsna as their lover — Gopijana-vallabha—-is perfect and complete.”

This conclusion forms a cornerstone of Caitanya Mahaprabhu’s teachings, *ramya kacid upasana vraja-vadhu-vargena ya kalpita*: “The worship most pleasing to Krsna is that of the Vraja gopis.”

Given all this evidence, we should understand the relationship between Gopijana-vallabha and the gopis, spoken of in the Gautamiya-tantra like this: “He \[Krsna\] is the master of the gopis.”

Let us now return to the discussion between Brahma and the sages headed by Sanaka. The sages were curious to know if there was any one gopi unique amongst others and especially dear to Gopinatha.

In a later verse, Brahma does mention one gopi, Rama, as the centre of Lord Krsna’s attention,

*barhapidabhiramaya ramayakuntha-medhase*  
*rama-manasca-hamsaya govindaya namo namah*

**“O Govinda, beautifully adorned with a peacock feather, whose plenary portion is Lord Rama and who is a swan that swims freely in the lake-like mind of Rama, I offer repeated obeisances unto You.”**

And from the second part of the Gopala-tapani Upanisad, it is clear that Krsna’s favourite gopi is the leader of the gopis, called Gandharvi. Commentators identify Her to be Sri Radha. Thus Rama is Radha.

Later in the text, during Durvasa Muni’s conversation with Srimati Radharani, he also tells Her, *sa vo hi svami bhavati*: “The Supreme Lord, Krsna, is Your beloved Lord and husband.”

More explicitly, this Vedic language means, “Gopijana-vallabha is Krsna, the beloved of Srimati Radharani, who constantly engages Radha and Her gopi associates in pastimes of amorous love.”

Backed by these substantial references, Brahma’s earlier answer (*gopijanavidyṁ kala-prerakas*) can now be taken to mean, “Gopijana-vallabha is He who engages the embodiments of prema-bhakti”

Of course this analysis cannot provide a complete understanding of unlimited Gopijana-vallabha, but it can increase our gratitude to Him and inspire greater attention to His presence in the Gopala mantra.

Only at the stage of perfection can we hope to be subject to the spells Krsna artfully weaves with the mystical sound of His flute, spells that hypnotise the gopis and entice them into flower-filled groves.

Such lofty aspirations may be deemed inappropriate for conditioned souls, but sincere and serious devotees should never lose hope in Krsna’s all-merciful nature or the miracles He performs.

We may lament our apparent lack of love for Krsna, or our disqualifications for pure devotion, or our past sinful activities; in fact, we may even feel we are without any good qualities at all.

Nevertheless, it is well known that Gopijana-vallabha bestows His mercy on even the most fallen, so we can maintain hope that we may yet become recipients of Krsna’s mercy, even if such hope causes pain.

Caitanya Mahaprabhu encourages all of us, even in the conditioned stage, to cultivate our identities as servants of the servants of Gopijana-vallabha’s transcendental entourage of cowherd damsels.

He taught us to practise Krsna consciousness and at the same time to avoid identifying with our varna or dsrama, but rather be eternal servants, *gopi-bhartuh pada-kamalayor dasa-dasanudasah*.

Caitanya Mahaprabhu portrayed Gopijana-vallabha as “Lord Sri Krsna, the maintainer of the gopis, an ocean of nectar, and the cause of universal transcendental bliss who exists always with brilliance.”

These words from the Lord and our acaryas serve as beacons to guide those of us riding the sturdy boat of chanting the Hare Krsna and Gopala mantra over the turbulent ocean of Kali-yuga.

Srila Prabhupada urges his followers to become especially dear to the Lord by aspiring to become gopijana themselves. His Divine Grace says,

**“Thus Krsna also became known as Gopijana-vallabha —**

**“Which indicates that His only business is to protect the gopijana. This Krsna consciousness movement aims at teaching people how to become gopijanas, or pure lovers of Krsna.**

**“When we reach that stage of pure love of God, the Lord will save us from any danger, even if it means His lifting a hill or a mountain.”**

Who could fail to be inspired by these words of His Divine Grace!

In summary, we must cultivate humility and serve the servants of Lord Caitanya’s mission. And we must make every effort to learn the science of becoming one of Krsna’s gopijanas by serving Him purely.

Worship progresses in successive stages of purity and matures at the stage of spontaneous love. We are then qualified to directly serve the gopis beloved. Until then, we should hear about Him with faith.

At the end of his reply, Brahma disclosed to his sons a prayer with which he personally worships the Lord,

*ballavi nayandmbhoja-maline nrtya-saline*  
*namah pranata-palaya sri krsnaya namo namah*

**“O dearly beloved of the gopis! The cowherd damsels of Vraja offer You their beautiful lotus faces, eager to receive Your affectionate kisses, while in turn You greatly relish their ecstatic love.**

**“You wear a garland of forest flowers, but Your best ornament is the lotus garland of the gopis’ blue lotus-eyes reflecting upon Your body as they gaze at Your handsome, beguiling beauty.**

**“O maintainer of surrendered souls! O Krsna, whose raii-cloud-hued body is the condensed form of sac-cid-ananda, I offer my respectful obeisances to Your lotus feet again and yet again.”**

In commenting on Krsna’s childhood pranks, Srila Prabhupada gives a meaning of Gopijana-vallabha that also encompasses the elder gopis of Vrndavana like Mother Yasoda and her peers.

Child Krsna would often go with His friends to a neighbour’s home and steal butter or yogurt. If He were caught in the act, Krsna would sometimes become angry and accuse His captor of being a thief.

In His anger Krsna would sometimes pass stool and urine inside the house, and when the elder women of the village brought this mischief to Yasoda’s notice, Krsna would listen quietly and feign innocence.

If Krsna thought that His mother was being persuaded by the gopis’ complaints, He would sit silently, looking so fearful that Mother Yasoda would at once abandon any thought of scolding Him.

And when the other ladies looked at Krsna’s beautiful face, their hearts became so flooded with transcendental bliss that they forgot their complaints and gazed at Krsna instead. Srila Prabhupada says:

**“Because of the features of Krsna’s face, the mothers were so attracted that they could not chastise Him. Instead of chastising Him, they smiled and enjoyed hearing of Krsna’s activities.**

**“Thus the gopis remained satisfied, and Krsna enjoyed their happiness. Therefore another name of Krsna is Gopijana-vallabha because He invented such activities to please the gopis.**

**\*\*\*\***

The Gopala mantra concludes with svaha, which is taken as one word in the Gopala-tapani Upanisad, but two words (sva and ha) in the Brahma-samhita. I will treat it as one word with two syllables.

The fourth and final question the sages put to Brahma was, kenedam visvam samsaratiti: “By whose inspiration does this creation move?” The creator succinctly replied, “By the inspiration of svaha.”

This indirect answer confused the sages. By saying svaha, Brahma meant to point out that material nature — which throws the living entities into illusion — was the immediate cause of creation.

But by mentioning svaha in the same breath as Gopljana-vallabha, Lord Brahma also implied that Krsna, who remains invisible to conditioned souls, is the remote cause of creation.

Two points confused the Kumaras. First, if Gopijana-vallabha, the gopis’ lover, is the last word in knowledge, why did Brahma say that svaha is the cause of material existence and not Gopijana-vallabha?

Second, if they took svaha to mean “an offering of surrender,” and if, as Brahma had said, knowledge of Gopijana-vallabha grants liberation, then why would he recommend another means of worship?

To better understand their father’s reply and to eliminate their own uncertainty, the brahmanas requested further clarification by asking, kah ka svaheti, “What is the meaning of the word svaha?”

Lord Brahma replied, *tan-maya ceti*: “Svaha means maya” Since maya means “potency,” it should have at least become clear to the sages that Brahma was not referring to a fourth potent feature of Gopala.

For us to come to a deeper understanding of Brahma’s definition of svaha and its significance, however, it will help to understand the etymology of the three meanings of maya as they relate to svaha.

The meanings of maya are mahamaya, yogamaya, and mercy, all of which are potencies of Gopala. Informed persons, then, can hardly mistake maya for Gopala, who is maya’s potent source.

Scriptures consistently speak of svaha in this way:

**“From the syllable sva the knower of the field of activities is manifested, and from the syllable ha spiritual knowledge is manifested.**

**“When these two syllables meet in the mouth they become the word svaha. It is with this word that the material universe is destroyed \[meaning that the word svaha liberates one from matter\].”**

In other words, since svaha denotes two aspects of surrender — our own selves (sva), and the act of offering (ha) —we pronounce svaha as we offer oblations into the fire or chant the Gopala mantra.

In chanting svaha, then, we are saying, “I offer myself and everything that’s mine to Lord Hari.” This is the essence of Krsna’s ultimate instruction, mam ekam saranam vraja: “Surrender unto Me alone.”

This meaning of svaha gives Brahma’s answer — “svaha means maya,” — an added dimension: juxtaposed with Gopijana-vallabha, the answer shows how the word maya relates to surrender.

Lord Brahma first takes maya to mean mahamaya with the words *svahasrito jagad ejayat suretah*: “Svaha is the impetus and shelter of the material world \[mahamaya\] and the living beings inhabiting it.”

Illusion and surrender may seem unrelated, but studying the workings of maya through the teachings of liberated souls inspires us to free ourselves from illusion, which is possible only through surrender.

Conditioned souls initially surrender to maya for the sake of independent enjoyment. But when we finally surrender to Krsna we overcome all illusion and gain real liberation in service to the Lord.

Krsna tells Arjuna that many sages have attained perfection by knowledge of the modes of nature. He also explains that the essence of that knowledge is to surrender at His lotus feet.

What is the result of full surrender to the Lord? He protects us from maya in all her beguiling features by bestowing upon us love for Him, a task only He, the most powerful (suretah) can perform.

Still we may wonder, “Is Krsna not also subject to the bewildering effects of mahamaya?” The answer is that Krsna is the master of maya, and so He never comes under her control.

By calling Krsna *niskalaya* (“one who is beyond the jurisdiction of maya”) and *vimohaya* (“He who destroys illusion”), Brahma confirms that the Lord is always in a unique transcendental position.

Lord Brahma next takes mdyd to mean yogamaya. This meaning is significant particularly for practising devotees because it offers them clear insight into the goal of chanting the Gopala mantra.

When we chant svaha to invoke the Lord’s spiritual potency, we are praising the greater opulence that pervades Krsna’s spiritual realm, wherein His eternal companions swim in never-ending bliss.

This spiritual potency, known as yogamaya, reveals to us Krsna, Govinda, and Gopijana-vallabha in proportion to the degree of our surrender to the Lord — provided our lives are free of offence.

Sri Krsna personally makes this truth known in the famous verse of Bhagavad-gita,

*ye yatha mam prapadyante tams tathaiva bhajamy aham*

**“As all surrender unto Me, I reward them accordingly.”**

In addition to revealing Krsna to us, yogamaya inspires our progressive surrender by rewarding us with the inner joy that comes from gradually drawing closer to the reservoir of all pleasure.

And when we surrender fully, yogamaya rewards us with prema. That love then suffuses our calls of svaha with an intense spiritual longing that inevitably attracts Krsna and draws Him into our presence.

Yogamaya, then, first inspires us, nudging us through devotional service in practice, then to ecstatic devotion, and finally to loving devotion, at which time she offers us at the lotus feet of the Lord.

Since yogamaya pervades devotional service, just as mahamaya pervades mundane activities, it is only when svaha invokes the grace of yogamaya that the words of the Gopala mantra are effective.

Krsna can only be known through His mercy, and yogamaya delivers that mercy. Our utterance of svaha, then, must first invoke yogamaya for the names of the mantra to reveal their unique spiritual treasures.

Brahma declared this utter dependence on yogamaya when he said to Krsna,

**“If one is favoured by even a slight trace of the mercy of Your lotus feet, he can understand the greatness of Your personality.”**

In summary, when we take mdyd to mean mahamaya, then our chanting of svaha conveys our surrender to Krsna as an appeal to protect us from the conditioning influence of the modes of nature.

But when we take maya to mean yogamaya, then our chanting of Svaha conveys our surrender to Krsna as an appeal to deliver us to the eternally liberated spiritual nature, to blissful eternal life.

Finally, when we take maya to mean mercy, then our chanting of svaha, as already related, is an appeal for the Lord’s causeless grace, without which devotion cannot rise from practice to perfection.

Calling out for Sri Krsna’s mercy reminds us of two renowned embodiments of that mercy: Sri Radha and Caitanya Mahaprabhu, both features of Gopijana-vallabha, the last word in transcendence.

As already explained in the discussion of klim, Radha and Krsna are inextricably linked as worshipper and worshipped. And They combine as Gauranga to worship Gopijana-vallabha, the worshipful.

The only honest path to Radha and Krsna, then, is the mercy of Gauranga, just as the only path to Krsna is the mercy of Radha. This is the true path of surrender, and any other path is simply pretense.

Gauranga and Radha are always distributing Krsna’s mercy to others, always trying to connect others to Krsna. Is it any wonder, then, that Krsna proclaims the preaching spirit as most pleasing to Him?

And it follows that when we take svaha to mean mercy, we are calling out to Gauri, Gaura, and Gopala to inspire us by Their mercy to help those who have not yet heard of pure devotion to Lord Krsna.

In conclusion, svaha is an appeal to Hari. It says, “I offer myself at Your lotus feet. You do not need me or my service, but because You are my all-merciful friend, I beg You to please accept me.”

By combining these three meanings of maya we come to the full expression of surrender represented by svaha. That utterance is the piteous voice of a greatly afflicted soul entangled in samsara.

The result of having achieved pure devotion, through the mercy of the Gopala mantra and the guidance of yogamaya, is that we finally call out the Lord’s names with true faith and pure longing.

Such pleas for divine service must be sincere. In other words, surrender means not only offering one’s very self to the Lord but also being accepted by the Lord. That is divine grace.

Although the Gopala mantra does not follow the gayatri format, svaha is equivalent to pracodayat, signifying the two kinds of prema that impel the Lord in His Vraja pastimes — lust and relationship.

For example, devotees who aspire for the mood of the young gopis offer themselves into the same fire of divine lust (kama) that attracts and pleases Gopijana-vallabha, the connoisseur of divine mellows.

The aim of such devotees is the grace of the gopijanas, by which they may be admitted into this sacred means of expressing their love for Govinda and given their own place in the circle of His gopi beloveds.

After fully understanding their father’s teachings on the Gopala mantra — including the call for full self-surrender (svaha) — the Kumaras were convinced that they too should be devotees of Krsna.

Hoping to cultivate the sort of pure devotional service that leads to unconditional surrender, Sanaka requested that his father instruct them in the upasana process of worshipping the Lord.

Brahma happily taught his intelligent sons the process of Deity worship connected to the chanting of the Gopala mantra, and in this way he showed them the path leading back to Godhead.

In addition to these instructions, Lord Brahma taught the four Kumaras other prayers and directed them to repeat those prayers along with the Gopala mantra as part of their daily devotions.

A prayerful mood is well suited to all of us who aspire for love of God. Just as we regularly chant the prayers of Sri Brahma-samhita, we may also chant the prayers of the Gopala-tapani Upanisad.

Those wonderful prayers conclude with the words,

**“Be kind, O blissful one! Be kind, O Supreme Lord! Bitten by the snake of material distress and writhing in pain, I beg You, ‘Rescue me!’**

**“O Krsna! O beloved husband of Rukmini! O enchanter of the gopis! O spiritual master of the universe! Drowning in the ocean of repeated birth and death, I call out to You, ‘Please rescue me.’”**

**\*\*\*\***

The glories of the Gopala mantra are like a shoreless ocean. Taking a few palmfuls of those glories, I have, by the kindness of that ocean, offered its water back into itself. May Gopala accept my attempt.

Material existence is also a great ocean, and the Gopala mantra is a boat that many great persons have taken to cross it in order to attain the Vraja-vasis’ merciful guidance and the lotus feet of Sri Krsna.

Bilvamangala Thakura, for example, was reprimanded for his misplaced attachment by Cintamani, who reawakened his devotional love for Radha’s and Krsna’s amorous pastimes in Vrndavana.

At the first opportunity, Bilvamangala sought out the great Vaisnava Somagiri and disclosed his ardent desire to be sheltered by the lover of Sri Radha. Somagiri was pleased by this change of heart.

On the bank of the Krsna-venva River, Somagiri initiated Bilvamangala with the king of mantras, the Gopala mantra, and the new initiate’s ecstasy in separation from Krsna erupted like a volcano.

In time Bilvamangala accepted sannyasa and left for Vrndavana. By continually chanting the Gopala mantra, his ecstasy matured into prema, and Krsna’s pastimes and companions appeared in his mind.

The dhama’s sublime atmosphere fueled Bilvamangala’s feelings of loving separation and he eventually had direct vision of Radha’s and Krsna’s pastimes as Cintamani had once narrated them to him.

Bilvamangala’s incomparable poetry, including his magnum opus, Krsna-karnamrta, won him the name Lilasuka. After many years of living in Vraja, he entered into the pastimes he had written about.

Bilvamangala depicted amorous love in separation in such verses as

*ṁḍīñó adhanyani dinantarani hare tvad-alokanam antarena*  
*anatha-bandho karunaika-sindho ha hanta ha hanta katham nayami*

**“O my Lord! O friend of the helpless! You arc the only ocean of mercy! Because I have not met You, my inauspicious days and nights have become unbearable. I do not know how I shall pass the time.”**

Such is the great potency of Krsna’s primary names as they appear from klim— the Gopala mantra’s kama-bija — or from Caitanya Mahaprabhu’s lips as the maha-mantra, Hare Krsna.

At the conclusion of his teachings, as recorded in the Gopala-tapani Upanisad, Brahma instructed his sons to worship the Lord with the prayers and the eighteen-syllable mantra he had given them.

Lord Brahma said, *sri krsnam dhyayantah samsrtim tansyathi*: “My sons! O great sages! By meditating on Lord Sri Krsna as I have taught you, you will transcend the world of birth and death.”

Then, after speaking the following glorification of the eighteen- syllable Gopala mantra, the creator concluded by saying,

**“The five-word mantra that I have pronounced is directly the form of Krsna.**

**“Any person who constantly chants this mantra easily obtains the company of that transcendental cowherd, Krsna. In other words, he attains the infallible realm of pure goodness known as Goloka.**

**“That divine realm is unchangeable, beyond the reach of the mind and senses, and unattained even by great demigods full of mystic potency and empowered for universal administration. Om tat sat.”**

To practice the type of remembrance emphasised by Brahma, we need a humble state of mind, a level of humility encapsulated by Caitanya Mahaprabhu in the third verse of His famous Siksastaka prayers:

**“One who thinks himself lower than the grass, who is more tolerant than a tree, and who does not expect personal honour but is always prepared to give all respect to others can very easily always chant the holy name of the Lord.”**

Humility is the soil that nourishes serious and determined sadhana. Such sincere practice forms fertile furrows in the garden of devotion.

The soil of that garden is also enriched by transcendental knowledge, which encompasses realisations of Krsna’s divinity, His identity as a cowherd, and His ultimate feature as the gopis' lover.

A transcendental seed is planted there — the eighteen-syllable, five-word Gopala mantra that we receive from our initiating spiritual masters, klim krsnaya govindaya gopijana-vallabhaya svaha.

Impelled by klim, and expressed as svaha, the balmy sun of Krsna- meditations overhead and the water of offenceless chanting raining down from the chalice of pure aspiration, a divine creeper sprouts.

If we patiently tend this delicate, budding creeper, it will luxuriate with unfolding leaves, blossoming flowers, and ultimately the luscious fruits of devotional mellows that we can taste with Krsna in Vraja.

Depending on our individual aspiration — the fixed intention with which we offer ourselves in service to the Lord of our heart — the creeper of the Gopala mantra bears its fruits accordingly.

Those of us who aspire for service to Sri Krsna, whom we know to herd cows and cavort with cowherd girls, will relish the fruit of servitude in Vraja under the supervision of devotees like Raktaka.

Those of us attracted to the service of Govinda, whom we know as the lover of the gopis and the source of all incarnations, will be rewarded with the taste of friendship in the mood of Sridama.

And those of us who adore Gopijana-vallabha, known also as wonderful Krsna and the cowherd Govinda, taste the fruit of amorous enjoyment from the desire vine of the Gopala mantra.

To the extent that we cultivate our little garden plots and inspire others to do the same, to that extent the whole world will become overgrown with rasa-bearing creepers, choking Kali-yuga’s evil weeds.

In this way the kama-bija and the Gopala mantra bestow selfless love upon those who yearn for it, but give merely liberation or a place in heaven to less-intelligent brahmanas intent on such paltry fruits.

Wise Vaisnavas know that it would be pure folly to ask the wealthiest man, Krsna, for the pile of dust that is material enjoyment. Instead they request from Him the priceless treasure of loving devotion.

In pursuit of pure devotion, we may sometimes pray for the fulfilment of a need that may seem material but is not a detriment to our service, and may even aid our spiritual advancement.

This kind of prayer is still within the realm of pure devotion, as Rupa Gosvami suggests with the inclusion of the suffix ita in the phrase anyabhilasita-sunyam (“complete freedom from desires”).

Ramanujacarya, for instance, instructed his blinded servant Kuresa to petition Varadaraja with the prayer, “Dear Lord! Please return my eyesight so that I may again serve my spiritual master as before.”

Under superior spiritual direction we may also chant the Gopala mantra to get free from situations so distressing that they obstruct another Vaisnava execution of devotional service, or even our own.

For this reason I will outline a few meditations that — by the mercy of the Lord — grant relief from adverse circumstances when coupled with the chanting of the wish-fulfiling Gopala mantra.

The effectiveness of this process is dependent upon the purity of one’s chanting and, ultimately, upon Krsna’s will. Though we petition Him in one way, the independent Lord may act in quite another.

For instance, Narada Muni brought Maharaja Citraketu’s dead son back to life in answer to the king’s pleas, hut the boy lived only long enough to instruct his illusioned father before again departing.

With these thoughts in mind, devotees suffering during famine or other food shortages may chant the Gopala mantra while meditating upon baby Gopala being breastfed by His mother or His nurses.

To relieve a child from high fever one may meditate upon how Krsna caught Bakasura and, by tearing one half of the demon’s beak from the other, killed he who was like a fever to the residents of heaven.

To counteract the effects of severe rain, lightning, or other storms, one may meditate upon Krsna holding up Govardhana Hill with His left hand while playing His flute with His right.

If suffering from drought or high fever one may pray for relief by remembering Krsna and the gopis playfully splashing one another with the cool, blackish water of the Yamuna River.

At times one may be poisoned by spoiled food or by an enemy. Toxins can be neutralised by thoughts of Krsna dancing upon the heads of Kaliya, who had contaminated the Yamuna with his poison.

And for deliverance from illness or general misfortune, devotees may meditate upon how Krsna killed the demoness Putana, an embodi ment of all misfortune and a paragon of the false preceptor.

We must be careful when soliciting the Lord in ways like these, lest we become enchanted and thus distracted from our goal by the wondrous benefits granted by the cintamani Gopala mantra.

Having come to the very threshold of Goloka, we must be wary that maya allurements do not prevent us from receiving the rightful inheritance due to us through the Gopala mantra and maha-mantra.

But even if we foolishly take devotional service as a means to fulfil material desires, Krsna schemes in ways that compel us to abandon material calculation and wholeheartedly surrender to the Lord.

Krsna is so wonderful! He never allows even an errant servant to stray from His lotus feet and abandon the nectar of pure devotional service. Once, in Bharata-varsa, the demigods praised the Lord:

**“Whenever Krsna is requested to fulfil one’s desire, He undoubtedly does so, but He does not award anything which, after being enjoyed, will cause one to petition Him again to fulfil further desires.**

**“When one has other desires but serves the Lord, Krsna forcibly gives one shelter at His lotus feet, where one will naturally forget all other desires.”**

In short, having once served Him, none can escape Him.

**\*\*\*\***

In Sri Caitanya-caritamrta, Caitanya Mahaprabhu explains to Ramananda Raya that an advanced Vaisnava sees his own worshipable Deity (nija ista-deva) everywhere.

The Deity form of the Lord to whom one’s heart becomes especially attached and to whom the fortunate devotee consequently dedicates his very life is called one’s worshipable Deity, or ista-deva.

Even though the great devotee Pradyumna Brahmacari, for example, would serve the Deities of Lord Jagannatha and Lord Sri Caitanya Mahaprabhu, his ista-deva was Lord Nrsimhadeva.

Similarly, Hanumanji knew that Ramacandra was the same person as Narayana, and yet lotus-eyed Rama alone was the be-all and end-all of his life, his ista-deva. Such spiritual partiality is spontaneous.

I first accepted as my ista-devas Kisora-Kisori of iskcon Chicago. I happily strung garlands for Them every day, and They forcefully captured my mind, becoming my constant meditation.

When Kisora-Kisori sent me to the United Kingdom, despite my affection for the Deities presiding over my new home, I was unable to give my heart to Them as I had done in Chicago. Thus I lamented.

But when Radha-Syamasundara entered my life as the isvara and isvari of Hungary, I felt that same spontaneous flow of affection even stronger than before. Once again I had found my ista-devas.

Now, in reciting prayers or mantras, in the midst of kirtana or while chanting japa, Radha-Syamasundara arc my meditation and goal. Even in other Radha-Krsna Deities, I look for Radha-Syama.

Indeed, just as Srila Prabhupada once prayed to Radha-Gokulananda to be engaged in the service of Radha-Rasabihari, I pray to every Deity I meet to engage me in the service of Radha-Syamasundara.

Our ista-devas make indelible impressions, called sphurti, within our minds. When I appeal to Krsna, Govinda, and Gopijana-vallabha, my thoughts are always fixed on Sri Syamasundara of New Vraja-dhama.

I have often seen devotees in our society display a special attachment to their worshipable Deities. Sometimes it is to the first Deity with whom they developed a relationship by seeing and serving.

The Deity one first worships and becomes attracted to often becomes one’s ista-deva, just as the patha-pradarsaka-guru, who first points a person to the path leading to Krsna, often becomes his diksa-guru.

Srila Prabhupada explains this in relation to the role Suniti played as Dhruva Maharaja’s patha-pradarsaka-guru, or the spiritual master who first shows one the way to Krsna, also known as the siksa-guru.

His Divine Grace writes, “Generally the siksa-guru later on becomes the diksa-guru.” The diksa-guru is the one Vaisnava amongst many through whom his disciples offer their acts of devotion to Krsna.

Let me now describe how Krsnadasa Kaviraja Gosvami revealed Madana-mohana as his family Deity (ista-deva) and how the Lord ordered His servant to compose Caitanya Mahaprabhu’s biography.

Having received the order of the Vaisnavas to compose Caitanya-caritamrta , but being anxious within his heart, he went to the temple of Madana-mohana in Vrndavana to ask the Lord’s permission also.

Krsnadasa Kaviraja Gosvami prayed at Madana-mohana’s lotus feet, begging for His blessings. In response, the Lord caused a garland to slip from His neck to the ground as a sign of divine reciprocation.

Kaviraja Gosvami accepted the garland from Madana-mohana’s pujari, and with it he accepted the Lord’s order and immediately began writing. In first undertaking that task, he humbly prayed:

**“Caitanya-caritamrta is not my writing but the dictation of Madana- mohana. My writing is like the repetition of a parrot. As a wooden doll is made to dance by a magician, I write as the Lord orders me.”**

He concluded his prayers with this intimate thought,

**“I accept as my family Deity Madana-mohana, whose worshippers are Raghunatha Dasa Gosvami, Rupa Gosvami, and Sanatana Gosvami.”**

I have already begged permission from my ista-devas and my readers to reveal some of the acts of mercy Radha-Syamasundara have showed to me, and to divulge some of my meditations upon Them.

Before I began to write this book, the leaders of New Vraja-dhama requested me to include some details of my spiritual journey as a resident of their community and a servant of Radha-Syamasundara.

To fulfil the Vaisnavas’ request, with the hope that what I share will be of help to Radha-Syamasundara’s devotees, I have sprinkled my personal history, prayers, and aspirations throughout this work.

In this and the following chapters, which are meant to glorify most explicitly Radha-Syamasundara, the rain cloud of my attachment to Them has increased from a sprinkling to a shower.

I pray that devotees who serve elsewhere in ISKCON will also find my words helpful in nurturing attachment to their own ista-devas, who in the true sense of the word are actually the same as mine.

This chapter concludes with some of the events surrounding my receiving the treasure of the Gopala mantra and some thoughts as to how I developed personally as I repeated this divine sound vibration.

**\*\*\*\***

I make offering of oblations unto Lord Krsna, who is Govinda, the lover of the gopis." In this translation of the Gopala mantra, Srila Prabhupada emphasises service to the cowherd lover of the gopis.

Just as his spiritual master, Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati Thakura, had taught him, Srila Prabhupada teaches us that regulated devotional service is the proper way to cultivate the seed of spontaneous love.

We receive the seed of the creeper of pure devotional service in the form of transcendental sound carrying Sri Krsna’s message as taught by Lord Caitanya and relayed through our Gaudiya parampara.

Srila Prabhupada defines this seed:

**“The methods, rules and regulartions by which one is perfectly trained in devotional service constitute the bhakti-lata-bija, or seed of devotional service.”**

Devotees on all platforms of spiritual realisation dutifully perform the activities of regulated devotional service, although one’s unique mood and meditation evolve internally as the heart is purified.

The process of internal cultivation is included in the teachings, but its growth requires support and guidance from saintly persons, hearing Srimad-Bhagavatam, and chanting Krsna’s names.

I received brahminical initiation in early 1974. Even now I remember the bliss of first chanting the diksa-mantras, although at that time I had little understanding of what I was doing or why.

But even by immaturely chanting the diksa-mantras, because I was sincerely trying to follow Srila Prabhupada’s instructions, I was put under the care and guidance of the presiding Deities of Vraja.

Let me start at the beginning, or at least at the time in my life when I first made significant contact with Krsna consciousness and when Lord Krsna began to gently nudge me towards Vrndavana.

In 1969 I was living in a tent on the Keys—islands that stretch westward from Florida’s southern tip — subsisting on coconuts and the nectar from MacMillan’s blue first edition of Bhagavad-gita As It Is.

Though at first sceptical that Krsna’s teachings were interrupted by “purports,” I was convinced by Bhakti vedanta Swami’s teachings and his assertion that Krsna is divine. I thought, “Krsna is God!”

A mere three-word sentence, but no meagre realisation. After twenty years, my life suddenly had a solid anchor. In the months and years that followed I purchased whatever books on Krsna were available.

From a series of paperbacks extracted from the Second and Third Cantos of Srimad-Bhagavatam, I learned that Krsna was the source of many incarnations. I gazed at a picture of the boar with a crown.

The Second Canto also contained a striking verse and purport that taught how to “perceive the Supreme Lord by reason and perception.” I memorised the argument and regularly presented it to others.

**“The Personality of Godhead Sri Krsna is in every living being along with the individual soul. And this fact is perceived and hypothesised in our acts of seeing and taking help from the intelligence."**

It was, and is, comforting to think that Krsna is in my heart. As I read more in the Srimad-Bhagavatam about the process of creation, I could better recognise Krsna’s greatness, opulence, and wisdom.

Teachings of Lord Caitanya became my next primary interest. In its pages I learned that Krsna had appeared as recently as five hundred years ago as Lord Caitanya to introduce the sankirtana movement.

On a visit to the iskcon temple in Miami, I bought a set of “Krsna Book,” newly arrived from Japan. The temple president told me, “It you read a chapter before going to sleep, you will dream of Krsna.”

I can’t remember dreaming of Krsna, but I do remember coming to the conclusion that Krsna’s divinity is ultimately “human-like” and that “Back to Godhead” means “Back to Goloka Vrndavana.”

Around that time the cassette player had become widely available, giving me access to Srila Prabhupada’s recorded lectures. His accent made listening a challenge, but I had faith in what I did understand.

Perhaps because they were easier to listen to, I often played tapes of the “Krsna Book” that had been produced for radio. Of those narrations I was mesmerised by “The Gopis Attracted by the Flute.”

The beautifully poetic images in the gopis’ words, and the sweet emotions those images invoked, held me transfixed and inspired the thought-provoking realisation that Krsna was a lover of many women.

As mentioned earlier, I had been fascinated by a richly coloured Brijbasi print of Radha and Krsna. I felt drawn to enter the idyllic countryside it depicted, though I wasn’t sure what I would do there.

In 1973 I finally left material life behind and joined ISKCON Montreal. The volume of Srila Prabhupada’s literary output at that time was astonishing; indeed, we could not read at the pace he wrote.

Over the next few years (1973 to 1977) I intensified my study of Srimad-Bhagavatam, and the Caitanya-caritamrta became a source of inspiration for my preaching, as it had become for many of my peers.

During those years my services alternated from book distribution, to selling incense, to taking responsibility as a temple president, and then, happily, back to book distribution again — for some time.

And then, in November of 1977, that seemingly endless stream of nectarean transcendental publications sadly stopped its flow when Srila Prabhupada departed from this mortal world.

The time of Srila Prabhupada’s disappearance was for everyone a period of sober reflection. Personally, I pondered how my march back to Godhead could succeed in my ever-well-wisher’s physical absence.

“But then,” I concluded, “Srila Prabhupada has guided me thus far almost exclusively through his books and his disciples. Why should it be any different from now until we meet again?”

If I had to identify a time at which my Krsna consciousness became enhanced by tangible, dynamic attachment to Krsna’s Vrndavana pastimes, it would be just after Srila Prabhupada’s departure.

It was then that my understanding of Krsna’s divinity became significantly enriched by attraction to the Lord as a cowherd boy in Vraja, a spiritual development typically overseen by Govindaji.

Looking back, something significant also happened prior to this time. On Radhastami in 1975 I spent the whole day with devotees, reading aloud about Srimati Radharani from Srila Prabhupada’s books.

As we read, the atmosphere in the temple room became enhanced by an exceptionally intense and special quality that none of us had felt before. It was a taste of being with Krsna’s pleasure potency.

From that memorable event many realisations lingered on. Especially significant were Srila Prabhupada’s words, “In Vrndavana Krsna is addressed as Radha-Krsna, or Krsna, the property of Radharani.”

From my first days as a devotee I was made aware that love of God was the goal of life, and over the years I had other encounters of a spiritual nature, not unlike that Radhastami celebration.

It wasn’t until after Srila Prabhupada’s disappearance, however, that I noticed a novel and enduring attachment for the presiding Deities of iskcon Chicago, Kisora-Kisori, growing within my heart.

Attachment became absorption, which persisted throughout the day until I dozed off to sleep at night. And it greeted me again as soon as my eyes opened the next morning. It was intense and it was blissful.

And what exactly was the nature of that absorption? Remembrance of Their Lordship’s graceful forms! In my mind I would sometimes make offerings to Them and sometimes offer arati. It was upasana.

At times I felt that moonlight had condensed itself into the soothing white forms of a youthful teenage couple, or that perhaps the full moon itself had become the comforting face of sweet Kisora.

Through Kisora, Govindaji acted as my siksa-guru by helping me to cultivate affection for the divine couple, which introduced me to a realm of sadhana-bhakti wherein devotion was mixed with attraction.

Srila Prabhupada says that Govindaji teaches devotees how to love and serve Radha and Krsna. Learning to love means to awaken the love for the divine couple that is lying dormant in the heart.

Devotional service in practice cleanses the soot from the lamp of the heart and reveals the flame of love that burns within. Like a gentle breeze, meditating on love’s object makes the flame burn brighter.

Attachment then fosters material detachment. One of my aspirations in Krsna consciousness had been to take sannyasa. My increasing attachment to Krsna compelled me to cut my worldly connections.

I accepted the renounced order of life in Mayapur in the spring of 1979, and the sannyasa-mantra I received confirmed my understanding of the goal of Krsna consciousness.

The sannyasa-mantra impels its chanter to offer himself in the mood of the gopis. I understood the theory, but the practical application had not yet become clear to me. Nonetheless, the mantra would act.

The prayer of the Avantipura brahmana chanted by Lord Caitanya after His sannyasa was further confirmation of surrender: “I shall cross over the insurmountable ocean of nescience —

By being firmly fixed in the service of the lotus feet of Krsna. This was approved by the previous acaryas, who were fixed in firm devotion to the Lord, Paramatma, the Personality of Godhead.”

**\*\*\*\***

I had made a lifetime commitment to serving Kisora-Kisori, but They sent me across the ocean, where I did not have the same rapport with the Deities as I had had with Their Lordships in Chicago.

I was puzzled. Why had I lost my ista-devas? Had They rejected me? But Dayala-Nitai and Gaura-Hari appeared, filled the vacuum in my heart, and made me appreciate why things had happened as they had.

Although we may have tasted the sweetness of Radha and Krsna, without worshipping Nitai and Gaura the path to Vraja will be long and fraught with dangers. By serving Gaura it all becomes easy.

**“If one appreciates the merciful pastimes of Lord Caitanya and feels ecstasy and sometimes cries, this process will immediately help him to understand the eternal pastimes of Radha-Krsna.”**

“If one says, ‘Let me dive deep into the waves of the nectarean ocean of the transcendental loving movement introduced by Gaura,’ one immediately becomes a confidential servant of Radha-Krsna.

Through my studies, association, and sadhana, the mercy of Gaura-Nitai and Govindaji gradually manifested itself, and I discovered that the fullness of time reveals our specific attraction to Krsna.

Srila Prabhupada explains that even practising devotees may be naturally attracted to a certain mood îf vraja-bhakti, and thereafter inclined to follow in the footsteps of a specific associate of Krsna.

He quotes Srila Bhaktisiddhanta:

**“When one has developed such attachment \[for Krsna’s names, forms, etc.\]...a devotee can follow in the footsteps of one of the eternal associates of Krsna in Vraja.**

**“In the friendly sakhya-rasa one can become a friend like... Sridama, in...parental affection like...Yasoda and in...conjugal love like... Lalita... \[or\] Rupa. This is the essence of all instruction in bhakti”.**

Srila Prabhupada also often warned against trying to artificially accelerate that which will develop naturally if given time. He deplored the behaviour of the sahajiyas who attempt to do just that.

Srila Prabhupada expected his disciples to learn to love Krsna, but he ordered them to preach tirelessly and always act in an exemplary way while keeping their internal spiritual progress to themselves.

In Srila Prabhupada’s words, loving Krsna is our “only business.” And although I did not — and still do not — love Krsna, the inclination to awaken my love for Krsna increases with every passing day.

The mood of devotional service that most attracts me is that of Sri Radha’s maidservants, such as Rupa-manjari. And although that lofty goal may elude me for lifetimes to come, still I aspire for it.

Srila Prabhupada, after all, writes,

**“Special natural appreciation of the descriptions of a particular pastime of Godhead indicates the constitutional position of a living entity.”**

One book in particular, Lalita-madhava, contributed to solidifying my desire to serve Sri Radha. I have read it many times, perhaps twenty or more, and one of those readings inspired in me something special.

I was in Vrndavana. I had been reading Lalita-madhava to our neigh-hour, Arca-vigraha, who was preparing to leave her body. At the conclusion of the book I became very emotional and excused myself.

I didn’t want to be seen, and so instead of returning home I took refuge in a garden off the parikrama path. There I lay on the ground, baring my heart to Radha and Krsna. For how long, I don’t know.

When I finally got up I saw, hardly two paces away and moving straight towards me, a male and female cobra. I barely had time to step aside and let them pass into the undergrowth.

Upon returning home I told Govinda Maharaja what had happened. He remarked, “Seeing a cobra couple is very auspicious.” I took that omen to be a kind of answer to my prayers.

Significant spiritual effects come from residing in Puri, Mayapur, or Vrndavana. As Srila Prabhupada says of Vraja, “...Anyone who goes there will never want to return to this material world.”

During the eighties and nineties I would visit yearly and dî parikrama at these three places. I even built a house in Vrndavana where I once stayed for six months. As a result, I became attached to Vrndavana.

As that attachment developed I came to realise that Vrndavana is my real home. On the strength of that conviction I have endeavoured to bring Vrndavana to Hungary as a home for the devotees there.

To live in Vraja is to live with Radha and Krsna. To become attached to the dhama is to become attached to the divine couple. Vrndavana and its pastime places are therefore an impetus for loving the Lord.

But the essence of visiting Vrndavana is to hear from saintly persons. Actually, the company of Vaisnava saints, wherever they may be, is the real way — in fact the only way — to see Vrndavana.

Over the years I have been fortunate enough to have the company of godbrothers both attached to and learned in the truths of Vraja. They have helped stoke the fire of my attachment by their guidance.

Commenting on Narada Muni’s instructions to King Pracinabarhi, Srila Prabhupada writes,

**“If one gets the chance to hear from pure devotees in such a place \[Vrndavana-dhama\]—**

**“Allowing the constant flow of the river of nectar to come from the mouths of pure devotees, then the cultivation of Krsna consciousness becomes very easy.”**

Of course, it has been just as Prabhupada says.

Yet from my very first days in devotional life, chanting Hare Krsna was the most significant thing protecting me from the material energy and guiding me towards my worshipful Deities.

Alongside my burgeoning attachment to the divine couple, the invaluable jewels of japa, bhajana, and kirtana sparkled with ever-newer facets, reflecting the things I had seen and heard about Vraja.

As my chanting of the Hare Krsna maha-mantra was enriched, meditation on my diksa-mantras became a more meaningful and increasingly relevant source of nourishment for my spiritual journey.

While chanting gopijana-vallabhaya svaha with sincere feeling, I endeavoured to offer myself into the same fire of loving service to Radha and Krsna stoked by the sannyasa-mantra.

And when I began to regularly dress Radha-Syamasundara, that feeling became more a meditation on direct service to Them, in either Their Deity forms or in Their original forms in Goloka.

During those meditations a desire arose to serve in an identity compatible with Their Lordship’s transcendental forms, and thus my identification as a servant of Lord Krsna was further enhanced.

I had been bathing and dressing Dayala-Nitai—Gaura-Hari daily for over fifteen years, but when I began to do the same for Radha-Syamasundara the emotions I had felt for Kisora-Kisori returned.

Suddenly, the spiritual emotions I had been cultivating—by reading, chanting, associating, and going on pilgrimage — found their centre in Radha-Syamasundara, who unfailingly reciprocated with affection.

Kaviraja Gosvami says,

**“The ideal place to execute Krsna consciousness is Vrndavana, where the people are naturally inclined to love Krsna and Krsna is naturally inclined to love them.”**

If I were to say that Radha-Syamasundara were generous in responding to my service, I would not be exaggerating. Devotees in New Vraja-dhama are likely to give similar testimonies.

By expanding Their personal opulences, Radha-Syamasundara worked Their magic on me through the thrill of seeing Their ever fresh beauty and the joy of being engaged in Their personal service.

Like the many pieces of a jigsaw puzzle, the three decades of my devotional life finally came together in the most pleasing and rewarding way. I was finally Radha-Syamasundara’s servant.

Sometimes I feel I was more an observer of that process than a dynamic part of it. Srila Prabhupada describes that same process as the development of natural attraction on the way to perfection.

“This development of conjugal love can be possible only with those already engaged in following the regulative principles of devotional service, specifically in the worship of Radha-Krsna in the temple.

“Such devotees gradually develop a spontaneous love for the Deity, and by hearing of the Lord's exchange of loving affairs with the gopis, they gradually become attracted to these pastimes.

“After this spontaneous attraction becomes highly developed, the devotee is placed in either of the abovementioned categories \[one of two relationships with Krsna in the perfected form of a gopi.\]”

Although I was happy and worry-free in Radha-Syamasundara’s company, my duties did not allow me to remain in New Vraja-dhama. In my travels as a sannyasi I was often separated from Them.

I knew well the teachings of Caitanya Mahaprabhu, which assert that service in separation nourishes spiritual attachment and makes meeting with the beloved all the more relishable.

Yet that knowledge did not soften the pain I would feel when leaving New Vraja-dhama, nor did it fill the emptiness I felt in separation from Radha and Syama, despite my constant effort to think of Them.

In my prayers I would sometimes lament that the source of my life’s greatest happiness had become the cause of my greatest distress. Unexpectedly, it was Lord Jagannatha who solved my dilemma.

One year, while visiting Jagannatha Puri, my desire to always be with Radha-Syamasundara was fulfilled when Radha-Damodara accepted me as Their caretaker. It was incredible mercy I did not deserve.

I pray, “O worshipable Deities of Caitanya Mahaprabhu’s most intimate associate! Whenever I think of who You are and what I am, I am able to describe neither Your kindness nor my insignificance.

“O Damodara! Tradition has it that You personally moved Your arms to offer Sri Radha Your flute. If You can perform miracles such as that, no doubt You can also make me worthy to serve You.”

I have described some of Govindaji’s teachings by which the faint reflection of the shadow of attachment to Radha and Syama has appeared in my heart. May real attachment come in a future lifetime.

I worship Sri Krsna, who has appeared in New Vraja-dhama as a cowherd boy, bringing with Him Sri Radha, the regal garland of jasmine flowers in the decorated braid that is the gopis of Vraja.

The Gopala mantra calls Krsna, lover of the gopis, Gopijana-vallabha. And His service is meant for perfected souls who, in their realised spiritual bodies (siddha-deha), follow after the eternally perfect gopis.

Since I remain a conditioned soul attached to my physical body, I can only aspire for such perfection, hoping that with every oblation of Svaha I draw nearer and nearer to my desired destination.

Sitting on the near shore of this vast ocean of material existence, I look out over the surging waves of material desire and wonder how and when my spiritual yearnings will become reality.

And yet I have firm faith that Govinda and His associates routinely make the impossible possible. Thus I chant my diksa-mantras and the maha-mantra, Srila Prabhupada’s gifts, and wait. And wait.

## Chapter 35 - Kama-gayatri

Radha and Krsna manifest Themselves in transcendental sound as the Kama-gayatri mantra. The syllables of this mantra are the limbs of Their bodies, and the pure chanting of the mantra Their pastimes.

Chanting the Kama-gayatri awakens the desire to serve Krsna as the gopis do. The more one chants, the more one cultivates that desire, until it finally reaches maturity at the stage of transcendence.

We may question whether it is proper for Srila Prabhupada’s followers to cultivate such desires. The answer is inherent in the kama-bija we received from His Divine Grace along with our other diksa-mantras.

Srila Prabhupada initiated his disciples, who in turn initiate theirs, with the seed of desire (klim). By its very nature this seed arouses desire for service to the presiding Deities of those diksa-mantras.

Pure chanting naturally awakens a desire in us to serve Gaura, Gopala, and Kamadeva in the ways glorified by Their mantras, ways consistent with our constitutional relationship with the Lord.

The answer, then, is yes. To aspire for eternal service after carefully watering the seed of desire (kama-bija) is to be true to Srila Prabhupada’s guidance. Indeed, such sincere desire surely pleases him.

The Kama-gayatri (*klim kamadevaya vidmahe puspa-banaya dhimahi tan no 'nangah pracodayat*) is a transcendental hymn that, as Srila Prabhupada says, "... simply does not belong to this material world.”

This is Srila Prabhupada’s translation of the mantra:

**“Let us meditate on the master of the senses, who carries the arrows of flowers. Let us meditate on Him being thus enthused by the transcendental Cupid.”**

First let us hear Kama-gayatri’s general attributes. Then I will analyse the meanings of each word in the mantra and how those meanings correspond to meditations on Krsna’s names and the mantra itself.

The Gayatri prefixed by the kama-bija and invoicing Cupid is called Kama-gayatri, and because it expounds the most confidential meaning of klim, Kama-gayatri is considered the best Gayatri of all.

The Kama-gayatri is the natural extension of the Gopala mantra because it elaborates on the full meaning of gopijana-vallabhaya svatha, which is the prayojana portion of the Gopala mantra.

To enter into the pastimes of Gopijana-vallabha — Krsna, the lover of the gopis — we must recite Kama-gayatri in the same mood of spontaneous devotion and unconditional love praised by the mantra.

When chanted in that mood, Kama-gayatri reveals Gopijana-vallabha to be the ever fresh Cupid of Vrndavana, whose names, forms, pastimes, and qualities are not encapsulated fully by any other hymn.

Krsna’s flute playing on the harmonic scale attracts all living entities, but He reserves the fifth note to call the gopis. The Kama-gayatri, born from this fifth note, is thus rich with amorous mellows.

To fully appreciate the Kama-gayatri, which portrays Krsna’s amorous desire, first we must cultivate knowledge of and properly appreciate Vrndavana, the place that inspires transcendental amorous desire.

Due to a paucity of such knowledge, the demigods were perplexed when they heard about Goloka from Indra. They said, “We have heard you, yet we cannot understand what you have told us.”

But how could unenlightened souls comprehend a place where the soil is cintamani, every tree a kalpataru, every cow a surabhi, every cowherd girl a goddess of fortune, and the flute the Lord’s attendant?

Indra was prepared to fight with Krsna over the loss of a mere parijata tree, so how could he estimate the value of Goloka, where a single blade of grass is worth more than all the gardens in heaven?

To achieve the perfection of life, in which we relish Goloka and its spiritual existence, our hearts must be free of ulterior motives, and we must receive the blessings of Rupa and Raghunatha.

As long as we are overcome by our material conditioning, we will continue to mistake Krsna’s romantic exploits with the gopis for those of an ordinary young boy whose senses are conquered by Cupid.

Ramananda Raya therefore nullifies such material conceptions of Krsna by defining Him as the non-material (aprakrta) or spiritual Cupid of Vraja, and as the object worshipped by the king of gayatris.

Ramananda Raya said,

**“In the spiritual realm of Vrndavana, Krsna is the spiritual, ever-fresh Cupid. He is worshipped by the chanting of the Kama-gayatri mantra, with the spiritual seed klim.”**

Jiva Gosvami points out that at the time of initiation, a qualified Vaisnava receives the Kama-gayatri along with the empowerment “for receiving, practising, and transmitting the mantra.”

By attentive worship of the Kama-gayatri maha-mantra, devotees become purified at heart and proficient in the culture of a mantra-sadhana that leads them to Gayatri’s presiding Deity, Kamadeva.

One example of proficiency in mantra-sadhana is found in Dhruva Maharaja, whom Narada Muni initiated long ago in Madhuvana with a twelve-syllable mantra — om namo bhagavate vasudevaya.

Initially, Dhruva’s spiritual practices were materially motivated, but by the cleansing power of the mantra, and by contact with Narada, he became pure at heart and had a direct vision of Narayana.

An example even more relevant than Dhruva’s is Brahma’s initiation into the Kama-gayatri mantra, which was a turning point in the creator’s devotional austerities and a significant precedent for all.

Brahma’s ascent to an advanced stage of sadhana was the result of his combined austerity, chanting of other mantras — the Brahma-gayatri and Gopala mantra — and his friendship with Lord Krsna.

Impelled by the karma of his previous birth and the desire for universal creation, Lord Brahma appeared from the golden lotus that grew from the navel depression of Garbhodasayi Visnu.

Brahma saw darkness all around him, and so he descended into the water from which his lotus-seat had sprouted. Unable to find the source of the lotus, he resurfaced. He then heard a disembodied voice.

The voice was that of the original goddess of learning, Divya Sarasvati, who said,

**“O Brahma, this mantra, klim krsnaya govindaya gopijana-vallabhaya svaha, will surely fulfil your heart’s desire.”**

Sarasvati instructed Lord Brahma to perform penance (tapa) in addition to reciting his mantra and promised that if he did so with devotion for the Lord, he would realise his desire to create a universe.

Eager to be empowered to create, Brahma diligently endeavoured to please the Supreme Lord, and in time, by the power of the Gopala mantra, his consciousness was elevated to that of a Vaisnava.

With a purified mind spontaneously attracted to Krsna in Goloka Vrndavana, Brahma’s meditation deepened. He was then able to conceive of himself as a maidservant of the divine couple.

In meditation Lord Brahma saw lotus-shaped Goloka. At the centre of that lotus was a golden throne upon which Govinda gracefully sat, playing His flute, surrounded by countless gopis and their assistants.

Because he was still a conditioned soul, Brahma also saw Mahamaya worshipping Govinda from outside Goloka. She appeared ashamed of her service of keeping rebellious souls away from the Lord.

Although Brahma was still under the grip of the modes of nature, and although his bhajana was impure, it was effective enough to inspire Krsna to initiate him and raise him to the status of twice-born.

By the sound of His divine flute, Krsna first imparted to Brahma the Brahma-gayatri, which is the source of the Vedas, and then the Kama-gayatri, the source of desire and sublime transcendental love.

Thus equipped with new mantras and enriched by transcendental knowledge, Brahma applied himself to strict sadhana and gradually rose to the stage of an intermediate devotee, a *madhyama-bhakta*.

By the power of the Kama-gayatri mantra, Brahma came to understand that he was a maidservant of Krsna. But because he was still tainted by the mode of goodness, his realisation was incomplete.

Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati Thakura writes,

**“Brahma thought thus within himself, ‘By the recollection of Kama-gayatri it seems to me that I am the eternal maidservant of Krsna.’”**

By the strength of the three mantras upon which he had meditated, Brahma could understand the essence of the Vedas — Krsna’s divine sports in Goloka. He then sang hymns in praise of Govinda.

The beautiful verses of Brahma-samhita are the record of Brahma’s spontaneous meditation (svarasiki), which was the result of long years of painstaking and determined efforts to attain perfection.

The reality of Kama-gayatri, then, is that Krsna in His original feature as the Cupid of Vraja has taken the form of a Vedic hymn in order to bestow upon its chanters the gopis’ spontaneous devotion.

Krsna, the ever-fresh Cupid, is Kama-gayatri’s presiding Deity (devata); Brahma, its first speaker and presiding sage (rsi); and Sri Radha, who incites and is incited by Krsna, its potency (sakti).

Srila Prabhupada says that Kama-gayatri’s effect is like that of the rising full moon because it inspires selfish desires in the hearts of non-devotees and love for Krsna in the hearts of His devotees.

Visvanatha Cakravarti Thakura also says that a practising devotee who sincerely chants this mantra with devotion will at the end of this life attain a spiritual body and return to Goloka Vrndavana.

Blessed with a form fit for serving Gopijana-vallabha in celestial Vraja, we will eternally engage in satisfying the senses of the Lord. That is the ultimate blessing of the Kama-gayatri mantra.

**\*\*\*\***

Srila Prabhupada refers to Kamadeva as the master of the senses, Puspabana as the carrier of flower-arrows, and Ananga as the transcendental Cupid. These are three names of Gopijana-vallabha.

Later we will analyse each word of the mantra individually, but now let us take a closer look at Srila Prabhupada’s definition of Kamadeva and what it tells us about his translation of the mantra.

Why is Kamadeva the master of the senses? The answer to this question can be found in the correlation between Krsna, the supreme controller, and the mind, senses, and desires of the living entities.

The Vedanta-sutra proclaims in its aphorism ananda-mayo ’bhyasat that the constitutional positions of both the living entities and the Supreme Lord are by nature full of desire — the desire for happiness.

The living entities who desire to please Krsna’s senses actually enjoy, but those who desire to enjoy their own senses independently of Krsna enjoy a shadow of happiness, more accurately called suffering.

Both categories of souls harbour long-cherished desires for happiness. On the material plane, the mind — a subtle repository of desires — seeks to fulfil those desires through the gross senses.

Everyone’s desire, however, is under the control of the Lord. He is the master of liberated souls as the direct object of their desire, and He is the master of conditioned souls also, but indirectly, and in two ways.

First, because the very objects with which the mind and senses try to realise these desires are made of Krsna’s separated energies, Krsna is actually the object of even selfish cravings, albeit indirectly.

Krsna Himself declares,

**“Know that all states of being — be they of goodness, passion, or ignorance — are manifested by My energy. I am, in one sense, everything, but I am independent.”**
