Second, because as living entities we forget our desires when we change bodies, the Lord reminds us of past cravings from within our hearts, and in that way inspires each soul to fulfil its desires.

Thus, because Krsna controls the desires of conditioned and liberated souls, both of whose desires are expressed through the senses, the name Kamadeva, “Lord of desire,” also implies “Lord of the senses.”

In Srila Prabhupada’s translation of the Kama-gayatri mantra, the word kamadeva refers primarily to the controller of His devotees and associates, as the adjectives puspabana and ananga confirm.

In the preceding diksa-mantras, the emphasis has been on Krsna as the controller of wayward souls, but in Kama-gayatri, although implied, that truth is not the primary mood of the mantra.

In Kama-gayatri, the word kamadeva refers to the transcendental Cupid (Krsna), who carries flower-arrows with which He pierces the Vraja-vasis — especially the gopis — and takes control of their minds.

In this regard Sukadeva Gosvami says,

**“Whenever Krsna went to the forest, the minds of the gopis would run after Him, and thus the young girls sadly spent their days singing of His pastimes.”**

In summary, the direct meaning of the word kamadeva is Krsna, who, by His special quality of sweetness, captures the minds of His companions and brings their senses completely under His control.

Krsna’s blackish bodily hue confirms that He is the presiding Deity of the amorous mellow, which contains the qualities of all other mellows and thus fulfils the desires of all those over whom He reigns.

Later I will fully analyse the more esoteric meaning of the name Kamadeva, but in essence it is this: Although Krsna controls everyone’s senses, Krsna’s senses are always controlled by Radha.

Now let us briefly outline the three sections of Kama-gayatri: sambandha-, abhidheya-, and prayojana-jnana, principles attributed to Madana-mohana, Govindaji, and Gopinatha respectively.

Because these three principles have been already been studied in connection with the other diksa-mantras, I will now mention only the features of these principles unique to the Kama-gayatri mantra.

The mantra’s lessons on cultivation of relationship serve to engender the spontaneous love the gopis have for Krsna. This transcendental lust (ragatmika kama) has two divisions: direct and indirect.

When a gopi’s lust for Krsna leads to intimate contact, it is called direct, and when that lust leads to a relish for simply hearing about another gopis direct connection with Krsna, it is called indirect.

Devotional service practised with a genuine aspiration for either form of ragatmika kama is called raganuga bhakti, which is also divided into the same two categories, direct and indirect.

The followers of Rupa Gosvami generally aspire for the indirect form of spontaneous love, which is displayed by the maidservants of Srimati Radharani like Sri Rupa-manjari or Lavanga-manjari.

The Deity of Madana-mohana teaches the confidential principles of spontaneous devotion to devotees who yearn to know them, and who have already assimilated the practices of regulated devotion.

Rupa Gosvami has defined the underlying principle of these teachings:

**“Devotional service in spontaneous love is vividly expressed and manifested by the inhabitants of Vrndavana.**

**“Devotional service that accords with their devotional service \[ragatmika bhakti\] is called raganuga bhakti, or devotional service following in the wake of spontaneous loving service.” He also says:**

**“When an advanced, realised devotee hears about the affairs of the devotees of Vraja — in the five mellows of devotion — he becomes inclined in one of these ways, and his intelligence becomes attracted.**

**“Indeed, he begins to covet that particular type of devotion. When such covetousness is awakened, one’s intelligence no longer depends on the instructions of revealed scripture or on logic and argument.”**

To a qualified devotee who has assimilated the principles of spontaneous devotion — and how important it is to keep those principles confidential — Govindaji reveals the ways to practise them.

Rupa Gosvami explains the essence of this practice:

**“The advanced devotee who is inclined to spontaneous loving service should follow the activities of a particular associate of Krsna’s in Vrndavana.**

**“He should execute service externally as a regulative devotee as well as internally from his self-realised position. Thus he should perform devotional service both externally and internally.”**

The acarya elaborates on the preceding verse by saying,

**“The devotee should always think of Krsna within himself and should choose a very dear devotee who is a servitor of Krsna in Vrndavana.**

**“One should constantly engage in topics about that servitor and his loving relationship with Krsna, and one should live in Vrndavana. If physically unable to go there, he should mentally live there.”**

In short, there are two aspects to an advanced devotee’s cultivation of spontaneous devotion, the external and the internal. Externally, such a fortunate devotee should still behave like a neophyte.

One who has truly realised spontaneous devotion will continue to follow the principles of regulated devotion as prescribed in scripture, especially hearing and chanting, the most vital limbs of devotion.

Internally, the self-realised devotee serves Krsna according to his coveted mood of devotion. One who fully identifies with the purport of Kama-gayatri will internally serve Krsna in the mood of a manjari.

The reader may rightly ask, “What is our connection to Kama-gayatri— and the amorous mood it inspires — if our internal, and eternal, relationship with Krsna is not in the mood of the gopis?”

As a preface to the answer, let us recall that Radha’s and Krsna’s amorous pastimes play a central part in all the pastimes and are at the heart of all the mellows of devotion existing in Vrndavana.

The divine couple’s pastimes are the central point of many concentric circles upon the surface of the lake of transcendental mellows. Some are closer, some more distant, but all emanate from that single point.

The answer, then, is that practising devotees attracted to mellows other than the gopis’ amorous mood want to hear about the pastimes of Radha and Krsna as they are portrayed in Kama-gayatri.

Such knowledge may not be directly related to one’s personal mood of devotion, but it is still favourable in that it increases awareness of Krsna’s qualities and hence nurtures our affection for Him.

Gopa-kumara, for example, in his perfected state as the cowherd boy Sri Sarupa, had knowledge of Krsna’s intimate relationship with the gopis, even though he himself was not involved in those pastimes.

In describing Krsna’s pastimes upon his own return to Goloka, Gopa-kumara told his disciple, the Pragjyotisa-pura brahmana, that he had seen the gopis bathe, dress, and ornament Krsna at Nandagrama.

He went on to relate how Srimati Radharani and other gopis served Sri Krsna and received hints from Him regarding where they should meet Him later that night. Gopa-kumara told the brahmana:

“Then Sridama carefully took me to his own home, and what other pastimes Krsna enjoyed that night I cannot say. With great difficulty I passed the night and in the morning I went to Nanda’s home.

“I saw Krsna on the bed, sleeping and bearing the marks of amorous pastimes.” After describing Yasoda’s alarm at seeing what she mistook for marks left by Krsna’s gopa friends, Gopa-kumara said:

“Mother Yasoda repeated her words of concern over and over to the girls of Vrndavana as they arrived one by one with their faces shining with fear, smiles, and embarrassment.”

There are other examples of devotees who are not gopis being connected to Krsna’s amorous pastimes. The cowherd boy Subala and his followers assist Krsna in His romantic exploits.

A gopi once recounted the intimate services of Subala and his companions to Srimati Radharani: “My dear Krsangi (“delicate one”), just see how Subala is whispering Your message into Krsna’s ear —

“How he is delivering the confidential letter of Syama-dasi silently into eager Krsna’s hand, how he is delivering the betel nuts prepared by Palika into Sri Krsna’s mouth, and how he is —

“Decorating Krsna with the garland prepared by Taraka. Did you know, my dear friend, that all these most intimate friends of Krsna are always engaged in His service in this way?’

In conclusion, we know that chanting the Kama-gayatri mantra is greatly beneficial — whatever mellow of devotion we aspire for or have perfected — and so we take full shelter of its divine vibration.

**\*\*\*\***

The Kama-gayatri portrays the ultimate goal of transcendental realisation (prayojana) as Ananga, the transcendental Cupid, who can only be known and served by pure spiritual senses.

The word ananga means “one who has no body.” Obviously Krsna has a body, and so ananga means that He does not have a material body, but rather a completely spiritual body with spiritual limbs.

Lord Caitanya’s explanation of the word nirguna (“without qualities”) supports this logic. Sri Caitanya says the Absolute Truth is without material qualities — His qualities are completely spiritual.

Neither should Ananga be mistakenly identified with the mundane, worldly Cupid, whose body was burnt to ashes by Lord Siva. Brahma chanted Kama-gayatri from the stature of a brahmana.

By his chanting, Brahma became “acquainted with the ocean of truth” (vijnata-tattva-sagarah), a profound result impossible to attain by worshipping the mundane-sex-impulse personified, Cupid.

Indeed, loving devotion to the Ananga referred to in Kama-gayatri, the prince of Vraja, is the antidote to the erotic influence of the Jagad-ananga, who rules over the inhabitants of the material world.

Kubja, for example, was freed from Cupid’s lusty fire burning up her limbs (ananga-tapta) by smelling the fragrance of Krsna’s feet as she held them to her chest. By that act alone she was liberated.

Bhaktivinoda Thakura, addressing himself, depicts a similar path to liberation:

**“You served lusty desires, and they gave you nothing but a suffering that made Bhaktivinoda’s limbs burn as if in flames.**

**“Turn from desiring what is false to desiring what is real and just worship the Ananga of Vrndavana. Wounded by His flower-arrows, you will manifest a different, eternal body, filled with spiritual love.”**

In stark contrast, only self-deluded persons think they can worship Ananga-Krsna by offering their material bodies to Him in imitation of Kubja or the gopis, who serve Krsna in spiritual forms.

Such impostors become victims of the material Cupid. Entangled in his web of lust, they engage in sinful acts in the name of bhakti and remain far distant from Lord Krsna’s loving devotional service.

The divine love of which the Kama-gayatri sings — the gopis’ spontaneous love for Krsna — is devoid of any trace of lust. Indeed, it is as pure as gold smelted one hundred times. It is faultlessly pure.

For us to taste the kind of spontaneous love the gopis have for Krsna, and serve the object of their love, we must transcend the modes of nature and cultivate ecstatic devotion to the mature stage of prema.

This amorous love attains its zenith in Srimati Radharani, who is the very heart of Vraja and the focus of Krsna’s amorous attraction. As Krsna is the transcendental Cupid, She is the transcendental Rati.

Some gopis are friends of Radha, others Her maidservants, still others are Her competitors. Yet in their own ways, all the gopis enrich Radha’s service to Krsna. In that sense, they are all Her servants.

As the original pleasure potency, Sri Radha enchants Krsna completely. With the unlimited variety of loving mellows He tastes in Her company, She brings Him wholly under Her control.

From the philosophical point of view, Krsna is fully independent. But from the perspective of mellows, He is completely dependent on Srimati Radharani, for She is the source of pleasure for His senses.

We may possess senses, but if we cannot enjoy those senses, of what value are they to us? It is in this way that the objects that give us pleasure control us. In a similar way, Radha controls Krsna.

It is clear that Srila Prabhupada enjoyed stressing this truth in his books, lectures, and conversations. He writes, “The symbol of devotional service in the highest degree is Srimati Radharani.

“Krsna is called Madana-mohana, which means that He is so attractive that He can defeat the attraction of thousands of Cupids. But Radharani is still more attractive, for She can even attract Krsna.

“Therefore devotees call Her Madana-mohana-mohini—the attractor of the attractor of Cupid.” Because Radha enjoys this dominion over Krsna, who is the prince of Vraja, She is also called Vrndavanesvari.

Through Arjuna, Krsna advises us — whether we be practising or perfected — to perform devotional service under the care of Sri Radha. That is the meaning of His words *daivim prakrtim asritah*.

All these statements make it clear that Srimati Radharani is the energy (sakti) of Kama-gayatri; when we invoke Krsna, then, through chanting His names, Srimati Radharani is present automatically.

As we chant Kama-gayatri we should think of Radha alongside Kamadeva and Krsna’s other names. As Srila Prabhupada writes,

**“Devotional service is directly under the control of Radharani.”**

Once a maidservant was massaging Srimati Radharani’s limbs on a secluded bank of the Yamuna when she noticed that Krsna was intently watching His beloved from within a nearby kadamba tree.

The gopi could understand that the sight of Radha’s beautiful limbs (anga) was Krsna’s life force (ananga-jivam). In the same way, Sri Radha’s potency pervades Kama-gayatri, which is also Krsna’s body.

That the Kama-gayatri is identical with Krsna also indicates that its twenty-four and one-half syllables — sometimes counted as twenty-five— correspond to the moons that rise in Krsna’s body.

Srimati Radharani’s form is also present in the syllables of this Gayatri mantra, so when we chant Kama-gayatri we should see that the same type of correlation relating to Krsna also applies to Her.

While chanting the syllables of the Kama-gayatri mantra, then, we should meditate on the mantra’s implied subject, Radha, as we do on its explicit subject, Kamadeva. The two are inseparable.

The mantra’s syllables are ka, ḍṁ, de, va, ya, vi, dma, he, pu, spa, ba, na, ya, dhi, ḍṁ, hi, tan, no, ’na, ngah, pra, ṣî, da, ya, and t(a); these are twenty-five, but ya, the half syllable, makes twenty-four and a half.

The syllable ka is equivalent to the full moon of Krsna’s face; ḍṁ, His right cheek; de, His left cheek; va, the spot on sandalwood on His forehead; and ya, the half-moon of His forehead itself.

The word kamadevaya extols the mantra’s main feature: the spiritual, ever-fresh Cupid of Vrndavana (vrndavane ‘aprakrta navina madana). The other features of Kama-gayatri elaborate on Him.

Similarly, Krsna’s face is the king that rules over the other moons of His body, which act as the throne from which the king rules. In this way the syllables of kamadevaya submit to Krsna’s face.

Ten more syllables may be systematically placed from right to left upon the full moons of the fingernails on Krsna’s hands, and in the same way, the final ten upon the toenails on His lotus feet.

Caitanya Mahaprabhu describes Krsna’s fingernails as ten delicate moons that dance and sing upon the flute as it rests in Krsna’s hands. The song of those musical moons is the melody of the flute.

He also describes Krsna’s ten toenails as another set of moons that dance and sing as He walks. Their song is the tinkling of His anklets, from which Vrndavana’s sweetly cooing swans hide in shame.

But no matter how attractive these twenty moons and their performances may be, they cannot match Krsna’s full-moon face, which nourishes the other moons with the beams of its smile.

Indeed, all living beings in Vrndavana live on the sweetness of Krsna’s moon-like face. Some relish the moonbeams of His smile, some taste the nectar of His lips, but no one is ever fully satiated.

It may also be said that Krsna’s body resembles an ocean whose waves glisten with the light of His moon-like fingernails and toenails, and above them the moon-like sandalwood dot on His forehead.

Above that ocean is the extraordinary vision of Krsna’s full-moon face, and more stunning still, His sweet smile, radiating moonbeams of nectar. Recalling this metaphor, Lord Caitanya cited the following:

**“O my Lord! The transcendental body of Krsna is very sweet, and His face is even sweeter than His body. But His soft smile, which has the fragrance of honey, is sweeter still (*****madhuram madhura*****).”**

As numerous as the waves of an ocean may be, they can be counted. But the sweetness of Krsna’s facial features will never be fully glorified such that a Vaisnava can one day say, “I have done it!”

And yet we must mention one more hallmark of Krsna’s countenance— His sidelong glance. Although this gesture may also be found in great persons, like Lord Visnu, in Krsna it is unique.

Krsna’s eyebrows are like the two ends of a bow, curving out from the sandalwood-spot handle in the middle of His forehead, and His reddish eyes are the quiver that carries the arrows of His glances.

The bowstring is His mild smile, which makes the outer corners of His eyes stretch enchantingly towards His ears. With a “twang” He releases the arrow of a glance, just suited to His purpose.

And what — or who — is His target? Surrounded by gopis, Radha once entered the forest to meet Krsna, but upon finding Him at the meeting place, Radha became so shy She turned back to go home.

The gopis found the situation so amusing that they had to cover their laughing faces with their veils. But Krsna was not amused. To save the situation, He followed Radha a few paces and called after Her.

And when Radharani carelessly let Her guard down and turned to glance His way, Krsna pierced Her with a flower-arrow glance of His own, at once driving away Sri Radha’s rogue minister, shyness.

And so, to the gopis’ delight, Krsna lived up to His name (Ananga) and entered Srimati Radharani’s heart. In the unembodied form of a glance, He removed all barriers to Their mutual happiness.

Krsna’s facial and bodily features combine to devastate the gopis, who lose all composure at the sight of His sublime good looks. At that point their facade of chaste indifference runs to a distant place.

Once a gopi confided to her friend about the effect Krsna’s smile and glance had upon her: “Who was that boy, whose glorious youthful handsomeness is beyond anything ever seen in this world?

“The splashing waves of His gentle smile would make even Kamadeva faint in bliss. Who was that handsome boy? I don’t know! Somehow, just by seeing Him my peaceful composure has fled far away.

“My heart is always agitated, and I always weep. Why? Was it His dance-like gait, His swaying limbs, His enchanting smile, or the arrow-like glance He shot at me from the corner of His lotus eyes?

“The jasmine garland that hung from His neck swayed to and fro, intoxicating the black bees humming about it, and sandalwood-paste tilaka glistened like moons on His forehead and chest.

“I cannot understand why all these things agitate me so. But I do know that my heart is wounded and afraid of the gossip of hardhearted women. I dare not reveal my condition to anyone but you.”

Because Krsna’s bodily beauty and its effect on the gopis both defy description, devotees will understand why He is called Ananga: He possesses a body different from any of which we can conceive.

Inquisitive sages may try to compare the beauty of the Absolute Truth to various marvels in creation, but their spiritual masters can only answer, “Neti! Neti! It’s not like this, nor like that. It is ananga.”

One inconceivable feature of Krsna’s moon-like beauty is that His devotees’ thirst for it keeps increasing. Instead of being satisfied at the sight of Him, they curse the creator for giving them only two eyes.

Another feature of Krsna’s beauty is that devotees are never free from its influence. Even when out of sight, Krsna breathes the essence of His charm — the radiance of His smile — into His flute.

The sound of the flute then spreads in all directions, even up to the Vaikuntha planets. It mystifies the Vraja gopis’ minds, loosens their clothes, and makes them abandon their household chores.

One might think that when Krsna is far away and not playing the flute, devotees would be safe from His influence. But no! Once seen, Krsna’s smiling face permanently resides in one’s mind.

Being fully Krsna conscious is like having a picture of Krsna held before our eyes wherever we go. And what’s more, Caitanya Mahaprabhu describes that we constantly hear Krsna’s flute as well.

The Lord told Sanatana, “The vibration of His flute is just like a bird that creates a nest within the ears of the gopis and always remains prominent there, not allowing any other sound to enter their ears.

“Indeed, the gopis cannot hear anything else, nor are they able to concentrate on anything else, not even to give a suitable reply. Such are the effects of the vibration of Lord Krsna’s flute.”

This is the benediction we seek as we chant the final declaration of the Kama-gayatri mantra (tan no ‘nangah pracodayat), appealing to the embodiment of love, Sri Radha, and the object of love, Ananga.

Sometimes I hear devotees inquire whether it is proper to pray to the Lord for perfection. The answer is yes! Every one of us, at all stages of devotional service, should aspire for perfection in our own ways.

But our aspirations should match our level of Krsna consciousness. We cannot imitate the behaviour of those beyond our actual level of spiritual advancement lest we become degraded by deceitfulness.

In The Nectar of Devotion Srila Prabhupada describes the aspiration of perfected devotees and encourages devotees in general to strive for realising such ambitions and the perfections that come with them.

Srila Prabhupada writes,

**“One should feelingly pray and become eager to render his particular type of service to the Lord. This is the teaching of all great devotees, especially Lord Caitanya.**

**“In other words, one should learn how to cry for the Lord. One should learn this small technique, and he should be very eager and actually cry to become engaged in some particular type of service.”**

**\*\*\*\***

Tî gain a fuller sense of the mantra, let us now consider the individual words of Kama-gayatri. Of course, it is impossible to complete such an analysis, just as it is to fully praise Radha and Krsna.

We have already discussed the kama-bija (klim) and words like vidmake, common to Gayatri mantras. Now let us explore additional aspects and meanings of each word unique to the Kama-gayatri.

Klim! Because the letters of the kama-bija are the condensed form of Radha and Krsna, the divine couple are very pleased when we chant this transcendental sound vibration in the mood of sankirtana.

We might not think of chanting Gayatri as a part of the sankirtana-yajna or mission, but scriptures say that it is. Let us extract the essence of sankirtana, which is to chant in glorification of the Lord.

Applying this principle to the chanting of our diksa-mantras — ending with Kama-gayatri-we can glorify the Lord in a mood that begins with praise of the sankirtana mission and ends in our self-surrender.

This is the process of glorification Caitanya Mahaprabhu personally taught in His Siksastaka prayers, prayers that when applied yield wonderful fruit from chanting the maha-mantra and diksa-mantras.

If we are to reap the full benefit from chanting Krsna’s names, we must have implicit faith in those names, faith that they alone can free us from bad habits, purify our hearts, and bestow love for Krsna.

And although Lord Krsna has made Himself freely available through His names, beginners generally have little taste for chanting those names, a misfortune that must be regretted and rectified.

To reform faulty chanting and awaken krsna-ðrṛmṁ, we should think ourselves lower than straw in the street, more tolerant than trees, unworthy of others’ respect, but eager to offer all respect to others.

The more tolerant and respectful we become, the more our attachment for Krsna will thrive — even more so if we think ourselves devoid of devotion, mere aspirants for suddha-bhakti in future lives.

As we are gradually elevated, as our hankering to be the Lord’s servant (kinkara) increases, we see ourselves as thoroughly disqualified for such a position due to the lingering bonds of maya.

Knowing that chanting Krsna’s names is the only means to attract the Lord and nurture love for Him, in great humility we should beg for the empowerment to chant His pure name in ecstasy.

The attainment of that proficiency enriches our mood of devotion further with feelings of separation, which then awaken divine distress, lamentation, utter humility, and finally the perfection of life.

With the realised sentiments of a gopi, we can then enter Vraja to serve Radha and witness first hand the treasure chest of Her unequalled love, from which She showers us with devotional mellows.

Because the kama-bija fulfils all desires, we should accept it as the means to perfect each successive stage of our chanting, beginning with pure faith and continuing up to the ultimate goal, selfless love.

By chanting the Kama-gayatri, the seed klim not only fulfils our spiritual longings but also stimulates our eagerness to perfect each successive stage, which amazingly also fulfils klim's own desires.

Yes! The kama-bija also becomes satisfied. Isn’t it the sound incarnation of Radha and Krsna? And aren’t They always eager to increase Their circle of companions by helping us achieve perfection?

In summary, the natural result of pure contact with klim is the strong desire for Kama-gayatri’s promised perfection, the attainment of which is greatly pleasing to Srimati Radharani and Sri Krsna.

Praises of klim begin with its five component parts: ka, la, i, the halfmoon symbol, and the moon-dot above the m, which combine to represent Radha and Krsna and the bliss They enjoy from kissing.

The constituents of the kama-bija are the ingredients from which Brahma created the universe. They also delineate the various limbs of Sri Krsna’s Deity form. In short, klim is the same as Syamasundara.

And finally, as a means to fulfil His own desires, Krsna manifests from klim five flower arrows, which He shoots at the gopis to awaken their pure desire to engage in amorous dalliances with Him.

Let us now review the individual words of the mantra. The mantra is meant for the worship of Deva, the supreme Lord of Lords, who in His youthful form is also the amorous mellow personified.

Gaudiya Vaisnavas prefer to worship Krsna’s form as transcendental Cupid, over and above even His childhood and boyhood forms, not to mention the opulent forms that rule over the Vaikuntha planets.

Bilvamangala Thakura sings of wonderful Deva in this way,

*premadam ca me kamadam ca me vedanam ca me vaibhavam ca me*  
*jivanam ca me jivitam ca me daivatam ca me deva naparam*

**“O Lord! You and You alone are my worshipful Deity, the giver of love (prema) and amorous love (kama), which You alone teach. You alone are my power, wealth, vital force, and my very life.”**

The Kama-gayatri, then, reveals to us that it is Kamadeva Krsna, the performer and enjoyer of the rasa dance, to whom we should surrender and of whom we should take shelter — no one else.

Why? Because although Krsna’s other forms in Vraja give love for Him — the very means of attaining Him — only His eternally youthful form of Syamasundara gives the special treasure of amorous love.

Krsna is not only the sole teacher of knowledge of amorous sports and the bestower of that mood but also the only object of such knowledge for those who have abandoned all forms of speculation.

Similarly, Krsna is the sole opulence of those who disregard the bounty of Vaikuntha and its Lord. All these things considered, Deva is not merely our life’s breath, He is actually our very lives.

Visvanatha Cakravarti Thakura prefaces his word-for-word explanation of Kama-gayatri by disclosing how the words represent the many ways Krsna exerts His influence over Radha and the gopis.

This preface outlines the nature of the transcendental realm and identifies three transcendentally sweet exchanges to which the three phases of Kama-gayatri direct us. We should bear this in mind.

First, the syllable klim refers to Krsna’s flute and the sweetness of its melodies (venu-madhurya), which successfully steals the gopis’ minds and submerges them in thoughts of their beloved.

Second, kamadevaya denotes Krsna’s sweet pastimes (lila-madhurya), which when remembered—not to mention directly encountered— completely bewilder the Vraja gopis’ power to discriminate.

And third, puspabanaya makes known Krsna’s sweet personal qualities (guna-madhurya), which incite Radha and the gopis to seek meetings with Him, encounters that end in their mutual bliss.

The combined sweetness of all Krsna’s alluring features completely subjugates the gopis. In this way the Lord is able to draw the cowherd girls into the rasa dance and utterly crush Cupid’s pride.

What’s more, anangah identifies Krsna’s transcendental body, which He expands into many in order to be at the side of every gopi and at the same time stay with Radharani in His original form.

As usual, the first portion of the mantra (kamadevaya vidmahe) establishes relationship, which is further developed by the knowledge of Vraja’s transcendental Cupid revealed in the rest of this Gayatri.

The very word kama (“divine lust”) is enough to define the nature of the relationship between the worshippers (the gopis) and the worshipped (Kamadeva) as one of transcendental amorous love.

The words kama and vidmahe further elaborate on this relationship, which follows (anuga) in the wake of the cowherd girls and is characterised by a dominant mood of humble submission.

Thus, the relationship invoked through the Kama-gayatri mantra— in which the chanter aspires to follow the cowherd girls of Vraja, and Kamadeva is the worshipable Deity — is called êamanuga.

We should note that the success of one aspiring for kamanuga lies not only in cultivating a mellow of service to Kamadeva Himself but also in serving and pleasing the servants of the Lord’s servants.

The sambandha that lies at the heart of the Kama-gayatri is described by Caitanya Mahaprabhu Himself:

**“...\[I am\] the most obedient servant of the servants of the Lord, the maintainer of the gopis.”**

As pure devotees, then, we do not approach Krsna directly by trying to cultivate the kama of the gopis; instead, we try to please the servant of the Lord’s servants, and in this way please the Lord.

If we please Krsna, then chanting His names, hearing His pastimes, and following in the footsteps of His associates will automatically invoke in us the transcendental lust for which we aspire.

Srila Prabhupada once quoted his guru as saying,

**“Do not try to see Krsna. Render your service in such a nice way that let Krsna see you.” He added, “When Krsna sees you, then your mission is perfect.”**

Since the name Deva denotes a person absorbed in love-sports, Kamadeva is the name for Krsna who, as the carefree prince of Vrndavana, revels in love-sports according to His own desire (kama).

And vidmahe also means that as practising devotees aspiring for service in the mood of kama we want to know Kamadeva to the exclusion of all other Deities and all other forms of Godhead.

Consciousness pervaded by an intense and spontaneous desire to know Kamadeva is called Krsna consciousness, and the phrase kamadevaya-vidmahe refers to this state of awareness.

**\*\*\*\***

The essential pillar of sambandha-jnana is a proper understanding of the unfailing transcendental nature of Kamadeva Krsna, the self-satisfied Lord who reciprocates the love of His devotees.

Because the gopis worship Krsna by offering their bodies to Him in spiritual lust (êṁḍa), non-devotees mistake Kamadeva to be a lusty young prince who enjoys His senses in the company of beautiful girls.

This kind of ignorance obscures the truth that for Krsna — who is fully self-satisfied — a gopi offering Him her body in a secluded forest grove and a pujari offering Him incense in a temple are no different.

Krsna reciprocates not the nature of the devotee’s actions, but the mood of devotion. That is to say, He is as pleased by a stick of incense saturated with pure love as He is with amorous exchanges.

Scriptures relate a dialogue between Srimati Radharani and Durvasa Muni that clarifies the meaning of Kamadeva by settling the controversy about whether or not Krsna is the enjoyer of the gopis.

Early one morning, after having spent the entire night with Krsna under the spell of His enchantments, the gopis felt anxious over their impending separation from Him. Sweetly, they inquired:

“Is there some pure brahmana whom we can feed to win his blessings and thus stay forever in Your company?” Smiling, Krsna replied, “The great Durvasa Muni has such power. Why not feed him?”

Krsna and the gopis were in Vrndavana on the western side of the Yamuna, but Durvasa was on the eastern side. The maidens of Vraja were perplexed. How did Krsna expect them to cross the raging river?

They asked, “The Yamuna River is very deep. How can we cross it to get the blessings of Durvasa?” Krsna replied, “Humbly ask Yamuna-devi to let you cross her waves on the strength of My celibacy.

“Just say, ‘Krsna is a brahmacari,’ and the river will give you passage.” The gopis replied, “Why would the Yamuna give way by hearing Your name, and how arc You, the lover of many women, a brahmacari ?"

To remove the gopis’ doubts, Krsna said, “By remembering Me, a shallow person becomes deep. By remembering Me, an impure person becomes pure. By remembering Me, an impious person becomes pious.

“By remembering Me, one full of material desires becomes desireless. By remembering Me, a fool becomes learned.” The simple-hearted gopis accepted Krsna’s words and at once departed for the Yamuna.

As we shall see, this instruction forms the core of Durvasa’s teachings in this pastime. The gopis followed Krsna’s order, but because they did not understand it, they would question the Muni as well.

The gopis had faith in Krsna’s mystic powers. They had seen Him kill many demons, and they knew that the mere sound of His flute wielded powers that could solidify rivers and melt mountains.

As they approached the Yamuna, the gopis meditated on Durvasa Muni, the plenary portion of Lord Siva, and with full faith in their beloved Lord, called out over the waters, “Krsna is a brahmacari!”

The deep, raging river at once receded to make a pathway on its riverbed. The shocked but overjoyed gopis then hurried to Durvasa’s asrama, where they offered obeisances and worshipped the sage.

With great attention the gopis silently served Durvasa sweet rice and rice fried in ghee. Out of affection for them, he accepted their offering and gave some of it to his disciples and to the forest animals.

Durvasa gave the gopis the blessing they desired — Krsna’s constant company — and the gopis, now having to return home, again inquired, katham yasyamo ’tirtva sauryam: “How will we cross the river?”

The sage replied, “On the strength of my asceticism and self-restraint, if you just remember me, Durvasa, and request the daughter of the sun for passage to the far shore, she will grant it to you.”

At this the astonished gopis buzzed about Radharani like bees around a queen bee in a hive. After a brief discussion they agreed that She should question the sage. Gandharvi Radha then asked the Muni:

“How is Krsna a brahmacari, and how could Durvasa Muni be an ascetic? (katham krsno brahmacari / katham durvasano munih) Today we have heard these things, but we have also seen the opposite.”

The gopis had engaged in intimate affairs with Krsna, so how could He be a brahmacari? And they had just seen Durvasa eat a feast, so how could he claim self-restraint? Naturally, they were confused.

The gopis prompted Srimati Radharani with whispered comments and furtive glances. When they saw that the sage was about to answer, they sat around him silently, waiting for his words of wisdom.

Durvasa Muni said, sabdavan akasah: “The ether is filled with sound.” This statement was meant to show that it is the illusioned mind and not the spirit soul that is the enjoyer of sense objects.

It follows, then, that if a self-realised sage like Durvasa, who is fixed in his spiritual identity, does not enjoy sense objects such as food, what then of the Supreme Lord when He cavorts with the gopis?

But the gopis were simple village girls, and so the sage felt the need to elaborate on his initial reply. He explained that although sound requires ether to exist, the two are always distinct from each other.

Similarly, although the soul is present in ether and hears sound through the ears, the soul remains distinct from all three. How can it be said, then, that the spirit soul enjoys by hearing?

Although the soul is present in air and feels touch through the tactile sense, it is distinct from all three, as they are from one another. How then can it be said that the spirit soul enjoys by touching?

Also, though the soul is present in fire and sees form with the eyes, it is distinct from all three, as they are from one another. How then can it be said that the spirit soul enjoys by seeing?

Moreover, although the soul is present in water and tastes with the tongue, it is distinct from all three, as they are from one another. How then can it be said that the spirit soul enjoys by tasting?

Finally, although the soul is present in earth and smells aromas with the nose, it is distinct from all three, as they are from one another. How then can it be said that the spirit soul enjoys hy smelling?

This raises a question. If indeed the soul is separate from the senses and sense objects, and if it does not enjoy them, then how do we see, hear, or touch, and who is it that seems to enjoy these phenomena?

Anticipating that this question would arise in the gopis' minds, Durvasa said, *idam hi manas tesv evedam manute tan idam hi grhnati*: “The mind stays at the centre of the senses and accepts them."

Again the sage elucidated his point. He explained that the soul actually perceives the sense objects, but the mind, due to its intimate connection with the soul, thinks that it is the direct perceiver.

The mind is also connected to the senses. When it focuses on the senses, impelled by subtle desires emanating from the soul, the mind is inclined to enjoy the contact between the senses and their objects.

The mind retains past impressions of sense perception. If the mind deems contact between a sense and its sense object favourable, the mind enjoys. If it deems that contact unfavourable it suffers.

These impressions of happiness and distress are arbitrary, and therefore it is said that “one man's food is anther man’s poison.” Happiness and distress are both illusions born from ignorance.

In summary, the mind, surcharged with consciousness by contact with the soul, is made aware of the activities of the senses. Influenced by the soul’s desires, the mind then thinks it enjoys or suffers.

This identification with the mind, and its whimsical insistence on suffering and enjoying, is the primary illusion that results from the soul’s forgetting its spiritual identity and embracing matter instead.

Elsewhere, Maitreya describes this condition, *sasthas tu tamasah sargo óas tv abuddhi-krtah prabhoh*: “The sixth creation is the ignorant darkness of the living entity, by which the master acts as a fool.”

Upon attaining spiritual knowledge the soul directly perceives the embarrassment of identifying with the body and the futility of attempting to enjoy the senses. He is then established in the self.

In this enlightened state the soul is freed from all kinds of ignorance and accepts the basic necessities of life without attachment to or repulsion from them. Krsna describes this state of perfection:

“A person in the divine consciousness, although engaged in seeing, hearing, touching, smelling, eating, moving, sleeping, and breathing, always knows within himself that he actually does nothing at all.

“Because while speaking, evacuating, receiving, or opening or closing his eyes, he always knows that only the material senses are engaged with their objects and that he is aloof from them.”

After explaining all of this, Durvasa paused to determine whether Sri Radha and the gopis had understood what he meant. He then asked the rhetorical question, “How can I be a materialistic enjoyer?”

Durvasa was firmly situated in his spiritual identity. Although he consumed the food he had been offered, he did so out of obedience to Krsna and affection for the gopis, not with the intention to enjoy.

And because he had no desire to enjoy his senses, for Durvasa to eat a feast was equivalent to him not eating anything at all. The sage saw the look of understanding in the eyes of the gopis and continued.

Durvasa Muni then said, *ayam hi krsno yo vo hi presthah sarira-dvaya-karanam bhavati*: “Your beloved Sri Krsna is the original creator of two kinds of bodies — the gross and the subtle.”

Self-realised souls control their minds and senses. They never entertain the thought of enjoying through either. But Krsna is not just supremely self-realised, He is the creator of both mind and senses.

As the primeval creator, He is in full control and is fully aware of all bodies and all things. There is never a question of His being subjected to the illusion of enjoyment all other beings must rise above.

Some philosophers argue that if the Absolute Truth is the omnipresent cause of all causes, then He is distributed throughout His creation and must also suffer and enjoy according to material laws.

Krsna dispels this misconception: “By Me, in My unmanifest form, this entire universe is pervaded. All beings are in Me, but I am not in them. And yet everything that is created does not rest in Me.

“Behold My mystic opulence! Although I am the maintainer of all living entities and although I am everywhere, I am not a part of this cosmic manifestation, for My Self is the very source of creation.”

To explain how Sri Krsna is both within and yet aloof from everything, Durvasa gave a famous example from the Upanisads of the enjoying and witnessing birds sitting on the tree of the body.

The enjoying bird is the conditioned soul, and the witness bird the Supersoul, a partial expansion of Krsna. From this it is evident that Krsna neither enjoys nor suffers, for He is transcendental to both.

Maya places the conditioned souls in illusion to act out fruitive activities through material knowledge and ignorance. But Krsna, as Maya’s creator, is inherently transcendental to her influence.

Not only is Krsna free from any taint of ignorance, He is also unlimitedly full of transcendental knowledge. Thus Durvasa resolved, sa katham visayi bhavatiti: “How can He be a materialistic enjoyer?”

Durvasa Muni went on to enlighten the gopis by defining lust (kama), those who are controlled by it, and those who are free from it. This definition gives deep insight into Krsna’s role as Kamadeva.

He said,

*yo ha vai kamena kaman kamayate sa kami bhavati*  
*yo ha vai tv-akamena kaman kamayate so ’kami bhavati*

**“He who lusts after pleasure is lusty. He who does not lust after pleasure is not lusty.”**

What impels a lusty person? The desire to enjoy sense objects, or things outside of himself! But nothing is outside of Lord Krsna, and so He is not impelled to enjoy — He is not lusty (kami).

This raises a question. When Krsna appears in the company of the gopis, if He is not enjoying, then what is He doing? The answer is that He is reciprocating the loving affection of His devotees.

Krsna is the supreme enjoyer. But because He is self-satisfied, His enjoyment does not depend upon His senses being gratified; rather, His enjoyment is only in giving pleasure to His devotees.

Materialistic people, imagining themselves in Krsna’s position, naturally want to enjoy their senses with beautiful young girls. But Krsna does not see the cowherd girls of Vraja as objects to be enjoyed.

How does Krsna see the gopis? He sees them as worshippers offering their bodies to Him with spiritual desire (kama). As the worshipable Kamadeva, He accepts their pure devotion to make them happy.

Again, to materialists the exchange between Krsna and the gopis may externally appear to be a display of mundane passion, but the underlying intent of that exchange makes it different and unique.

Once, on the full-moon night of autumn, as the gopis were preparing for the rasa dance, they suddenly discovered that their beloved Krsna was no longer in their midst. He had mysteriously disappeared.

After the gopis had searched for Him throughout Vrndavana, acting out His pastimes and singing His glories in madness, Krsna finally returned, beguiling the gopis with His smile as if He had never left.

But the gopis were in anxiety. Why had Krsna put them through so much grief? Hadn’t they abandoned their families, risking everything to be with Him? They wanted to know what His intentions were.

After giving Krsna a seat on their shawls, the gopis interrogated Him. Why had He not reciprocated their love? In His own defence, Krsna spoke of three kinds of people: the selfish, the merciful, and the self-satisfied.

The first group show affection only to benefit themselves, the second show unmotivated affection, and the third show indifference, even to those who love them. Krsna said He was the second kind of lover.

Hearing Krsna place Himself amongst those who are merciful (*ye vai karunah*), the gopis displayed symptoms of transcendental indignation as they inquired, “But then why did You make us suffer?”

Sri Krsna lovingly replied, “The reason I do not immediately reciprocate the affection of living beings even when they worship Me, O gopis, is that I want to intensify their loving devotion.”

In other words, Krsna’s only interest in being with the gopis, or in being away from them, is to increase their love for Him. That’s all He wants from His devotees, in whatever way they offer Him their love.

On another occasion, after Krsna had left Vrndavana for Mathura, He sent Uddhava to the gopis with a letter in which He reiterated that everything He did was to draw the gopis closer to Him.

Krsna showed that His relationship with the gopis does not depend upon physical proximity, as sense enjoyment does. Their relationship, on the contrary, becomes more intense with separation.

And because separation intensifies the gopis love, Krsna left them. Krsna wrote,

*óat tv aham bhavatinam vai dure varte priyo drsam*  
*manasah sannikarsartham mad-anudhyana-kamyaya*

**“But the actual reason I, the beloved object of your sight, have stayed far away from you is that I wanted to intensify your meditation upon Me and thus draw your minds closer to Me.”**

Krsna’s only motive for physically separating Himself from the gopis was to increase their pleasure, because the apparent suffering they go through in separation is actually the topmost ecstatic happiness.

It is obvious to His devotees, and anyone else who takes Krsna at His word, that His relationship with the gopis is not as an enjoyer of sense objects — many women—but as the worshipable Deity of His servants.

He is called Kamadeva, then, because He is the Deity (Deva) of the gopis’ pure desire (kama). He reciprocates in kind, meaning that Kamadeva is the presiding Deity of pure amorous exchange.

This amorous exchange is not driven by sensual enjoyment — either on Krsna’s part or on the part of the gopis — but by pure loving reciprocation, which is free from any taint of material lust.

What is the evidence that the gopis’ love for Krsna is pure and selfless? Do they really want only to please Him, or are they motivated to enjoy their senses with the handsome folk-hero of Vrndavana?

The gopis own words are testimony to their unmotivated, selfless love: “O dearly beloved! Your lotus feet are so soft that we place them gently on our breasts, fearing that Your feet will be hurt.

“Our life rests only in You. Our minds, therefore, are filled with anxiety that Your tender feet might be wounded by pebbles as You roam about on the forest path.” How could such love not be pure?

Through Mahaprabhu’s lips, Radha disclosed the selfless love of the gopis, which aligns itself fully with the happiness of the object of their love, Kamadeva. She sang, “I do not mind My personal distress.

“I only wish for the happiness of Krsna, for His happiness is the goal of My life. However, if He feels great happiness in giving Me distress, that distress is the best of My happiness.”

This extraordinary attitude of the gopis— indeed of all pure devotees of Krsna — has no equivalent in worldly domains, where personal enjoyment is the fee expected for so-called loving exchange.

Srila Prabhupada summarises the pure devotional mood with words some might find shocking:

**“A pure devotee has no way of sensing happiness except by seeing that Krsna is happy in every respect.”**

**\*\*\*\***

Dî the gopis want to enjoy Krsna? Only if it makes Him happy! But if someone else's love makes Him happy, that is their happiness. Radha says, “If Krsna, attracted by the beauty of some other woman —

“Wants to enjoy with her but is unhappy because He cannot get her, I fall down at her feet, catch her hand and bring her to Krsna to engage her for His happiness.” This is the standard of the gopis’ love.

And what is the gopis’ opinion of those who want to enjoy Krsna? Radha says, “I condemn such a woman to be struck on the head with a thunderbolt, for we simply want the happiness of Krsna.”

Caitanya Mahaprabhu, in the ecstatic mood of Srimati Radharani, sang the following song, which emulates the mood of the gopis. Great devotees who repeat these words swim in great waves of ecstatic bliss.

Srimati Radharani once said to a friend,

*krsna — mora jivana, krsna— mora prana-dhana, krsna — mora pranera parana*  
*hrdaya-upare dharon, seva kari’sukhi karon, ei mora sada rahe dhyana*

**“Krsna is My life and soul, the treasure of My life, indeed He is the very life of My life. I therefore keep Him always in My heart and try to please Him by rendering service. That is My constant meditation.”**

This verse is laudable without doubt, but in the context of amorous exchanges with the one known as transcendental Cupid, our hearts might demand more information. Indeed, Mahaprabhu sings on.

Radha discloses how Her amorous love is Her service,

*mora sukha — sevane, krsnera sukha — sangame, ataeva deha dena dana*  
*krsna more ‘kanta kari’, kahe more ‘pranesvari’, mora haya ‘dasi’-abhimana*

**“My happiness is in the service of Krsna, and Krsna’s happiness is in union with Me. For this reason, I give My body in charity to the lotus feet of Krsna, who accepts Me as His loved one.**

**“In fact He calls Me His most beloved. It is then that I consider Myself His maidservant.”**

There can he no doubt that uniting with Krsna brings happiness to the gopis, but it is not their motivation.

Thus Radha highlights the eminence of service,

*kanta-seva-sukha-ðurṁ, sangama haite sumadhura, tate saksi—laksmi thakurani*  
*narayana-hrdi sthiti, tabu pada-sevaya mati, seva kare ‘dasi’-abhimani*

**“Service to My lover is the home of happiness and is more sweet than direct union with Him. The goddess of fortune is evidence of this, for although she constantly lives on the heart of Lord Narayana —**

**“She wants to render service to His lotus feet. She therefore considers herself a maidservant and serves Him constantly.”**

With these words Sri Radha concludes Her soliloquy on the gopis’ love for Krsna.

The preceding verses confirm that the relationship between Krsna and the gopis is based not on mutual exploitation tor enjoyment but on the most elevated form of sublime and selfless love.

Great scholars of devotion like Uddhava Mahasaya praise the gopis' love for Krsna as a love that “...is hankered after by those who fear material existence, by great sages, and by ourselves as well.”

Durvasa Muni concluded his lesson to the gopis by praising Krsna as the Supreme Brahman, the Absolute Truth, the one free of transformation, the unchangeable, transcendental Supreme Being.

Although Krsna lives in the sun, He also lives amongst the cows, and although He lives in everyone and in everything, He is also the gopis’ husband. Then how can He, the goal of the Vedas, be an enjoyer?

After hearing from the sage, the gopis understood how Krsna was an eternal brahmacari. A brahmacari is one who does not compromise his commitment to Brahman by succumbing to sex enjoyment.

But Krsna is not merely committed to Brahman, He is Brahman. And His Cupid-like exploits are not acts of enjoyment, but exchanges of transcendental love. Thus He is the perfect brahmacari.

Whatever Krsna does, whether it be stealing butter or sporting with the gopis, He is always transcendentally situated. His acts are always worshipable, as irreligious as they may seem to faithless persons.

After Sukadeva Gosvami described the rasa dance, Pariksit Maharaja could detect doubts in the minds of the assembled sages. They were, in fact, silently questioning Krsna’s character and motivation.

The king thus addressed his guru, “O brahmana! The Lord of the universe has descended to this earth along with His plenary portion to destroy irreligion and re-establish religious principles.

“Indeed, as the Supreme Personality of Godhead, He is the original speaker, follower, and guardian of moral laws. How, then, could He have violated them by touching other men’s wives?”

That great self-satisfied sage, Sri Sukadeva Gosvami, then explained that powerful controllers or transcendentally situated persons are not affected by apparent transgressions of morality.

To elaborate, Sukadeva gave the example of fire, which remains unpolluted even as it consumes all kinds of things, and of Lord Siva, who remained unaffected while drinking an ocean of poison.

In conclusion, Sukadeva deftly summed up Krsna’s amorous pastimes:

**“Material activities never entangle the devotees of the Supreme Lord, who are fully satisfied by serving the dust of His lotus feet.**

**“Nor do material activities entangle those intelligent, self-realised sages who have freed themselves from the bondage of all fruitive reactions by the power of mystic yoga.**

**“So how could there be any question of bondage for the Lord Himself, who assumes His transcendental forms according to His own sweet will?” Or, in the words of Durvasa, sa katham visayi bhavatiti:**

**“How could Krsna be a materialistic enjoyer?”**

He could not be. He is not one. This truth is implied by the name Kamadeva, which the Kama-gayatri mantra invites us to know for ourselves.

And so whenever we read in scripture such phrases as “Lord Krsna enjoyed with the gopis” we should understand them to mean “Lord Krsna enjoyed the gopis’ offerings of loving devotion.”

Similarly, when we read statements like “the gopis enjoyed with Krsna” we should understand them to mean “the gopis enjoyed offering their love to Krsna and accepting His love in return.”

In this context, then, the word “enjoy” does not refer to the amorous acts themselves, but to the exchange of love. And because loving exchange takes place through amorous acts, they too are enoyable.

For Krsna and the gopis, love is the only enjoyable thing. Just as an iron rod thrust into fire becomes like fire, amorous acts performed during enjoyable exchanges of love are themselves enjoyable.

Krsna doesn’t discriminate amongst the ways His devotees choose to express their love for Him. The lick of a cow, the wrestling of a friend, or the kiss of a gopi are all the same — exchanges of love.

And if devoid of love, those same physical acts — licking, wrestling, or kissing—hold no attraction for Lord Krsna. This is the truth to be understood by chanting the name of Kamadeva.

**\*\*\*\***

*Puspabanaya dhimahi*, the section of Kama-gayatri that deals with cultivation, is Govindaji’s domain, just as *kamadevaya vidmahe*, the section concerned with relationship, belongs to Madana-mohana.

That the word dhimahi is plural stresses the importance of absorbing the mind, senses, and intelligence in Cupid and His flower-arrows. If we do so, He will capture our hearts just as He captures the gopis.

We have already heard of these flower arrows and their effects, but now we shall elaborate on them by linking them to the five divine attributes of Krsna that are the exclusive sense objects of the gopis.

They are (1) the sweetness of Krsna’s personal beauty, (2) the sound of His voice and flute, (3) the aroma of His bodily limbs, (4) the cooling touch of His palms and feet, and (5) the taste of His soft lips.

Armed with these personal qualities as His weapons, which are like the flower arrows of Cupid, Krsna acts like a relentless hunter who sets out to track down and capture the hearts of the doe-like gopis.

In Vrndavana forest Krsna lies in wait nearby a path the gopis tread to fetch water from the Yamuna. There He sets out the trap of His beautiful face, in which He places the bait of His sweet smile.

He makes His earrings dance and flash to attract the curious gopis from afar, or like many hunters do, makes enticing sounds by playing melodies on His flute that draw the hypnotised gopis to Him.

When the gopis come near, His smile lures them helplessly into the noose of His charming face. But cruel Krsna, not satisfied to just capture His victims, also pierces them with the arrows of His glance.

We might think that by such a fate the gopis should have met their end, but by the touch of His broad chest, Krsna revives them, and with the scent of His limbs, subjects them to serving His every whim.

Having made the gopis His maidservants, and having pilfered from them all their possessions, Krsna occasionally rewards them for their intense endeavours with the soft, cooling touch of His pinkish palms.

Cooler and more gratifying than a host of moons, the touch of Krsna’s palms sweeps away the weariness that the gopis feel from their exertion and fills them with the bliss felt by a sated lover.

Once in Vrndavana, after being lured by the sound of the flute, the gopis obediently gathered around Krsna in the forest and spoke of their submission to His qualities. “Dear Krsna!” they exclaimed.

“By seeing Your beautiful face decorated with curling locks, by seeing the beauty of Your earrings resting on Your cheeks, and by seeing the glistening nectar of Your lips and Your beautiful smiling glances —

“As well as Your two arms, which assure complete fearlessness, and Your broad chest, whose beauty arouses amorous attraction, we have simply surrendered ourselves to becoming Your maidservants.”

We may be curious to know what makes Krsna’s features so attractive to the gopis. The answer is, in one word, sweetness. Krsna’s qualities are marked by a sweetness that eludes the pens of the best of poets.

Indeed, the sweetness of Krsna’s words, touch, beauty, fragrance, and lips is such that it actually drives the gopis mad. Their madness is incited by that sweetness but it is exacerbated by something else.

Each of the gopis’ five senses — eyes, ears, nose, tongue, skin — want to simultaneously taste Krsna’s sweetness, and so, like five wild horses, they drag their drivers, the gopis’ minds, in five different directions.

Consequently, their minds become deranged. And if we were to say “Dear gopis! Why not just bring your senses under control?” they would tell us that such a thing couldn’t possibly be done. Why?

Because Krsna’s sweetness also attracts their minds, which sympathise with the desires of their senses, a dilemma whose only solution— for both the gopis’ minds and senses — is to be with Krsna.

When I began to serve Radha-Syamasundara in earnest, They blessed me with a drop of Their sweetness. Ever since then I have felt bound to Them, hand and foot, with invisible but unbreakable chains.

To what can I compare Syama’s beautiful dark complexion? Lord Caitanya compares Krsna’s complexion to powdered eye ointment and the softness of blue lotus flowers. Who would not agree?

To that I would add that Syamasundara’s lustre is so pleasing that nothing compares to it. And might I also add that it bestows a satisfaction to the eye that makes the mind giddy with delight.

Fully attired in flowing robes, ornaments, a garland, and a turban, Syamasundara is like a merciful cloud that nourishes the cataka birds of the eyes with showers of unending sweetness.

As I bathe and dress Syamasundara, the coolness of His limbs soothes my sense of touch. It’s as if condensed moonshine is extinguishing the fire of passion that burns within my heart, limbs, and senses.

Amazingly, just as Krsna’s soothing touch puts out the fire of material enjoyment, it simultaneously stokes the embers of a different fire — the fire of enthusiasm to serve Him, body, mind, and soul.

Although I often smear musk, sandalwood, and aguru oils on Syama’s body, the natural scent of His limbs covers these scents with an aroma of its own and floods my nostrils with its distinctive bouquet.

As for the nectar of Syama’s lips, it is readily available in the remnants of His food. When tasted, it infects the mind with a sort of delirium that undermines the tongue’s ability to savour anything else.

Having become addicted to the remnants of Syama’s food, I must confess I am completely dependent on the nectar of His lips, which, aside from eating, can be tasted in the sight of His enigmatic smile.

To what can we compare the ambrosia of Syama’s smile? If we were to mix honey, camphor, mischief, elegance, mystery, moonshine, and ardent desire, that potion might begin to resemble that divine elixir.

But what would we use to apply that mixture? Nothing could be as effective at dispensing the nectar of Krsna’s smile as Krsna’s lips. One thing is sure: Radha and the flute are both experts on that elixir.

And even though I feel overjoyed to hear that Srimati Radharani yet continues Her eternal research into this essential spiritual science, Her feelings about the flute’s involvement are somewhat mixed.

On the one hand, the flute has the cheek to steal the property of the gopis. How can they condone that? On the other hand, the sound of the flute, when mixed with the nectar of Krsna’s lips, is faultless.

I take shelter of the aggregate of all rasas, the amorous rasa, fully manifest in Syamasundara’s human-like form, which now gives shelter to the universe and is decorated with a peacock feather.

No doubt Syama’s qualities are wonderful in their own right. Yet I must say that, standing next to Sri Radha, His features display a pinnacle of perfection that would not be visible if He were alone.

Just as iron placed next to a magnet or an object placed in front of a mirror reveal the essential quality of the magnet or the mirror, Radha’s company highlights Krsna’s qualities to the extreme.

Therefore, Kama-gayatri, in asking us to meditate upon Krsna (puspabanaya), implies that we include Radha in our thoughts, just as thinking of a hunter automatically includes thinking of his game.

Let us consider the qualities of Radha that attract Krsna’s flower arrows. Obviously, as a hunter is attracted by the game he hunts, Krsna’s senses are irresistibly attracted to Radha’s qualities.

But we may wonder how that can be. The answer is that Srimati Radharani and Krsna are two inseparable aspects of the one Absolute Truth; He is the possessor of all energies, and She embodies them.

As Krsna’s counter-whole, Radha is the refuge of all His energies, which naturally attract Him in turn. Again, we might ask, “Is Radha an exact replica of Krsna, or is there a difference between Them?”

To answer this question we must consider the example of the reflection in the mirror. Although the reflection is a perfect likeness of the original object, some of the reflection’s features are different.

The right hand of a person standing before a mirror, for example, is the left hand of his reflection. Similarly, the qualities of Syama are those of the beloved, while the qualities of Radha, those of the lover.

One day, as He was lost in thoughts of Srimati Radharani, Krsna Himself came to the same conclusion: “All the world derives pleasure from Me, but is there anyone who can give Me pleasure?

“Only one who has a hundred times more qualities than Me could please My mind, and yet, where in this world is such a person to be found? In Radha! Yes, Radha! She alone can give Me pleasure.”

Because Krsna’s happiness depends upon His ability to access Radha’s qualities, the two are as inseparable as fire and its heat. When we speak of Krsna’s qualities, then, we must also speak of Radha’s.

I am convinced that these words, spoken by a parrot of Vraja, apply to Radha in New Vraja-dhama and Her control over Syama: “That Sri Radha, who is a jewel-mine of qualities and a nectar-ocean of love —

“Who is praised by all the chaste girls of the three worlds for Her beauty, and who is the empress of the world-famous Vrndavana, appears here as the original goddess of fortune.

“She appears to control even Ajita, the invincible Krsna, with Her attributes and characteristics and Her pure, exclusive love. May I always worship Vraja’s original goddess of fortune.”

**\*\*\*\***

We residents of New Vraja-dhama have no doubt that Sri Radha is in full control of Syama. Aside from this being sastric truth, Radha’s dominant pose and assertive expression serve as supporting evidence.

The impression Her appearance makes upon the eyes cannot be suitably described with words like “beautiful” or “sweet.” Only a heart dedicated to Her will be able to make sense of what the eyes behold.

If I say Her complexion shames lightning, kunkuma, and gold, my heart replies, “It does more than that!” And if I say Her hair pales a monsoon cloud embroidered with moonlight, I hear the same reply.

I know that the nectar radiating from the moon of Her spotless face gives sustenance to Syama, but it also makes Him slightly dizzy. So I’m afraid how He might react if I call Her simply “moon-faced.”

It might be easier to describe the effect that seeing Her has on others. The sight of Sri Radha brings tears to devotees’ eyes, leaves them speechless, and increases their eagerness to see Her more and more.

On occasion I rub rose oil on Radharani’s delicate, translucent limbs. But due to Her natural fragrance, like a bouquet of roses, I notice no significant change, save and except the bliss I feel massaging Her.

I think the different roses of this world have been awarded their aromas by Her own: the scent of the red rose from Her kunkuma hue, that of the white rose from Her soothing, fragrant bodily effulgence.

The yellow rose must derive its bouquet from the fragrance of Her complexion; the pink, from Her palms and soles; the orange rose, Her lips; and the purple, from Her enchanting, lovely form.

And because Srimati Radharani is a syama heroine — as cool as camphor in summer and warm as sunshine in the winter — this bodily feature of Hers must have lent its fragrance to the black rose.

Radha’s fragrant limbs are as soft as puffs of cotton, making them most suitable pillows for Syamasundara to rest His head upon when the divine couple take naps in the afternoon or at night.

Hearts weak in devotion may doubt that a Deity’s body can be soft. But such scepticism can linger only until devotees themselves serve Their Lordships, at which time all doubts fly to distant places.

The softness of Radha’s limbs reflect the softness of Her heart, which is forever melted out of Her love for Krsna and Her kindness and compassion for the fallen conditioned souls of this world.

The taste of Sri Radha’s lips pervades the remnants of Her food, as does Krsna’s His. And though the remnants of the two are mixed together, the taste of Radha’s is uniquely palpable in Her smile.

The calm reserve in Radha’s iridescent smile belies the boundless ecstasy constantly erupting in Her excited heart from being always at Syamasundara’s side and never for a moment separated from Him.

In Their eternal Vrndavana pastimes, the divine couple’s constant cycle of meeting and separating make up the never-ending ebb and flow of waves in the limitless nectarean ocean of devotional mellows.

To taste the pleasure of a perpetual high tide of love, Radha and Syama disguise Themselves as Deities. Freed from the strictures of the Vraja-vasis’ codes of conduct, They never have to separate.

We residents of New Vraja-dhama not only approve of Radha’s and Syama’s perpetual union, but also live only to see it. It is our life. In this way we hope to serve our worshipable superiors forever.

When our chanting of Their names becomes especially euphoric, Radha’s smile broadens. Then the sunshine of Her lips lights up Her servants’ hearts and that shining streams forth from their own faces.

At such times Her lips appear like a fragrant, blossoming orange rose, whereas Syamasundara appears as if He might transform into a hungry bumblebee to taste the honey oozing from that rose.

Once Krsna tried to take romantic liberties with Radha. Pushing Him away, She stopped the advance of Krsna’s hands. May the words “No! No!” upon Her camphor-scented lips protect us all.

I do not know how deep the ocean of sweetness is, or how wide the ocean of beauty, yet I know that these immeasurable bodies of ambrosia fit within a single pinkie on one of Radha’s delicate hands.

Radha’s and Syama’s bodily features are like flower arrows with which They constantly pierce each other’s transcendental bodies and inflict the wounds of amorous love, thus exciting Their shared bliss.

Srimati Radharani’s qualities also equip Her with flower arrows of Her own, with which She pierces Krsna and gives Him bliss, and so She may be called Puspabani, just as He is called Puspabana.

Bhismadeva explains how one can enjoy being pierced by arrows:

**“On the battlefield, the flowing hair of Lord Krsna turned ashen due to the dust raised by the hoofs of the horses.**

**“And because of His labour, beads of sweat wetted His face. All these decorations, intensified by the wounds dealt by my sharp arrows, were enjoyed by Him. Let my mind thus go unto Sri Krsna.”**

I have not yet had the good fortune to personally hear Radha and Syama converse, so I have refrained from speaking of Their voices in this feeble attempt I’ve made to portray Their qualities.

But Lord Caitanya describes the sound of Krsna’s voice to be like the deep resonance of thundering clouds and His singing to be sweeter than the song of a cuckoo. Either one nullifies all other sounds.

The golden Lord also says that Krsna’s joking is possessed of an extraordinary sweetness that plays havoc with the hearts of all women and binds their ears to its indescribable verve.

Sri Radha’s voice is said to be the essence of all honeyed sounds, and when She speaks in riddles to Her beloved Krsna, the rich timbre of Her voice ties His mind in knots and He is soundly defeated.

Despite my not having heard these sounds, could it be that when we praise Radha-Syamasundara in kirtana with all our hearts, the “Hare!” is like His voice and the “Krsna!” and “Rama!” are like Hers?

I have discussed but five of the divine couple’s qualities. Srila Rupa Gosvami, however, enumerates sixty-four for Krsna and twenty-five for Radha. We can easily understand how well-armed They are!

But even though Her qualities are fewer, Radha carries the ultimate weapon—She always keeps Krsna under Her control! This confirms the eternal truth that quality triumphs over quantity.

As we patiently hear about the transcendental qualities of the divine couple, our natural attraction for Them grows, and our sensual attachment to the allurements of maya automatically slackens.

In this way we acquire both sense control and spiritual attachment, which at a certain stage become so strong that the mind attains a natural, unwavering attraction for the divine couple.

Krsna calls this “unfailing devotional service” (*avyabhicarini bhakti*). He says it is the key to transcending the modes of material nature and arriving at the spiritual platform (*brahma-bhuyaya kalpate*).

The process of meditation (dhimahi) on Radha’s and Krsna’s pastimes can award these perfections. But, like Arjuna, we often complain that our restless minds make such discipline impossible. What to do?

Krsna surely empathises with our plight as we sincerely try to control our wicked minds and absorb them in Him. What’s more, through His instructions to Arjuna, He gives us no alternative. It must be done.

And yet, Lord Krsna encourages us to persevere:

**“O mighty-armed son of Kunti! It is undoubtedly very difficult to curb the restless mind, but it is possible by suitable practice and by detachment.”**

Let us therefore meditate on Puspabana and His consort, Puspabani, who are superior to Cupid and Rati — and so much more so because Their flower-arrows are parts of Their very selves.

**\*\*\*\***

The cultivation and awakening of amorous affection — culminating in its fruition, love for Krsna — was shown by the sages of the Dandaka forest, who later took births as the *rsi-cari gopis* in Vraja.

I have already mentioned these renounced sages who worshipped Gopala in spontaneous amorous attraction by chanting the Gopala mantra with the desire to become Krsna’s gopi lovers.

When Lord Rama appeared before these Dandaka sages, they were elevated from firm faith to attraction, and then to attachment, just by seeing His transcendental form and receiving His merciful glance.

Lord Ramacandra assured the sages that their desires to serve in amorous love would bear fruit at the end of Dvapara-yuga, when the Lord would appear as Sri Krsna in Bhauma Vrndavana.

The sages did indeed take birth from the wombs of gopis in Vraja, but in the intervening millennia they had not managed to become entirely free from material contamination and reach perfection.

But they had awakened some spontaneous regard for Gopala through their devotional practices, and by the mercy of Lord Rama that regard had matured into the initial stages of spontaneous attachment.

Nonetheless, after they appeared in the families of Vraja-vasis, these rsi-cari gopis still had to refine that attachment before they could directly associate with Krsna and fulfil their long-cherished desire.

Amongst the rsi-cari gopis, some were elevated to the transcendental platform by hearing about Krsna from eternally perfect gopis like Lalita-devi and Rupa-manjari and being trained by them.

When these girls reached puberty, mature loving attachment to Krsna blossomed in them, even before they met Him. This type of spontaneous attachment prior to meeting is called purva-raga.

After puberty — but before the rasa dance - when they finally got the opportunity to meet Krsna, this group of rsi-cari gopis reached even more advanced stages of love, beginning with prema.

Krsna’s sudden disappearance before the rasa dance elevated these gopis’ feelings of love in separation even further, qualifying them to enjoy the highest loving exchanges during the dance.

How was it that Krsna touched these gopis after they were married? Yogamaya had created material facsimiles of the gopis’ bodies for their husbands. It was only their spiritual bodies that went to Krsna.

On that full-moon night, when Krsna had called these gopis with the sublime vibration of His flute-song, their husbands tried to stop them, and Yogamaya helped them escape and go to see their paramour.

But not all the rsi-cari gopis attained perfection in the same way. Due to their past deeds, others did not have the chance to associate with the eternally liberated gopis, and so they remained materially tainted.

Those gopis were obliged to submit to their husbands and give birth to children. Then, by hearing of Krsna’s celebrated deeds from gopis more advanced than they, they too were freed of all contamination.

In that purified state, intense attraction to Sri Krsna also awoke in those rsi-cari gopis who had been slower to mature. That attraction made them objects of the eternally liberated gopis’ good will.

Although this group also received spiritual bodies with which to serve the Lord, they did not receive Yogamaya’s blessings to answer the call of Krsna’s flute but remained trapped in their homes.

In whatever way those gopis tried to leave their homes, sometimes using ladders, their fathers, brothers, or husbands stood in the way brandishing sticks, while their mothers and sisters spoke harsh words.

This friction between the milkmaids and their families burnt every trace of family attachment from the hearts of those rsi-cari gopis. They saw their husbands and relatives as enemies and nearly died.

At death’s doorstep, this group of gopis thought of Krsna just as yogis remember the Supersoul while leaving their bodies, or as dying materialists remember the family members who surround them.

Having no other shelter than Krsna, they cried out to Him, “O Lord! Life of our lives! Since we cannot see Your smiling lotus face now, allow us to become Your maidservants in some future birth.”

Unable to see Him directly, those gopis remained where they stood. Beset by angry in-laws, they closed their eyes and in pure love absorbed their minds in Krsna, just like perfected mystic yogis.

Sri Suka describes this episode:

**“Some of the gopis, however, could not manage to get out of their houses, and instead they remained home with eyes closed, meditating upon Him in pure love.”**

Just as the fire of separation caused by Krsna’s sudden disappearance from the rasa dance refined the rsi-cari gopis who met with Krsna, it also fully cleansed the rsi-cari gopis who were unable to meet Him.

This example of the Dandaka sages shows how practising devotees attain perfection to become sadhana-siddha gopis through the cultivation implied by the phrase puspabanaya dhimahi.

**\*\*\*\***

The importance of cultivating feelings of separation, as taught by Caitanya Mahaprabhu to His followers, cannot be overstated, and so it has already been stressed several times in this work.

Kama-gayatri tells us to meditate upon He who carries flower arrows, a summons to all initiated devotees to practise remembrance — according to their qualification — in the mood of separation.

In the course of our advancement in Krsna consciousness, remembrance in separation ignites a fire that burns up past karma and the material aspect of our bodies to reveal our spiritual forms.

How these wonderful things happen can be understood by more deeply studying the example of the rsi-cari gopis, whose separation from Sri Krsna is poignantly narrated by Sukadeva Gosvami.

The sage relates more details of their path: “For those gopis who could not go to see Krsna, intolerable separation from their beloved caused an intense agony that burned away all impious karma.

“By meditating upon Him they realised His embrace, and the spiritual ecstasy they then felt exhausted their material piety. Although Lord Krsna is the Supreme Soul, the Personality of Godhead —
